# The Enneads in modern English, from the Greek (milestones E1 onward)

Written by `tools/modern_enneads.py` from `sources/translations/modern/enneads.txt`. The Workshop's modern English, machine-assisted: drafted with an AI model (Claude) from the Greek and not yet checked by a scholar. The Greek of each paragraph, and MacKenna's English of each chapter, are one tap away.

- Made from: Volkmann's Greek (Leipzig, 1883-1884), as First1KGreek gives it; checked against MacKenna's English (1917, 1921, 1924, 1926, 1930; the Internet Archive's OCR of the printed volumes, read by rule (tools/mackenna.py), not proofread).
- Porphyry's Life of Plotinus: Volkmann's own Greek (vol. I, 1883), proofread from the scan (`sources/neoplatonists/proofread/porphyry-life-plotinus.txt`).
- Treatises done here: Porphyry, On the life of Plotinus and the order of his books, I.1 What is the living being, and what is the human being?, I.4 On happiness, I.5 Whether happiness grows with time, I.6 On beauty, I.7 On the first good and the other goods, I.8 What evils are, and where they come from, I.9 On going out of life, II.1 On the heaven, II.2 On the movement of the heaven, II.3 Whether the stars are causes, II.5 On what is potential and what is actual, II.6 On substance, or on quality, II.7 On complete blending, II.8 On sight, or why distant things look small, III.1 On destiny, III.2 On providence, first part, III.3 On providence, second part, III.4 On the daemon allotted to us, III.5 On love, III.8 On nature, contemplation and the One, III.9 Various considerations, IV.1 On the being of the soul, first part, IV.4 On difficulties about the soul, second part, IV.5 On difficulties about the soul, third part, or on sight, IV.6 On perception and memory, IV.8 On the descent of the soul into bodies, IV.9 Whether all souls are one, V.4 How what comes after the first comes from it, and on the One, V.5 That the intelligibles are not outside intellect, and on the Good, V.7 Whether there are forms of individuals, V.8 On intelligible beauty, VI.1 On the kinds of being, first part, VI.2 On the kinds of being, second part, VI.3 On the kinds of being, third part, VI.4 That being, one and the same, is everywhere whole, first part, VI.5 That being, one and the same, is everywhere whole, second part, VI.6 On numbers, VI.7 How the many forms came to be, and on the Good, VI.8 On free will and the will of the One.
- The whole Enneads: 40 treatises here, 15 beside Taylor (milestone 27), 0 to come.
- Chapters: 489, each with a short title and a summary.
- Paragraphs: 1712, each with its Greek.
- Words: 207,581 modern, 156,835 Greek, 242,009 in MacKenna's English of the same chapters.
- Sentences (each can be marked in the reader): 7,174.
- Flags (where MacKenna and the Greek differ, or the Greek is hard): 1950.
- Notes (Plato's, Aristotle's and Homer's places, hard readings): 1137.
- Problems found: 0.

## Flags

| Paragraph | MacKenna's words | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Life.2.p1 | in a short while he contracted malign diphtheria | The Greek says "little by little" (κατ’ ὀλίγον) the disease grew on him, not "in a short while"; and its name is κυνάγχη ("dog-choke"), the ancient name for a severe inflammation of the throat. "Malign diphtheria" is a modern diagnosis put into Porphyry's mouth. |
| Life.2.p1 | addressing everyone by word of mouth | The Greek ἀπὸ στόματος πάντας προσαγορεύειν is most naturally "greeting everyone mouth to mouth", with a kiss, which is why his friends, afraid of his sores, kept away; "by word of mouth" makes it speech. |
| Life.3.p1 | he was told he was a " perverted imp," | The Greek ἀτηρὸν παιδίον is "a naughty (or wretched) child", a nurse's scolding; "perverted" is MacKenna's. |
| Life.3.p2 | At twenty he was caught by the passion for philosophy | The Greek says "in his twenty-eighth year" (εἰκοστὸν δὲ καὶ ὄγδοον ἔτος): MacKenna drops the eight. The count matters, since Porphyry then has him study eleven years with Ammonius and join Gordian's army at thirty-nine. |
| Life.3.p5 | presented to Hostilianus of Apamea | The Greek names him Ustillianus Hesychius (Οὐστιλλιανῷ Ἡσυχίῳ) of Apamea; MacKenna gives one name, in another form. |
| Life.4.p2 | the distribution was grudging and secret | The Greek says only that the books were not given out easily or lightly, "but with every care in judging those who received them"; "secret" is MacKenna's. |
| Life.4.p3 | On our Tutelary Spirit (II. 2) | The fifteenth book, "On the daemon allotted to us", is III.4 in the Enneads, not II.2, which is the fourteenth, "On the circular movement". The Ennead places are MacKenna's additions. |
| Life.5.p1 | Unity (II. 4, 5) | The two books "That being, one and the same, is everywhere whole" are VI.4 and VI.5 of the Enneads, not II.4 and II.5 (which are "On matter" and "On the potential and the actual"). |
| Life.5.p2 | After these come the following twenty | Porphyry simply goes on with the list; the heading, and the count of twenty, are MacKenna's. |
| Life.5.p2 | On the Impassibility of the Bodiless (III. 5) | This book is III.6 of the Enneads; III.5 is "On love", the fiftieth. |
| Life.6.p2 | On the First Good ; or, On Happiness (I. 8) | The last book, "On the first good", is I.7 of the Enneads; I.8 is "What evils are", the fifty-first. |
| Life.6.p3 | early manhood, vigorous prime, worn-out constitution | The Greek κατὰ πρώτην ἡλικίαν is "in his earlier age": by Porphyry's own count Plotinus was about forty-eight to fifty-nine when he wrote the first twenty-one, not in "early manhood". |
| Life.7.p1 | from Amereia, Unification, | ἀμέρεια is "being without parts", indivisibility, which the intelligible has; MacKenna's "Unification" is near the sense, but not the word. |
| Life.7.p2 | the political career in which he stood high | The Greek says only that Zethus was a man of politics, "with political leanings" (ῥοπὰς ἔχοντα πολιτικάς), which Plotinus tried to check; that he "stood high" in it is MacKenna's. |
| Life.7.p3 | only one meal a day | The Greek σιτεῖσθαι παρὰ μίαν is "to eat every other day", skipping a day between meals; MacKenna's "one meal a day" is a milder fast. |
| Life.8.p1 | in these habits, to our general surprise, he remained unchanged to the very end | What "we all marvelled at" (ὃ πάντες ἐθαυμάζομεν) is what follows, "for he would complete the argument in himself...": his writing straight from a finished thought, not his careless habits. |
| Life.8.p2 | by someone entering on business | The Greek says only "even when talking with someone and keeping the conversation going"; the visitor "entering on business" is MacKenna's. |
| Life.9.p2 | his intention towards the Supreme | The Greek is τὴν πρὸς τὸν νοῦν τάσιν, his "tension towards intellect"; MacKenna's "the Supreme" sets it higher, at the One. |
| Life.11.p3 | to my house where I had secluded myself | The Greek says only that Plotinus came to Porphyry "while I was staying in the house" (ἐν τῷ οἴκῳ διατρίβοντι); that he had shut himself away is MacKenna's. |
| Life.13.p1 | a slight moisture gathered on his forehead | The Greek says only that "a fine sweat ran down" him (λεπτός τις ἱδρὼς ἐπέθει); the forehead is MacKenna's. |
| Life.13.p1 | He was always as ready to entertain objections as he was powerful in meeting them | The Greek lists what showed in his face as he spoke: his gentleness, his kindness towards questions (τὸ προσηνὲς πρὸς τὰς ἐρωτήσεις) and his vigour (τὸ εὔτονον). MacKenna turns this into a sentence of his own about objections and his power in meeting them. |
| Life.13.p2 | a man called Thomasius | The Greek name is Θαυμάσιος, Thaumasius. |
| Life.14.p1 | He followed his own path rather than that of tradition | There is nothing for this in Volkmann's Greek, which goes straight from "speaking with inspiration and passion" to the Stoic and Peripatetic doctrines. |
| Life.14.p1 | is condensed in them, all but entire | The Greek says only that the Metaphysics "is packed into" his writings too (καταπεπύκνωται); "all but entire" is MacKenna's. |
| Life.14.p1 | Geometry, Mechanics, Optics and Music | MacKenna leaves out arithmetic (ἀριθμητικόν), which the Greek names after geometry. |
| Life.14.p2 | Longinus' work On Causes | The title is περὶ ἀρχῶν, On principles (or "On first principles"), not "On causes". |
| Life.14.p2 | a few words sufficed him to make clear the significance of some profound theory and so to pass on | The Greek ἀνίστατο is "he rose": having given the sense in a few words, he stood up and ended the meeting. |
| Life.15.p2 | should submit to the teacher without reserve | The Greek says the pupil should give himself "to the teacher who desires him" (ἐρῶντι ... τῷ καθηγεμόνι); "without reserve" is MacKenna's, and he leaves out the teacher's desire. |
| Life.15.p2 | during the very reading he repeatedly quoted | The Greek says that Plotinus "kept saying it at the meetings" (κἀν ταῖς συνουσίαις συνεχῶς ἐπιλέγειν), afterwards and often, not during the reading. |
| Life.15.p3 | the principles of Astronomy | The Greek κανόνες are "tables" (the astronomers' tables of the stars' movements), used to cast nativities; it is these that Plotinus studied "not at all as a mathematician". |
| Life.16.p1 | works by Alexander of Lydia, by Philocomus, by Demostratus and by Lydus | Volkmann's Greek reads "Alexander the Libyan" (τοῦ Λίβυος) and "Demostratus the Lydian" (τοῦ Λυδοῦ): MacKenna makes Alexander a Lydian, and the Lydian a fourth author, "Lydus". |
| Life.17.p1 | it is equivalent to Porphyry (Purple-robed) | Not in the Greek: Porphyry says only that Malchus, his native name, means "king". That "Porphyry" ("purple") also suits a king is MacKenna's addition. |
| Life.17.p2 | he bases the universe on the meanest of existents | The Greek ὑποβαλλόμενος is the same word as in "passing off" Numenius' work: the third charge is most naturally that what he passes off is the poorest of things. MacKenna reads it as "putting under", a foundation for the universe. |
| Life.17.p3 | a writer who, like Plotinus, now arraigned for the opinion we also hold | A hard place. Who "the man" is the Greek does not say plainly: most likely Numenius, whose thought some say agrees with ours. MacKenna takes him to be Plotinus. |
| Life.17.p4 | A busy man and far from the teachings of our master I must needs correct and recant | A hard place. The "distance" (διαστάσει) is most naturally the distance of the charge from Plotinus' doctrines, which Amelius sets straight and disowns; MacKenna makes Amelius himself far from the master's teachings. |
| Life.18.p2 | From that time on I put faith in Plotinus' writings | The Greek ἐπιστεύθην with "the books" is "I was entrusted with Plotinus' books": the books were put in his care (as chapter 24 shows), not that he came to believe in them. |
| Life.20.p4 | the Platonic Successors at Athens, Theodorus and Eubulus | The Greek name is Θεόδοτος, Theodotus. |
| Life.20.p4 | Origen wrote on Spirit-Beings | The title is περὶ δαιμόνων, On the daemons, the book named in chapter 3. |
| Life.20.p4 | some writing of a metaphysical order | "Of a metaphysical order" is MacKenna's: the Greek says only "if one of them wrote something". |
| Life.20.p5 | the two living at Athens | The Greek καταβιώσαντες is "who lived out their lives" in the city: they were dead when Longinus wrote, not "living". |
| Life.20.p5 | merely verses and duty-speeches | The Greek λόγους ἐπιδεικτικούς are "display speeches", the orator's showpieces, not speeches of duty. |
| Life.20.p7 | Content with setting side by side the most generally adopted theories and marking off the better from the worse | The Greek says the opposite: they took no care "either to gather what most hold or to judge what is better" (οὐδ’ οὖν ἢ συναγωγῆς ... ἢ κρίσεως τοῦ βελτίονος ἐπιμεληθέντες). |
| Life.20.p8 | My own method has been different | The Greek says the opposite: "we have done this already in other writings too" (ἤδη μὲν οὖν καὶ δι’ ἄλλων τουτὶ πεποιήκαμεν), that is, examined these men's opinions. |
| Life.21.p1 | followed the Pythagorean and Platonic schools | The Greek has "the doctrines of the Pythagoreans, and his own" (τὰ τῶν Πυθαγορείων αὐτοῦ τε): "Platonic" is MacKenna's, and he leaves out "his own". |
| Life.22.p2 | I Phoebus, Bathychaites, singing in the midst | βαθυχαίτης is an epithet, "thick-haired" (of deep hair), not a name; and "singing" is MacKenna's: the god says only that he is in their midst. |
| Life.22.p3 | Celestial ! Man at first | The Greek δαῖμον is "daemon", the first word of the poem's turn: Plotinus, a man before, is now a daemon, which the next line says outright ("the diviner lot of a daemon"). |
| Life.22.p4 | not granted to all that labour in wisdom's quest | The Greek οὔτις is "no one": the things Plotinus saw were such as "none" of the seekers of wisdom could easily see, not "not all". |
| Life.22.p4 | crowned with unfading life | The Greek κορυσσάμενος is "armed" (helmeted, as a warrior), and ζαμενέσσι ... ζωῇσι "mighty lives": nothing is crowned or unfading. |
| Life.23.p1 | that God appeared, the God throned above the Intellectual-Principle | MacKenna leaves out that this god has "neither shape nor any form" (ὁ μήτε μορφὴν μήτε τινὰ ἰδέαν ἔχων). |
| Life.23.p2 | by no mere latent fitness but by the ineffable Act | The Greek ἐνεργείᾳ ... καὶ οὐ δυνάμει is Aristotle's pair, "in act and not in potency": the union really happened, and was not only possible. |
| Life.23.p3 | not vouchsafed to all that take the philosophic path | As in the oracle, the Greek is "none" (οὔτις), not "not all". |
| Life.24.p2 | 8. On Evil. | Porphyry's title is "Where evils come from" (πόθεν τὰ κακά). |
| Life.24.p3 | 3. On the Stars. | Porphyry's title is "Whether the stars act" (εἰ ποιεῖ τὰ ἄστρα), the question the treatise asks. |
| Life.25.p1 | the class dealing with the origin of man | The Greek says the problem of the daemon is studied "in general" (καθόλου) and is one also "for those who look into the births of men" (τοῖς τὰ κατὰ τὰς γενέσεις τῶν ἀνθρώπων σκεπτομένοις), the casters of nativities; MacKenna has "not discussed as it is in itself" and "the origin of man". |
| Life.26.p2 | will be found Developments | The Greek ἐπιχειρήματα are "arguments" (Porphyry's outlines of the reasoning), not developments. |
| I.1.2.p1 | which Reason manifests | The Greek ἥντινα ἂν φήνῃ ὁ λόγος means "whatever the argument shows it to be": the "reason" (λόγος) here is the discussion now under way, not the soul's faculty of Reason. |
| I.1.4.p2 | Compare the passage (in Plato) where we read that " it is absurd to suppose that the Soul weaves " | MacKenna puts the saying in Plato; it is Aristotle's, in On the Soul I.4 (408b11-15), where he says that to call the soul angry is like saying that it weaves or builds. |
| I.1.7.p2 | Discursive-Reasoning, Sense-Knowledge and Intellection | The Greek has διάνοιαι καὶ δόξαι καὶ νοήσεις, "reasonings, opinions and acts of thought": δόξαι are opinions, not "sense-knowledge". |
| I.1.8.p1 | This also we possess as the summit of our being. | The Greek says we have intellect "above us" (ὑπεράνω ἡμῶν); MacKenna makes it the summit of our own being. |
| I.1.8.p1 | one of the emanations from the Intellectual-Principle | The Greek calls it a state (ἕξις) that the soul has, one of the things that come from intellect; the word "emanation" is MacKenna's. |
| I.1.8.p2 | the Divinity is contained in the Intellectual-Principle and Authentic-Existence | The Greek says God "rides upon" (ἐποχούμενον) the intelligible nature and real being: he is above it, not contained in it. |
| I.1.10.p1 | they are its repugnances, desires, sympathies | The Greek names "envies, jealousies and pities" (φθόνοι καὶ ζῆλοι καὶ ἔλεοι), as the feelings that show the vices belong to the compound. |
| I.1.12.p1 | We may take either view at will : they are easily reconciled. | The Greek is more careful: one may side with whichever account one wishes, "but perhaps one could find" how they do not conflict. There is no "easily". |
| I.1.12.p2 | Thus the Life is one thing, the Act is another and the Expiator yet another. | The Greek says the soul has "another life and other activities", and "what is punished is something different": not three things, but the soul's own life against the compound that is punished. τὸ κολαζόμενον is what is punished, not an "expiator". |
| I.1.13.p1 | Does this mean that the Soul reasons by possession (by contact with the matters of enquiry) ? | The Greek asks whether we inquired "by having" the soul; the words in brackets are MacKenna's guess, and not in the Greek. |
| I.1.13.p1 | Its Act subsists without movement | The Greek asks a question, "will it not, then, be moved?", and answers that we must grant the soul a movement that is not a body's: the soul is not said to be without movement. |
| I.4.1.p2 | it is impossible to deny the good of life to any order of living things | The Greek says that whoever takes living well away from "the other animals" is absurd; the plants have just been set aside, so it does not speak of every order of living things. |
| I.4.2.p1 | are really denying it to all living things | The Greek says they "run the risk" (κινδυνεύουσιν) of not granting it even to all the animals: the point is about the animals, and is put as a likelihood. |
| I.4.2.p3 | Let us try basing it upon Life. | These words are not in the Greek of this chapter, which ends with the people who are at a loss. The next chapter begins "let us say from the beginning what we take being happy to be", and only then puts it in life. |
| I.4.3.p2 | If mere Being is insufficient | There is no such clause in the Greek, which says: "if being happy belongs to what has life in the full (ἄγαν), that is, to what lacks nothing of life". |
| I.4.3.p3 | The Good, as a Divine Hypostasis | The words in brackets are MacKenna's explanation; the Greek says only "the nature of the Good". |
| I.4.4.p2 | for nothing is good that lies outside him | The Greek says "there is no good that he does not have" (οὐδὲν ... ἀγαθὸν ὃ μὴ ἔχει): it is about what he has, not about where goods lie. |
| I.4.4.p3 | to that in him which stands apart from the Supreme | The Greek says "what in him does not have intellect" (τὸ ἐν αὐτῷ νοῦν οὐκ ἔχον): the part without intellect, not a part that stands apart from the Supreme. |
| I.4.5.p1 | misery and disgrace | The Greek names poverty (πενία) and obscurity, or lack of good name (ἀδοξία): not misery and disgrace. |
| I.4.7.p1 | but they do serve towards the integrity of his being | The Greek says only that they contribute "to being" (πρὸς τὸ εἶναι), to his existing, as their opposites tend "towards not being". |
| I.4.7.p2 | or . . . Zeus ... by mortality among mortals | νὴ Δία is an oath, "by Zeus!": the Greek reads "who thinks wood and stones, and, by Zeus, the deaths of mortals, great things". The wood and stones are the razed city. |
| I.4.7.p3 | into his enemies' hands, into prison | The Greek says only "if he is led away captive" (αἰχμάλωτος), as a prisoner of war; and "the way out" is out of life, not an escape from prison. |
| I.4.8.p1 | when he is put to torture he will plan what is to be done | The Greek says "if it is drawn out" (εἰ παρατείνοι), of the pain; no torture is named. He "will take counsel", keeping his power over himself (τὸ αὐτεξούσιον). |
| I.4.10.p1 | When the Intellect is in upward orientation | The Greek says that awareness comes about "when the thought bends back" (ἀνακάμπτοντος τοῦ νοήματος); nothing is said of intellect turned upwards, and the words in brackets that follow are MacKenna's. |
| I.4.12.p1 | nor in any violent emotions | The Greek names περιχαρείας, transports of joy or wild delight: not violent emotions in general. |
| I.4.13.p1 | like a part of his being | The Greek says the greatest study is "always at hand and with him" (πρόχειρον ἀεὶ καὶ μετ’ αὐτοῦ); it does not call it a part of his being. |
| I.4.14.p1 | even a happy balance of temperament | The Greek has "nor, again, in perceiving well" (οὐδ’ αὖ ἐν τῷ αἰσθάνεσθαι εὖ): good senses, not good temperament. |
| I.4.15.p3 | it is to himself and in his own great concern that he is the Sage | The Greek says "he is such towards himself too, and in his own affairs" (τοιοῦτος γὰρ καὶ περὶ αὑτὸν καὶ ἐν τοῖς ἑαυτοῦ): the sternness is not kept for others; he is the same with himself. |
| I.5.2.p1 | at once time supplants moral excellence as the measure of felicity | The Greek says only that happiness "will no longer be measured by virtue" (τῇ ἀρετῇ); that time takes its place is MacKenna's gloss. |
| I.5.6.p2 | true happiness is not vague and fluid | The Greek says positively that happiness "has a limit and a bound" (ὅρον τε καὶ πέρας) and is always the same. |
| I.5.7.p1 | a mimic of eternity | The Greek says εἰκὼν αἰῶνος, "an image of eternity", Plato's words in the Timaeus (37d): an image, not a mimic. |
| I.5.8.p1 | last year's morality | The Greek is τὸ δὲ τῆς φρονήσεως, ὅτι πέρυσιν ἐφρόνουν, "and of wisdom, that last year I was wise": wisdom, not morality. |
| I.5.10.p2 | the good of the act is for all alike, no matter whose was the saving hand | The Greek says that "the pleasure at the saving of one's country could come to him even if another did it": it is about the good man's own pleasure, not a good shared by all. |
| I.6.1.p1 | another for the bodiless | The Greek asks whether beauty is "one thing in a body, another in something else" (ἐν ἄλλῳ); it does not yet speak of the bodiless. |
| I.6.1.p2 | so that there is a good deal between being body and being beautiful | The Greek says "as if being bodies were one thing and being beautiful another" (ἄλλου ... ἄλλου): the two are different, not far apart. |
| I.6.1.p4 | morality means weakness of will | The Greek says that self-control (σωφροσύνη) is foolishness (ἠλιθιότης), and justice a "noble simplicity" (γενναία εὐήθεια): these are the bad views of Thrasymachus in the Republic, not "weakness of will" and "honesty". |
| I.6.2.p1 | something which the soul names as from an ancient knowledge | The Greek says the soul speaks of it "as if it understood it" (ὥσπερ συνεῖσα): no ancient knowledge is named, though the soul is "reminded" of itself later in the chapter. |
| I.6.2.p3 | by communicating in the thought that flows from the Divine | The Greek says "by sharing in a reason (λόγου) that has come from gods" (ἀπὸ θεῶν), in the plural: a forming principle, not a thought, and from gods rather than from "the Divine". |
| I.6.3.p1 | never in doubt whenever any lovely thing presents itself for judgement | The Greek says that nothing has more authority to judge its own objects "when the rest of the soul judges along with it" (ὅταν καὶ ἡ ἄλλη συνεπικρίνῃ ψυχή): a judgement shared with the rest of the soul, not a power that is never in doubt. |
| I.6.3.p3 | The beauty of colour is also the outcome of a unification | The Greek says the beauty of colour is "simple" (ἁπλοῦν), by shape and mastery of the dark: Plotinus has just said that colours are beautiful without being composite, so they are not a unification of parts. |
| I.6.5.p2 | courage of the majestic face | The Greek says courage "with a grim face" (βλοσυρὸν ἔχουσαν πρόσωπον), fierce to look at, as Homer's Ajax is "grim of face" (Iliad 7.212); not majestic. |
| I.6.5.p3 | thinking, in the little thought it has, only of the perishable and the base | The Greek says "thinking all that it thinks as mortal and lowly things" (πάντα φρονοῦσα ἃ δὴ καὶ φρονεῖ θνητὰ καὶ ταπεινά): "the little thought it has" is MacKenna's. |
| I.6.5.p4 | by a fall, a descent into body, into Matter | The Greek speaks of "mixing and blending and inclining" (νεύσει) towards body and matter: a leaning, not a fall or a descent. |
| I.6.6.p1 | And Wisdom is but the Act of the Intellectual-Principle withdrawn | The Greek says wisdom is "thinking" (νόησις), "in a turning away from the things below": the soul's own thinking, not an act of intellect. |
| I.6.6.p2 | Hence the Soul heightened to the Intellectual-Principle is beautiful to all its power | The Greek says the soul led up to intellect "is beautiful the more" (ἐπὶ τὸ μᾶλλον): more beautiful, not beautiful to all its power. |
| I.6.6.p2 | for from the Divine comes all the Beauty and all the Good in beings | The Greek says "from there comes the beautiful and the other portion of beings" (ἡ μοῖρα ἡ ἑτέρα τῶν ὄντων); the Good is not named. The phrase is hard, and translators read it differently. |
| I.6.7.p1 | Even the desire of it is to be desired as a Good | The Greek says "it is desired as good, and the desire is towards it" (ἐφετὸν μὲν γὰρ ὡς ἀγαθὸν καὶ ἡ ἔφεσις πρὸς τοῦτο): the Good is what is desired; the desire itself is not called desirable. |
| I.6.7.p1 | who will set all their forces towards it | The Greek says those who "have turned round" (ἐπιστραφεῖσι): the soul's turning back to what is above, not an effort of all its forces. |
| I.6.7.p2 | Gods or Supernals | The Greek has "of gods or daemons" (θεῶν ... ἢ δαιμόνων), the spirits between gods and men; "Supernals" is MacKenna's word. |
| I.6.7.p3 | spurning the world of sense from beneath his feet | The Greek says only "leaving these behind and looking down on them" (καταλιπών τις ταῦτα καὶ ὑπεριδών), the kingdoms and rule just named; the world of sense under his feet is MacKenna's picture. |
| I.6.9.p1 | the perfect goodness surely established in the stainless shrine | The Greek says "self-control standing on a holy pedestal" (σωφροσύνην ἐν ἁγνῷ βεβῶσαν βάθρῳ), the Phaedrus' words (254b): self-control, not "perfect goodness". |
| I.6.9.p3 | who cares to see God and Beauty | The Greek says "the Good and the beautiful" (τἀγαθόν τε καὶ καλόν): the Good, not God. |
| I.6.9.p4 | the Primal Good and the Primal Beauty have the one dwelling-place | The Greek puts this as another way of speaking: "or he will place the Good and the first beautiful in the same" (ἢ ἐν τῷ αὐτῷ ... θήσεται), "but in any case the beautiful is there": a choice left open, not a conclusion. |
| I.7.1.p2 | it can but remain unmoved | The Greek says the Good is "at rest" (ἐν ἡσύχῳ), "in quiet": not "unmoved" in Aristotle's sense, though the meaning is close. |
| I.7.1.p3 | try all you will to separate the light from the sun, or the sun from its light, for ever the light is in the sun | The Greek says "even if you wish to cut it off on one side, the light is towards the sun" (πρὸς τὸν ἥλιον): the light still stretches back to its source. "Or the sun from its light" is not in the Greek, and the light is "towards" the sun, not "in" it. |
| I.7.2.p1 | But the Universe outside ; how is it aligned towards the Good ? | The Greek asks "and all the other things, how are they towards it?" (τὰ δ’ ἄλλα πάντα): it does not speak of a universe outside. |
| I.7.2.p1 | are no more than images of the Ideal-Form | The Greek says that what each thing shares in are "images of being and of the one, and the form likewise" (εἴδωλα ὄντος καὶ ἑνός, καὶ τὸ εἶδος ὡσαύτως): images of being and the one, not of the Ideal-Form. |
| I.7.3.p1 | the misfortune is still life, a life of a definite character | The Greek says only that life is evil there too, "since it is not life alone" (ὅτι μὴ ζωὴ μόνον): it is life with something added, the punishment; "a life of a definite character" is MacKenna's. |
| I.7.3.p2 | feel itself at home | The Greek says the soul "will be able to receive both" (ἀμφοτέρων δεκτική), life and death: it can be in either state, not that it feels at home in both. |
| I.8.1.p2 | are nearer to Non-Being | The Greek says the worse are "rather a privation" (στέρησις μᾶλλον), the lack of a form, Aristotle's word; MacKenna's "nearer to Non-Being" says something else. Where the question comes back, he has "utter Lack" for the same word. |
| I.8.2.p2 | And the First Act is the Act of The Good stationary within Itself, and the First Existence is the self-contained Existence of The Good | In the Greek it is intellect that "is the first activity of that one and its first being" (πρώτη ἐνέργεια ἐκείνου καὶ πρώτη οὐσία), while the Good "stays in itself": the Good does not itself act or exist as intellect does. MacKenna gives the first activity and the first existence to the Good. |
| I.8.2.p3 | circles around the Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says the soul "dances" round it (χορεύουσα), as a chorus round an altar: Plotinus' image for the soul's ordered life about what it loves. |
| I.8.3.p1 | of an utterly different order from Authentic-Being | The Greek says non-being here is "only other than being" (ἕτερον μόνον τοῦ ὄντος), Plato's sense of non-being in the Sophist; MacKenna makes it "utterly different". |
| I.8.3.p1 | there is no question here of movement or position with regard to Being | The Greek says this non-being is not like "motion and rest, which are about being" (ὡς κίνησις καὶ στάσις ἡ περὶ τὸ ὄν): two of the Sophist's greatest kinds, which are "not being" only because they are other than it. MacKenna's "position" is rest (στάσις). |
| I.8.3.p2 | that part is all lawless void | The Greek says only "that too is all of these" (καὶ αὐτὸ πάντα ταῦτα): the whole list of what evil is. "Lawless void" is MacKenna's. |
| I.8.3.p3 | become a cause of evil by consecration to this Absolute | The Greek says such a thing is evil "by looking towards it" (βλέπον πρὸς αὐτό), or "makes things such" (ποιητικόν): there is no "consecration". |
| I.8.4.p1 | Soul, on the contrary, since not every Soul is evil, is not an evil Kind | The Greek has two claims: the soul "in itself is not evil" (καθ’ ἑαυτὴν μὲν οὐ κακή), and "nor is every soul evil"; MacKenna leaves out "in itself" and makes the second the reason for a conclusion of his own. |
| I.8.4.p1 | all the states, foreign to the true nature | The Greek calls them "involuntary affections" (ἀκούσια παθήματα): the soul does not choose them. |
| I.8.4.p1 | not by their true value but by the mere test of like and dislike | The Greek says the false opinions make the soul "think evil and good the things it flees and pursues": it mistakes what it avoids and seeks for evil and good. There is no "true value" or "like and dislike". |
| I.8.5.p3 | the Evil which holds men down binds them against their will | The Greek speaks of the evils (ἃ δ’ ἂν ἀνθρώπους κατάσχῃ) "that take hold of men", in the plural; it means the evils, not "the Evil". |
| I.8.6.p1 | with justice and piety in the light of philosophy | The Greek, quoting Plato, says "just and holy with wisdom" (μετὰ φρονήσεως): practical wisdom, not philosophy. |
| I.8.6.p2 | The Good in the Supernal | The Greek says only "that Good" (ἐκείνῳ τῷ ἀγαθῷ). |
| I.8.6.p2 | the good thing by which Matter is brought to order | The Greek says virtue is "a good, which makes us master matter" (ὃ κρατεῖν τῆς ὕλης ποιεῖ): mastering, not ordering. |
| I.8.6.p2 | established by practical proof | The Greek says "shown by induction" (τῇ ἐπαγωγῇ δεδειγμένον), by going through the cases one by one: Aristotle's argument in the Categories. |
| I.8.7.p2 | Since you possess only a derivative being | The Greek of the Timaeus says "since you have come into being" (ἐπείπερ ἐγένεσθε); MacKenna's words of introduction, "in which the Father of Gods addresses the Divinities of the lower sphere", are his own explanation. |
| I.8.8.p1 | not in the concrete signification but in the presence or absence of just such forms | The Greek says "fillings, emptyings, and not fillings simply, but fillings with such and such things" (πληρώσεις τοιῶνδε): being full of this food or that. There is nothing about concrete significations or the absence of forms. |
| I.8.8.p3 | the incoming Idea should have been able to conquer the Matter | The Greek says only "but one should master it, someone might say" (ἀλλὰ κρατεῖν ἔδει): it is we, or the reason in us, who should master the body's desires; "the incoming Idea" is MacKenna's. |
| I.8.8.p3 | in times of stress, we are not the same | The Greek speaks of being "full" and "empty" (πλήρεις, κενοί), filled with this or with that: our desires and thoughts change with what we have eaten and drunk, not with stress and peace. |
| I.8.9.p1 | by means of the philosophic habit | The Greek says "by intellect itself and by wisdom" (νῷ αὐτῷ καὶ φρονήσει), and gives the reason MacKenna leaves out: "for it knows itself". |
| I.8.9.p2 | an ugly appearance in Matter | The Greek has "an ugly face" (αἰσχρόν τι πρόσωπον), an example taken from life. |
| I.8.10.p1 | give it Quality and in its very Evil it would almost be a Form | The Greek says matter is evil not by having a quality, "so that perhaps it is not evil as being a form" (ἵνα μὴ ᾖ ἴσως κακὴ εἶδος οὖσα), but as a nature contrary to form; MacKenna's "give it Quality" and "almost" are his reading. |
| I.8.11.p1 | This objection may be answered by applying the principle to the case of Evil in the Soul | In the Greek the objection goes on: if evil is privation, it is in the soul, as its vice, and "nothing outside"; there is no answer until the next paragraph ("But if privation..."). MacKenna makes the objector's conclusion into an answer. |
| I.8.11.p2 | it has much good in it | The Greek says the soul "has something good" (ἔχουσά τι ἀγαθόν), "a trace of intellect": the argument needs only some good, not much. |
| I.8.13.p2 | where, fallen from all our resemblance to the Divine, we lie in gloom and mud | The Greek says "sinking into it, one will have fallen into dark mud" (εἰς βόρβορον σκοτεινόν): "fallen from all our resemblance to the Divine" is MacKenna's gloss on "the place of unlikeness". |
| I.8.13.p2 | And the death of Soul is twofold | The Greek says only "its death": "while still in the body" to be sunk in matter, "and when it has gone out" to lie there. The two times are there, but "twofold" is MacKenna's. |
| I.8.14.p1 | as hasty to compromises | The Greek says the evil soul is "rash in its assents" (προπετῆ εἰς συγκαταθέσεις): quick to agree to impressions, the Stoics' word for judgement, not "compromises". |
| I.8.14.p1 | burned away by every heat | The Greek says such weak things are "easily carried about" (ῥᾳδίαν τὴν φοράν) by winds and the heat of the sun: carried about, not burned away. |
| I.8.14.p3 | for the Soul cannot lift up this foreign thing close by, since the evil of it makes it invisible | The Greek says the source of light "does not bear with" matter (οὐ γὰρ ἀνέχεται αὐτήν), though matter is present, "because matter does not see" it, by its badness: it is matter that cannot see, not matter that is invisible, and nothing is "lifted up". |
| I.8.15.p2 | within that place of its choice | The Greek says the soul must be "with intellect alone, settled in it" (ἐν αὐτῷ ἱδρυμένην): "that place of its choice" is MacKenna's. |
| I.8.15.p3 | not destitute of Images of the Good and Beautiful | The Greek has "images of the beautiful" (εἰδώλοις τοῦ καλοῦ): "the Good" is added. |
| I.9.1.p1 | For wheresoever it go, it will be in some definite condition | The Greek says "it will go out having something" (ἔχουσά τι), something of the body, "if it goes out at all": the reason for the opening command, not a condition it will be in. |
| I.9.1.p2 | movements which it is unlawful to indulge | The Greek says only "but one must do nothing" (δεῖ δὲ μηδὲν πράττειν): nothing is done that would come from these feelings. There is no law in the Greek. |
| I.9.1.p2 | That is not likely in the Sage | The Greek's σπουδαῖος is "the good man", serious about virtue, as elsewhere in the Workshop (see I.4); MacKenna's "Sage" makes him a Stoic figure. |
| II.1.1.p1 | however true an explanation, is utterly inadequate | The Greek says only that this would "perhaps" (ἴσως) be true, but would give no clarity (σαφήνεια): the complaint is that it explains nothing, not that it is "utterly inadequate". |
| II.1.1.p1 | The Will of God is able to cope with the ceaseless flux | In the Greek this is part of what "someone will perhaps claim" (μήποτε ... ἀξιώσει), a view Plotinus puts forward to test; MacKenna states it as fact. |
| II.1.1.p3 | by such communication | The Greek says "even in this way and in this manner" (κἂν οὕτω καὶ τοῦτον τὸν τρόπον); there is no word for "communication". |
| II.1.2.p2 | no need, then, to call on a soul or on any perdurable conjunction | The Greek says he would need nothing from the soul "except" (ἢ) that the two be always together to make up a living being: the lasting union with soul is the one thing still needed, where MacKenna has it not needed either. |
| II.1.3.p2 | co-operates by its upward tendency as earth by its lingering below | The Greek says only that fire is "sharp and quick" because it does not stay down here, as earth is because it does not stay above; there is no word for "co-operates". |
| II.1.3.p3 | occur in some of the superiors from which the celestial fire depends | The Greek says "if it had something else" (εἰ ἄλλου τινὸς ἔχοι), some other element besides fire in the heaven, which might flow away too; there are no "superiors" on which the fire depends. |
| II.1.4.p1 | docile to the purposes of the stars | The Greek says the fire there "suits the nature of the stars" (τῇ τῶν ἄστρων πρόσφορον φύσει); there is nothing about their "purposes". |
| II.1.4.p2 | moving in its marvellous might | The Greek says the soul is "set" or "lies" (κειμένην) next to the best things, with a wonderful power; it is not said to move. |
| II.1.5.p2 | whose region does not in fact extend so low as to the moon | The Greek says the opposite: the things under the heaven are not parts of it, "or else the heaven would not reach (only) as far as the moon" (ἢ οὐ μέχρι σελήνης ὁ οὐρανός). The heaven ends at the moon; what is below it is not the heaven. |
| II.1.5.p3 | bringing with it some effluence from the Divine Reason | The Greek says the higher soul comes when the body is already formed, "helping a little, from reasoning" (μικρὰ ἐκ λογισμοῦ), towards its being; there is no "effluence" and no "Divine Reason", only our own reasoning. |
| II.1.6.p1 | Plato accepts it as indicated by all the appearances | The Greek says Plato judged the view by "the likely" (τῷ εἰκότι), the "likely account" of the Timaeus, not by "all the appearances". The appearances come next, and lead the other way. |
| II.1.6.p1 | derive from them the impression of illumination | The Greek names sight and "the grasp of touch" (τὴν τῆς ἁφῆς ἀντίληψιν): to both senses the stars seem made of fire. MacKenna leaves out touch. |
| II.1.6.p3 | It is still not proven that fire is necessary to the visibility of earth | The Greek is a question to be looked into (ἐπισκεπτέον, "we must look at whether"), and its point is the next sentence: if each element needed the others, nothing would have a being of its own. |
| II.1.6.p5 | there are living beings of fire among the Celestials | The Greek says "among the daemons" (δαιμόνων): MacKenna's "Celestials" makes them gods of the heaven. |
| II.1.7.p3 | extinguished by the currents of air opposed to it | The Greek says the flame is quenched when it "meets more air" (ἐντυχοῦσαν πλείονι ἀέρι); nothing is said of currents opposing it. |
| II.1.7.p3 | constitutes also the still higher heavenly bodies | The Greek says "the rest of the heaven" (τὸν δ’ ἄλλον οὐρανόν), the heaven between the stars, is of this light too, and is not seen; there are no "still higher heavenly bodies". |
| II.1.8.p1 | no match for what it would be opposing in those higher regions | The Greek compares that fire with the fire down here: it is "lesser, and not as strong as the things here" (ἰσχύον τε οὐκ ἴσα τοῖς ἐνθάδε), since flame dies down as it rises (chapter 7). |
| II.1.8.p2 | a Principle not dwelling in the higher regions | The Greek says only "another nature presiding over them" (ἄλλης ἐπιστατούσης φύσεως); where it dwells is MacKenna's gloss. |
| II.2.1.p1 | being self-centred it is not of unlimited extension (and consequently must move ceaselessly to be omnipresent) | A hard place. The Greek says only "or is it in itself, not being continuous?" (ἢ ἔστιν ἐν αὑτῇ οὐ συνεχὴς οὖσα;); MacKenna's "unlimited extension" and his words in brackets are his reading of it. |
| II.2.1.p3 | its straight line would finally fling it outside the universe | The Greek says only that fire "will be scattered" (σκεδασθήσεται) in a straight line; "outside the universe" is MacKenna's (Plotinus says next that there is no place beyond). |
| II.2.1.p3 | itself its own sphere | The Greek says "itself its own place" (αὐτὸ αὑτοῦ τόπος). |
| II.2.1.p4 | a living thing whose nature binds it within a body | The Greek says "a body that is alive and in its natural state" (ζῶντι καὶ κατὰ φύσιν δὲ ἔχοντι σώματι); nothing binds a living thing within a body. |
| II.2.1.p4 | the custom the All-Soul has established | The Greek says nature is "what has been ordered by the whole soul" (τὸ ὑπὸ ψυχῆς τῆς πάσης ταχθέν): an ordering, not a custom. |
| II.2.1.p4 | dwelt wholly and solely in the realm in which every member is at rest | The Greek "as being only there where each thing stays" (ὡς ἐκεῖ οὔσης μόνον, οὗ ἕκαστον μένει) means a place in the heaven, not the intelligible realm; MacKenna reads "there" (ἐκεῖ) as Plotinus' "There", the world above. |
| II.2.2.p1 | (which it desires to possess alone) | A hard place: the Greek says only "because it does not have it alone" (ὅτι μὴ μόνον ἔχει), which may mean that it has other things besides the soul, or that it does not have the soul in one place only. MacKenna's words in brackets are his. |
| II.2.2.p3 | what in us is of this circling nature (the soul) | The Greek says "what is spherical in us" (τὸ σφαιροειδὲς ἡμῶν), which is earthy: the head, which the Timaeus (44d) makes spherical in imitation of the All. It is a part of the body, not the soul. |
| II.2.2.p3 | the Spirit which dwells with the Soul does thus circle about the divinity | The Greek πνεῦμα here is the "breath", the fine body around the soul that the later Platonists call its vehicle; MacKenna's capital "Spirit" can be read as something higher. |
| II.2.2.p3 | to each a revolution around their common centre | The Greek gives each star a motion "around their own centre" (τὴν περὶ τὸ κέντρον αὐτῶν), each turning in its own place, as in the Timaeus (40a-b); not around a common centre. |
| II.2.3.p1 | another phase possesses sensation, while yet another includes the Reason | A hard place: MacKenna counts three powers. The Greek "the one whose nature is to perceive and the one that receives the reason that forms opinions" (ἡ δὲ αἰσθάνεσθαι πεφυκυῖα καὶ ἡ λόγον δοξαστικὸν δεχομένη) goes with one singular verb and "itself" (ἑαυτήν), and the chapter goes on with two: the lower power woven through the All, and the perceiving power above. |
| II.2.3.p1 | this upward tension | The Greek says "its turning" (ἡ δὲ ἐπιστροφὴ αὐτῆς), Plotinus' word for the soul's turning back to what is above it. |
| II.3.1.p2 | so that they must be supposed to change their plans as they stand at their zeniths or are declining | The Greek says "on the centres" (ἐπὶ κέντρων), the astrologers' four cardinal points of the sky: where a star rises, culminates, sets, and is lowest under the earth. The zenith is only one of them. |
| II.3.1.p3 | so that, after all, they possess no definite nature but vary according to their angles of aspect | The Greek says only "as if they were not themselves" (ὥσπερ οὐχ αὑτῶν ὄντας), but one kind when they see and another when they do not; "no definite nature" and "angles of aspect" are MacKenna's. |
| II.3.2.p1 | emanations merely corporeal cannot differ greatly from star to star | The Greek says the outflow from each is "the same" (τῆς ἀπορροῆς ἑκάστων τῆς αὐτῆς οὔσης), not that it cannot differ greatly. |
| II.3.2.p3 | suppose the stars to have life and mind and to be effective by deliberate purpose | The Greek says only "being ensouled" (ἔμψυχα ὄντα), acting "by choice"; "mind" is MacKenna's. |
| II.3.3.p3 | Like the birds of augury, the living beings of the heavens, having no lot or part with us, may serve incidentally to foreshow the future | The Greek puts it as a condition: their work towards us is incidental "if, as with birds, signifying is incidental" to them (εἰ ὥσπερ ὄρνισι κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς τὸ σημαίνειν). MacKenna makes it a statement. |
| II.3.5.p1 | When the cold planet, we are told, is in opposition to the cold, both become menacing | The Greek says the cold one in opposition "to the hot" (τὸν ψυχρὸν τῷ θερμῷ), which is why Plotinus says they ought rather to blend. |
| II.3.5.p1 | as if the first one could be darkened by night | The Greek says "the other" (τοῦ ἑτέρου), the fiery star that is glad at night; MacKenna makes it the first. |
| II.3.5.p2 | the reason alleged is that the force of Mars is all-sufficient since it contains more fire than it needs | A hard place: the Greek says only "for that one's is enough, being more fiery than towards that one" (αὐταρκεῖ γὰρ τὸ ἐκείνου πυρωδεστέρου ἢ πρὸς ἐκεῖνον ὄντος), which is probably damaged; "than it needs" is MacKenna's reading, and the modern version's. He names Mars where the Greek says "the fiery one". |
| II.3.5.p4 | it is to be the inciter of the animal spirits | The Greek says the gall had to "rouse spirit" (θυμὸν ἐγείρειν), the part of the soul that is angry; "animal spirits" is MacKenna's. |
| II.3.5.p4 | but all the members will be in sympathy with the entire animal frame to which they belong | The Greek says all are in sympathy "through what is without reason in them" (τῷ ἀλόγῳ αὐτῶν): the sympathy of the All works in its irrational life, not in reason. |
| II.3.6.p1 | And who could accept the fancy that their happiness comes from their seeing each other in this or that relative position and not from their own settled nature ? | The Greek says "and that there is no limit (πέρας) for them": no end to their desire. Nothing is said of their settled nature. |
| II.3.6.p2 | such a one, they see, has risen by so many degrees, representing so many of the periods of its upward path | The Greek says that as many degrees as each sign rises in, "so many years" (ἐνιαυτοὺς τοσούτους) of the rising: the astrologers' way of reckoning the length of a life. "Periods" is MacKenna's. |
| II.3.7.p1 | Obviously, unless the particular is included under some general principle of order, there can be no signification. | The Greek says "for there would be no signifying in an ordered way if each thing did not come about" in order (οὐ γὰρ ἂν ἐσημαίνετο τεταγμένως μὴ ἑκάστων γινομένων): the order is in the events, not a general principle above them. |
| II.3.7.p2 | but more markedly so since in this case the parts are not merely members but themselves Alls, members of the loftier Kind | The Greek says only that they are "not only parts but also wholes, and greater" (μὴ μόνον μέρη, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὅλα καὶ μείζω); "members of the loftier Kind" is MacKenna's. |
| II.3.7.p3 | all is one scheme of differentiation, starting from the Firsts and working itself out in a continuous progression of Kinds | The Greek says only "for something else comes from these too, and next, by nature, another" (ἄλλο τι καὶ ἐκ τούτων καὶ ἐφεξῆς κατὰ φύσιν ἄλλο); the Firsts and the Kinds are MacKenna's. |
| II.3.8.p1 | alike in its direct course (its tendence towards the divine ?) and in its divagation (its activity towards the lower ?) | The Greek says "whether it goes straight or is thrown into disorder" (κἂν εὐθυποροῖ καὶ ταράττοιτο αὖ): the soul's acting rightly or wrongly, which justice then follows. MacKenna's guesses in brackets are his. |
| II.3.8.p1 | it is the cause of all by its possession of the Thought of the First Principle | The Greek says soul makes all things "having the standing of a principle" (ἀρχῆς ἔχουσα λόγον): it is itself a principle. Nothing is said of the First Principle or its thought. |
| II.3.8.p2 | For our part, nature keeps us upon the work of the Soul | The Greek says "we do the works of soul according to nature" (ψυχῆς ἔργα κατὰ φύσιν ποιοῦμεν): nature is how we do them, not what keeps us on them. |
| II.3.9.p1 | To Plato the Spindle represents the co-operation of the moving and the stable elements of the kosmic circuit | The Greek says the spindle is "what wanders and what does not wander" (τό τε πλανώμενον καὶ τὸ ἀπλανὲς) in the revolution: the planets and the fixed stars. "Co-operation" is MacKenna's. |
| II.3.9.p2 | The " We " is the actual resultant of a Being whose nature includes, with certain sensibilities, the power of governing them. | The Greek says "what we truly are" (ὅπερ ἐσμὲν κατ’ ἀλήθειαν ἡμεῖς), to whom nature gave the power to master the passions. For MacKenna the "We" is a "resultant"; for Plotinus it is the true self, not the composite. |
| II.3.9.p2 | virtue the unconquerable | The Greek ἀδέσποτον is "with no master over it", Plato's word: the herald of the myth of Er says that "virtue has no master", and each will have more or less of it as he honours it (Republic X, 617e). |
| II.3.9.p3 | no longer profiting, merely, by the significance of the sidereal system | The Greek says that for one who lives in destiny the stars "do not only signify" (οὐ μόνον σημαίνει) for him: they act on him as well. Nothing is said of profiting. |
| II.3.9.p4 | body is the agent while, at the same time, it becomes the vehicle through which is transmitted something of the star's will | The Greek says the body gives, a part to a part, "while the star's choice and its soul that truly is look to the best" (προαιρέσεως τοῦ ἄστρου καὶ ψυχῆς τῆς ὄντως αὐτοῦ πρὸς τὸ ἄριστον βλεπούσης). Nothing of the star's will is passed on through the body: the star's will is turned the other way. |
| II.3.9.p5 | But (with every allowance to the lower forces) all follows either upon that Highest or rather upon the Beings about It — we may think of the Divine as a fire | A hard place: the Greek says "the other things follow on it, or rather not on it, but on those around it" (παρακολουθεῖ δ’ αὐτῷ τὰ ἄλλα, μᾶλλον δ’ οὐκ αὐτῷ, ἀλλὰ τοῖς περὶ αὐτόν). "It" is masculine, most likely the star, not "that Highest"; and the fire is only an image of how heat spreads, not "the Divine". |
| II.3.9.p5 | " is a mighty spirit and its ways are subdivine." | The Greek is δαίμων μέγας, "a great daemon", and the passions in it are "daemonic" (δαιμόνια): Plato's words for Love in the Symposium (202d-e), a great daemon between gods and mortals. "Spirit" and "subdivine" hide the quotation. |
| II.3.10.p1 | for it could never touch body except under stress of a powerful inner impulse | The Greek says it would not come into a body "unless it had a great capacity to be affected" (μὴ μέγα τι παθητικὸν ἔχουσα): something passive in it, not an impulse. |
| II.3.11.p1 | and ambition ... in love . . . | MacKenna's dots mark a gap; Volkmann's text has none here: "what lies in the love of honour and is concerned with beauty" (τὸ τιμῆς ἐν ἔρωτι ὂν καὶ περὶ τὸ καλὸν ἔχον) makes a desire for what only seems beautiful. |
| II.3.11.p1 | intellectual power at its lowest produces the extreme of wickedness, for wickedness is a miscalculating effort towards Intelligence | The Greek πανουργία is "knavery", cleverness ready for anything, not "the extreme of wickedness"; and it comes from "the outflow of intellect" (νοῦ ἀπόρροια), not from intellect "at its lowest". |
| II.3.12.p1 | though sometimes Matter is the dominant, and the human principle takes the second place | The Greek says "matter prevails, and not nature" (ἡ ὕλη κρατεῖ, οὐχ ἡ φύσις); "the human principle" is MacKenna's. |
| II.3.13.p1 | the entire force of the Soul is represented in the All but, in the parts, Soul is present only in proportion to the degree of essential reality held by each of such partial objects | The Greek says only "in the whole there is all, but in the parts only as much as each is" (ἐν μὲν τῷ ὅλῳ ἐστὶ τὰ πάντα, ἐν δὲ τοῖς μέρεσι τοσοῦτον μόνον, ὅσον ἐστὶν ἕκαστον); "the degree of essential reality" is MacKenna's. |
| II.3.13.p3 | just as in us the members rank lower than the Soul | The Greek says "as are the second things of soul in us" (οἷα καὶ τὰ ἐν ἡμῖν ψυχῆς δεύτερα): the soul's own lower powers, not the members of the body. |
| II.3.13.p4 | which by the medium of the material frame, become a power to debasement, has been delivered over, though never in its essence, to the inferior order of being | The Greek says the part becomes "incidentally a cause of badness" (κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς φαυλότητος αἴτιον) to the soul that is in sympathy with the body; "though never in its essence" is MacKenna's turn of "incidentally", and the debasing body is his. |
| II.3.14.p1 | Wealth may be due to personal activity | The Greek ἀνδραγαθία is "manly excellence", brave and good deeds, not activity in general. |
| II.3.14.p1 | must be attributed to virtue exclusively | The Greek gives virtue alone "the most" (τὸ πλεῖστον), not all of it, and adds the divine gifts that came with it. |
| II.3.14.p2 | As for good fame, it is either deserved and then is due to the services done and to the merit of those appraising them | The Greek says the cause goes back to the deeds and "to the better" judgement in those who hold the opinion (τὸ παρὰ τοῖς δοξάζουσι βέλτιον): their good sense, not their merit. |
| II.3.15.p1 | that once done, only the Spindle-destiny is valid ; it fixes the chosen conditions irretrievably | The Greek says that Plato afterwards gives "those in the spindle" (τοὺς ἐν τῷ ἀτράκτῳ), the stars that turn in it, as "fellow workers" (συνεργοὺς), helping to bring what was chosen to completion; nothing is said of the spindle alone being valid. |
| II.3.15.p1 | the elected guardian-spirit | The Greek is ὁ δαίμων, the daemon each soul chooses in the myth of Er; "elected" is MacKenna's. |
| II.3.15.p2 | the Soul, possessing the Idea which belongs to a Principle | The Greek ἀρχῆς λόγον ἔχουσαν is "having the standing of a principle", as in chapter 8; "the Idea" is MacKenna's. |
| II.3.16.p1 | For the present let us explain in what sense we have described the All as the expressed idea of the Governing Soul. | The Greek says "in what sense we said that soul governs the All according to a forming principle" (πῶς τὸ κατὰ λόγον ψυχῆς διοικούσης τὸ πᾶν εἴπομεν), in chapter 13; MacKenna makes the All the soul's "expressed idea". |
| II.3.16.p3 | ever labouring to bring about perfection, planning to lead all to an unending state of excellence | The Greek says the soul is never freed from care "as one who had put the finish on the work and contrived once for all that it should be well for ever" (τέλος ἐπιθεῖσα τῷ ποιήματι καὶ ὅπως ἕξει καλῶς καὶ εἰς ἀεὶ ἅπαξ μηχανησαμένη): that is what it has not done. MacKenna turns it into the soul's plan. |
| II.3.16.p3 | the ruin and all the consequences of flaw | The Greek says "the works that come from vice" (τὰ ἀπὸ κακίας ἔργα): vice, not a flaw. |
| II.3.16.p4 | since, amid the clash of these antecedent Principles, Matter had already from its own stock produced the less good | The Greek speaks of matter "shaken" (τῷ σεισμῷ) by the forming principles that went before, as the receptacle of the Timaeus is shaken (52e-53a): not of a clash among the principles. |
| II.3.17.p1 | where the formation of the ring is conditioned by something distinct from that force itself (i.e. by the existence and nature of the water) | The Greek says "something else having given it the power to make this" (ἄλλου ἐνδόντος εἰς τοῦτο τὸ ποιεῖν): whatever started the ring, as a stone dropped in. MacKenna's words in brackets, the water's nature, are his. |
| II.3.17.p2 | hindering its own life | The Greek δυσχεραῖνον τὴν αὑτοῦ ζωήν is "discontented with its own life", finding it hard to bear; not hindering it. |
| II.3.18.p1 | Are the evils in the Universe necessary because it is of later origin than the Higher Sphere ? | The Greek asks whether they are necessary "because they follow on what went before" (ὅτι ἕπεται τοῖς προηγουμένοις), the things that came first in the chain of chapter 16; it says nothing of the All's being later than the higher sphere. |
| II.3.18.p1 | it stirs us to thoughtful living | The Greek says vice moves us "to wisdom" (εἰς φρόνησιν). |
| II.3.18.p2 | the divine Thought-Forms will pour forth into that phase of the Soul | The Greek says "the forming principles will flow" (ῥεύσονται οἱ λόγοι), the principles that chapter 17 says are not thoughts. |
| II.5.1.p1 | Actuality (an absolute, abstract Principle) | The Greek asks only whether activity (ἐνέργεια) is the same as being actually (τὸ ἐνεργείᾳ εἶναι); "an absolute, abstract Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| II.5.1.p2 | water (= primalliquid) as potentially bronze | The Greek says only "water" (τὸ ὕδωρ); "primal liquid" is MacKenna's gloss. |
| II.5.1.p2 | the Principle or Abstract in which potentiality | The Greek says that if the potential is spoken of in relation to activity, and not only to the actual, it would be a power too; the "Principle or Abstract" in MacKenna's brackets is his. |
| II.5.1.p2 | in regard to the process of Actualisation | The Greek pairs "the potential" with "the actual" (τὸ ἐνεργείᾳ) and "power" with "activity" (ἐνέργεια): two pairs of words, not a process and a principle. |
| II.5.2.p1 | a person with a capacity for education becomes in fact educated | The Greek is γραμματικός, one who knows letters, a grammarian: Aristotle's stock example of the learner. |
| II.5.2.p2 | but by virtue of Actuality (the abstract) | The Greek says the power has the activity "from itself" (παρ’ αὐτῆς): it does not act by virtue of an abstract Actuality. |
| II.5.2.p2 | between a temperament and its expression in act | The Greek is ἕξις, a settled state, Aristotle's word for the state a virtue is, not a temperament. |
| II.5.3.p1 | who (with us) assert Matter in the Intellectual Realm | "With us" is MacKenna's; it is true to Plotinus, who set out an intelligible matter in II.4 (chapters 2-5), but the Greek says only "those who set matter there too". |
| II.5.3.p2 | the Form-Idea has mastered each separate constituent | The Greek asks whether it is actual "because each has received its form" (ὅτι τὸ εἶδος ἕκαστον ἀπείληφεν); "mastered" is MacKenna's. |
| II.5.3.p2 | (and not, like material things, merely in image) | MacKenna's words in brackets are his; the Greek says only that the soul of growth "too is actually what it is". |
| II.5.4.p1 | this potentiality of the future mode of being is an existing mode | The Greek says the thing that is potential is already actually something else, and is said to be potentially in relation to another; it says nothing of a "future mode of being". |
| II.5.4.p1 | it is not even a phantasm of Reason | The Greek is ἴνδαλμα λόγου, a phantom of a forming principle: the bodies that are "falsely said to be" are images of forming principles, and matter is not even that. |
| II.5.5.p1 | annex for itself even a visible outline | The Greek says it could not even "be coloured" (χρωσθῆναι) by the things it wished to slip into: a colour, not an outline. |
| II.5.5.p1 | it is remoter (from Real-Being) even than they | The Greek says that, caught by the things that came to be after it, it "settled as the last of these too" (ἔσχατον καὶ τούτων κατέστη): last in the order, not further from real being. |
| II.5.5.p2 | kept as the unchanging substratum | The Greek is ἀνώλεθρον, "indestructible": matter must not perish, which it would if it became actual. |
| II.6.1.p1 | envisage Being as the substance stripped of all else | The Greek says only "being stripped of the others" (τὸ μὲν ὂν ἀπηρημωμένον τῶν ἄλλων). MacKenna's "Reality" in this treatise stands for οὐσία, which the modern version calls substance. |
| II.6.1.p2 | elaborates the Reality into the particular thing | The Greek says the quality is "an addition after the substance of the thing" (μετὰ τὴν οὐσίαν τοῦ πράγματος προσθήκην); it says nothing of making the particular thing. |
| II.6.1.p4 | (not a combination of material phenomena) | The words in brackets are MacKenna's; the Greek says only "fire is a substance". |
| II.6.1.p4 | from the pure Being which is above Reality | MacKenna's gloss in brackets; the Greek asks only how substance there comes "from what is not substance" (ἐξ οὐκ οὐσίας), being and its activities. |
| II.6.1.p4 | though in the truth it is a diminution | The Greek hedges: substance is "perhaps" (τάχα) more lacking than being; MacKenna makes it certain. |
| II.6.2.p1 | body being the Reality, fire is a warmed body | The Greek says "a hot body" (σῶμα θερμόν): heat that is in it, as the snub nose is in you, not heat given to it. |
| II.6.2.p2 | that is the something in which these qualities inhere | The Greek says "this is a qualified thing" (τοῦτο δὲ ποιόν): what is seen and burns is already qualified, not the subject in which the qualities are. |
| II.6.2.p2 | the warming and lighting and other effects of fire | The Greek is τὸ λευκαίνειν, "whitening", not lighting. |
| II.6.2.p2 | by which human beings take the quality | The words in brackets are MacKenna's; the Greek names only "the arts and the aptitudes". |
| II.6.3.p1 | (as do the corresponding Activities here) | The words in brackets are MacKenna's; the Greek sets no activities here beside them. |
| II.6.3.p2 | other than those constituting the substratum of a thing | The Greek says the states and dispositions "of the substrates" (τῶν ὑποκειμένων) are qualities: they are in the substrates, not set against what makes them. |
| II.7.1.p1 | must be whittled away if there is to be mixture without any gap | The Greek says the masses would be cut "into pieces" (εἰς τομάς), divided without end, not whittled. |
| II.7.1.p3 | one substance sinking its original magnitude in the other | The Greek says "if matter too goes through and through, with the size that is first upon it" (μετὰ τοῦ ἐπ’ αὐτῇ πρώτως μεγέθους): the matter goes with its size, rather than sinking it. |
| II.7.2.p1 | without regard to the agreement or disagreement of that theory | The Greek asks "what opinion agrees with what is said, or what other will appear" (τίς δόξα σύμφωνος τοῖς λεγομένοις): the agreement is what Plotinus looks for. |
| II.7.2.p1 | when papyrus-pulp gives up its moisture | The Greek is ἡ βίβλος, papyrus, the reed or a sheet of it; the "pulp" is MacKenna's. |
| II.7.2.p2 | communicate quality and receive matter | The Greek says "one body gives of the quality and takes from the other" (δίδωσι τῆς ποιότητος καὶ λαμβάνει σῶμα θάτερον παρὰ θατέρου): "body" is what gives and takes, not matter received. |
| II.7.2.p3 | how we came to say that body passing through | The Greek says "one might stop and ask how it is said" (πῶς λέγεται): the claim that body cuts body is the opponents', not Plotinus' own. |
| II.7.3.p1 | that other Principle in man | The Greek speaks of "the separable" forming principle, "the one in intellect" (ὁ ἐν νῷ); nothing is said of man. |
| II.8.1.p1 | stripped, so to speak, of magnitude as of all other quality | The Greek goes on: "so that only its forming principle arrives" (ὡς τὸν λόγον αὐτοῦ ἀφικνεῖσθαι μόνον); MacKenna leaves it out. |
| II.8.1.p1 | by observing the salience and recession of its several parts | The Greek says we know a size "by going through it and passing over it" part by part (ἐν διεξόδῳ καὶ ἐπελεύσει): sight travels over the parts; nothing is said of parts standing out or falling back. |
| II.8.1.p2 | exactly as the palate perceives by no indirect knowledge | The Greek of the Workshop ends the comparison with "by accident" (κατὰ συμβεβηκός); the modern version, like MacKenna, takes the sense to be that taste, like hearing, knows the strength directly, and leaves the words out. |
| II.8.1.p3 | It was the detail that prevented a near object deceiving | The Greek says only that "how great the near is does not escape us, for the same reasons" (διὰ τὰ αὐτά): seeing the forms of its parts. |
| II.8.2.p1 | No part of the eye is unoccupied | The Greek speaks of the sight (ἡ ὄψις) that the mountain leaves nothing of, not the eye. |
| II.8.2.p1 | the eye directed to it could not cover so vast an expanse | The Greek says the sight could not "be poured out" (χυθῆναι) so far, "stretching up to it": sight goes out from the eye, as in Plato's Timaeus. |
| III.1.1.p2 | we can make no place here for unwarranted | The Greek says "empty swerves" (παρεγκλίσεσι κεναῖς): the swerve of Epicurus' atoms, named by its own word; MacKenna's "unwarranted slantings" hides whose doctrine is meant. |
| III.1.1.p3 | by manual or intellectual labour | The Greek says "from labours or a craft" (ἐκ πόνων ἢ τέχνης); "intellectual" is MacKenna's. |
| III.1.1.p3 | or, more immediately, a special organic aptitude | The Greek says "a little further off" (ὀλίγῳ προσώτερα), not more immediately, and names "a good flow towards begetting" (εὔροια εἰς παιδοποιίαν), not an aptitude of the organs. |
| III.1.2.p1 | even upon Real Being | The Greek says only "into the things that are" (εἰς τὰ ὄντα), the things of this world; MacKenna's capitals make it the intelligible world. |
| III.1.2.p2 | the leading principle which animal life presupposes | The Greek says "the ruling part in each animal" (τοῦ ἡγεμονοῦντος ἐν ἑκάστῳ τῶν ζῴων), the Stoics' word for the commanding part of the soul; "which animal life presupposes" is MacKenna's. |
| III.1.2.p3 | Of this last opinion we will have something to say | The Greek says "we shall speak about these" (λεχθήσεται δὲ περὶ τούτων), "but now we must go to the first" (ἐπὶ τοὺς πρώτους), "then look at the others in order": the first named, the atoms, are taken first, in chapter 3. |
| III.1.3.p1 | A good deal of truth has resulted | The Greek says "many true arguments have been given about these" (πολλοὶ εἴρηνται λόγοι ἀληθεῖς), that is, against the atoms; MacKenna makes the theory itself yield truth. |
| III.1.3.p1 | admitting a down and an up | The Greek has only "let there be a down" (ἔστω γάρ τι κάτω); the "up", and MacKenna's words in brackets on the universe, are his. |
| III.1.3.p1 | this Universe, when it achieves existence, is all order | The Greek says "what has come to be, when it has come to be, is wholly so" (τὸ δὲ γενόμενον τοῦτο, ὅτε γέγονε, πάντως): it is settled only after the event, which is why nothing can be foretold. Nothing is said of the universe or of order. |
| III.1.3.p2 | lead a third to the philosophic life | The Greek says "another will be wise" (ὁ δὲ σοφὸς ἔσται): wisdom, not a way of life. |
| III.1.4.p2 | dictated by the mind and will | The Greek says the parts move "as the ruling part" moves them (κατὰ τὸ ἡγεμονοῦν), the Stoics' word; nothing is said of mind or will. |
| III.1.4.p2 | the mind itself (and not destiny) is the prime mover | The Greek says "what first moved the leg is that" (ἐκεῖνό ἐστι πρῶτον τὸ κινῆσαν τὸ σκέλος): the ruling part both gives and is the movement, and what receives it is the leg, not the mind, as MacKenna has it. |
| III.1.5.p1 | show a different produce according to their situation | The Greek says the places on earth "differ from one another" (διαφέροντας ἀλλήλων εἶναι) by their relation to the universe and the sun; the "produce" is MacKenna's. |
| III.1.5.p2 | our entire personality | The Greek has "our vices and impulses" (κακίας τε καὶ ὁρμάς); "our entire personality" is MacKenna's. |
| III.1.5.p2 | A further confirmation is found in the efforts we make to correct | The Greek speaks of "the struggles against the blends of the bodies and the desires" (αἱ ... ἐναντιώσεις): resisting them, not correcting them. |
| III.1.5.p3 | a girl's fate includes that of a future husband | The Greek says "from wives what concerns their husbands, and the other way round" (ἐκ γυναικῶν τὰ περὶ τοὺς ἄνδρας): husbands and wives already married; the "future" husband is MacKenna's. |
| III.1.5.p4 | this implies that ugliness and beauty are so caused | The Greek says the astrologers themselves say that likeness, "and beauty and ugliness", come from home (οἴκοθέν φασι καὶ κάλλος καὶ αἶσχος ἰέναι); it is their own word, not an inference. |
| III.1.6.p1 | wreak vengeance, as for a wrong | The Greek says the stars give evils "when afflicted" (κακούμενοι), the astrologers' word for a planet in a bad place; vengeance and wrong are MacKenna's. |
| III.1.6.p2 | on which the augur, acquainted with that alphabet | The Greek says "those who know such a grammar" (τοὺς τὴν τοιαύτην γραμματικὴν εἰδότας), readers of the stars; the augur, who reads birds, comes only in the comparison. |
| III.1.7.p2 | personal action becomes a mere word | The Greek says "what is up to us will be only a name" (ὄνομά τε μόνον τὸ ἐφ̓ ἡμῖν ἔσται): "what is up to us" is the standing term for freedom in Aristotle and the Stoics. |
| III.1.7.p2 | we seek for other explanations of our action | The Greek says "everyone sees this and does not dispute it, but looking for other causes of this impulse, they do not stop" at it as at a principle (τῆς ὁρμῆς ταύτης ἄλλας αἰτίας ζητοῦντες): all men look past impulse for its causes. |
| III.1.8.p1 | we must place at the crest of the world of beings | The Greek says we must "bring in soul into the things that are, as another principle" (ἀρχὴν οὖσαν ἄλλην ἐπεισφέροντας εἰς τὰ ὄντα); the "crest" is MacKenna's. |
| III.1.8.p1 | bodiless and therefore supreme over itself | The Greek says "being without body, it is most master of itself" (ἄνευ μὲν οὖν σώματος οὖσα): while it is without a body, set against when it is brought into one; MacKenna makes it a soul that is bodiless. |
| III.1.8.p2 | largely led by secondary causes | The Greek says "chances" (τύχαι), "all the things around it": what it happens to meet, not secondary causes; MacKenna gives the right word only in brackets. |
| III.1.9.p1 | this compromise between evil and accidental circumstance | The Greek says "choice and chances blended" (προαιρέσει καὶ τύχαις κραθέντα): choice (προαίρεσις), Aristotle's word for deliberate decision, not evil. |
| III.1.10.p1 | Unwisdom, then, is not due to the Soul | The Greek says "the causes of not being wise are other things" (τοῦ μὲν μὴ φρονεῖν ἄλλα αἴτια εἶναι): the things around, which hinder the soul. |
| III.1.10.p1 | when the passions are in abeyance | The Greek says only "insofar as they are let breathe again" (καθ̓ ὅσον ἄν ἀναπνεύσωσι συγχωρηθέντες); the passions are MacKenna's. |
| III.2.1.p2 | we can link the secondary with it | The Greek says: set down the providence of the universe, "and let us join what follows to it" (τὰ ἑξῆς συνάπτωμεν), the next steps of the argument; nothing is said of linking particular providence to it later. |
| III.2.1.p2 | a guidance and partial providence, therefore, such as is indicated | The Greek says that, if the world had begun in time, its providence would be "the same as we said there is in particular things" (οἵαν καὶ ἐπὶ τοῖς κατὰ μέρος), a foresight and reasoning of God; it is a comparison, not a second kind of providence. |
| III.2.1.p2 | a universal consonance with the divine Intelligence | The Greek says providence for the universe is "its being according to intellect" (τὸ κατὰ νοῦν αὐτὸ εἶναι): a way of being, not a consonance, and "divine" is MacKenna's. |
| III.2.1.p3 | shows difference at no point | The Greek says it "has no alteration" (ἀλλοίωσιν οὐκ ἔχει): it does not change. It is not without difference; Plotinus finds difference in the intelligible world (V.1.4). |
| III.2.1.p3 | it does not make over any of its content into any new form | The Greek says "it does not act one thing upon another" (οὐδὲ γὰρ ποιεῖ ἄλλο εἰς ἄλλο): nothing in it works on anything else, which is why it has no alteration. |
| III.2.2.p1 | it must pursue something outside itself for its fulfilment | The Greek says the part "is kept in being by another, and is hostile to that by which it is kept" (σωζόμενον τῷ ἄλλῳ πολέμιόν ἐστιν ὑφ’ οὗ σώζεται): what preserves it is what it fights, as fire lives on what it consumes. Nothing is said of pursuing fulfilment. |
| III.2.2.p2 | the divine Realm imposes the one harmonious act | In the Greek the things themselves, harming and destroying one another, "set up one harmony" (μίαν ... ἁρμονίαν ἐνεστήσατο), while reason makes the harmony over them; "the divine Realm" is MacKenna's. |
| III.2.2.p2 | sometimes of choice and sometimes incidentally | The Greek says "some willingly, some unwillingly" (τὰ μὲν ἑκόντα, τὰ δὲ καὶ ἄκοντα). |
| III.2.2.p3 | the meeting ground of Necessity and divine Reason | The Greek says "intellect and necessity came together" (συνελθόντος νοῦ καὶ ἀνάγκης), the pair of the Timaeus; MacKenna has "divine Reason" for intellect. |
| III.2.2.p3 | Its two extremes are Matter and the Divine Reason | The Greek says the mixture "ends in" (λήγει) matter and reason and "begins from" soul (ὅθεν δὲ ἄρχεται ψυχή). "Divine" is MacKenna's: the reason here is the one that has come down into the world, not the intelligible. |
| III.2.3.p1 | serving at once its own purpose and that of all its parts | The Greek says the whole is "dear (φίλον) to itself and to its parts", the friendship of chapter 2; "purpose" is MacKenna's. |
| III.2.3.p2 | nations of Spirit-Beings and lofty souls | The Greek has "peoples of daemons (δαιμόνων δῆμοι) and good souls (ψυχαὶ ἀγαθαί)": daemons, not spirits, and souls that are good, not lofty. |
| III.2.4.p1 | individual or partial things exist by virtue of Universals | The Greek says "bodies live by their form (κατ’ εἶδος), and as wholes each" (καθ’ ὅλα ἕκαστα): bodies live on in their kinds and as whole masses, though single ones perish. "Universals" are MacKenna's. |
| III.2.4.p2 | since things here are derived | The Greek says "for they did not come to be everlasting" (οὐ γὰρ ἀίδια ἐγένετο): animals must die, and so may be eaten; "derived" is MacKenna's. |
| III.2.4.p4 | ends by establishing a set habit where there was at first only a fall | The Greek says the first slip, overlooked, "made a choice (αἵρεσιν) of what one fell into": the fall becomes a choice, not a habit. |
| III.2.5.p1 | Men are not born divine | The Greek asks "what is terrible if those who have not become divine do not have a divine life?" (μὴ θείους δὲ γενομένους): becoming divine is something done, as in I.2, not a matter of birth. |
| III.2.5.p1 | it acts as a lesson in right doing | The Greek says vice became "a pattern of justice" (παράδειγμα δίκης): in the punishment it brings, an example of justice done, not a lesson in right doing. |
| III.2.5.p1 | to make it the material of unknown forms | The Greek says "for other forms" (εἰς ἑτέρας μορφάς), not unknown ones. |
| III.2.6.p1 | why should what essentially offends our nature fall to the good | The Greek says simply "what is against nature" (τὰ παρὰ φύσιν), and "what is according to nature" for its opposite: the Stoics' names for sickness, poverty and the like, and for health and wealth. MacKenna makes them what "offends our nature" and what it "demands". |
| III.2.6.p2 | we cannot be absolved from the attempt to show in what way the detail of this sphere is just | The Greek says "how each of these things is well" (ὅπῃ ἕκαστα τούτων καλῶς ἔχει), not that it is just. |
| III.2.7.p1 | must begin from this principle of gradation | The Greek says only "setting this down" (ταῦτα τοίνυν ὑποθέμενον), that the mixed must not be judged by the unmixed; "gradation" is MacKenna's. |
| III.2.7.p2 | it is not abstract Spirit | The Greek says souls are "not souls only, but already living things" (οὐ ψυχαὶ μόνον, ἀλλὰ ζῷα ἤδη): souls joined to bodies. "Abstract Spirit" is MacKenna's. |
| III.2.7.p2 | to presuppose it | The Greek says "to bring it into being" (ὑφιστάναι), to make it subsist. |
| III.2.7.p3 | And even granting that those less noble members are not in themselves admirable | The Greek says it is neither holy nor pious "to grant that these things are not well (ἐνδόντας τὸ μὴ καλῶς ταῦτα ἔχειν) and to find fault with the work": the granting is the impiety. MacKenna has Plotinus grant it. |
| III.2.8.p1 | they have not trained themselves in self-defence | The Greek says they "are not good, and have not prepared themselves not to suffer" (οὐκ εἰσὶν ἀγαθοὶ οὐδὲ παρεσκεύασαν αὑτοὺς μὴ παθεῖν): what they lack is goodness, not training in self-defence. |
| III.2.8.p2 | in the disaster to their human quality | The Greek says their first penalty is to be "wolves and ill-starred men" (λύκοις εἶναι καὶ κακοδαίμοσιν ἀνθρώποις): men with an evil daemon, unhappy, whose wickedness is itself their misfortune. |
| III.2.8.p3 | This at once brings us outside the gymnasium with its fun for boys | The Greek says "such things are not a wrestling school, for there it is play" (ἀλλ’ οὐ παλαίστρα τὰ τοιαῦτα: παιδιὰ γὰρ ἐκεῖ): life is not the wrestling school, but war. |
| III.2.8.p3 | Not even a God would have the right to deal a blow for the unwarlike | The Greek says "it was not for a god to fight for the unwarlike himself" (οὐ θεὸν ἔδει ὑπὲρ τῶν ἀπολέμων αὐτὸν μάχεσθαι): what is fitting, not what is a god's right. |
| III.2.8.p4 | Yet death would be better for us | The Greek says death is better "for them" (αὐτοῖς), those who will not do what the gods command; MacKenna takes it to us all. |
| III.2.8.p4 | the triumph of weaklings would not be just | The Greek says only "this is just, and that is not" (τοῦτο γὰρ δίκαιον, καὶ οὐκ ἐκεῖνο): it is just that the cowardly be ruled, and the other way would not be; "the triumph of weaklings" is MacKenna's reading of "the other". |
| III.2.9.p1 | It would not be just, because Providence cannot be a something reducing us to nothingness | The Greek begins "For providence must not be such that we are nothing" (οὐ γὰρ δὴ οὕτως τὴν πρόνοιαν εἶναι δεῖ, ὥστε μηδὲν ἡμᾶς εἶναι); "It would not be just" is MacKenna's link to the last line of chapter 8. |
| III.2.9.p1 | is to annihilate the Universe | The Greek says that providence, if it were all, "would not exist either: for of what would it still be?" (οὐδ’ ἂν εἴη: τίνος γὰρ ἂν ἔτι εἴη;): providence itself would vanish, having nothing to provide for. |
| III.2.9.p2 | their prayers should bring others to sacrifice themselves for their sakes | The Greek says it is not a lawful prayer for the bad "to ask that others be their saviours, having thrown themselves away" (ἑαυτοὺς προεμένους): it is the bad who have given themselves up, and nothing is said of others sacrificing themselves. |
| III.2.9.p2 | nor do they take any steps to improve themselves | The Greek says they never took care that good rulers should come to be "so that they themselves might be cared for" (ὅπως αὐτοὶ εἶεν ἐπιμελούμενοι); nothing is said of improving themselves. |
| III.2.9.p3 | to increase the dominance of moral value | The Greek says "making virtue the stronger" (ἐπικρατεστέραν ἀρετὴν ποιοῦν). |
| III.2.9.p3 | wisdom, intelligence, executive power and right doing | The Greek has "wisdom, intellect, craft (τέχνης) and justice (δικαιοσύνης)": craft, not executive power, and justice, not right doing. |
| III.2.10.p1 | Men are no doubt involuntary sinners in the sense that they do not actually desire to sin | The Greek says they are unwilling "because error is unwilling" (ὅτι ἁμαρτία ἀκούσιον), Socrates' saying that no one errs willingly; nothing is said of desire. |
| III.2.10.p2 | exists only in the sense of a universal relationship | The Greek says the necessity is "not from outside, but in that it holds altogether" (ἀλλ’ ὅτι πάντως): the deed, once done by the doer, is done of necessity; nothing is said of a relationship. |
| III.2.10.p2 | Now Men are Principles, or, at least, they are moved | The Greek says "men too are principles" (ἀρχαὶ δὲ καὶ ἄνθρωποι), without hedging; "at any rate" (γοῦν) gives the proof, that they are moved by their own nature. |
| III.2.11.p1 | it cannot desire all to be good | The Greek says reason does "not wish (οὐ βουλόμενος) all things to be good": it is reason's choice, not something it cannot do. |
