| IV.4.43.p3 | but you should see him naked | Not in the Greek: MacKenna adds, in brackets, the next words of Plato's dialogue, where Socrates says one should see the people stripped. |
| IV.4.44.p1 | that unity is all he perceives | The Greek says "what he contemplates is himself" (τὸ θεωρούμενον αὐτός ἐστι): seer and seen are one, as in III.8.6-8 (E14). |
| IV.4.44.p1 | this is the first condition of the misfortune | The Greek speaks of "the premises of the affection" (αἱ τοῦ πάθους προτάσεις): action reasons from what affection proposes, as from a premise; πάθος here is affection, not misfortune. |
| IV.4.44.p2 | rather than to withdraw from it | MacKenna ends the sentence here and leaves out its last words, "because in this way he was bewitched" (ὅτι οὕτως ἐγοητεύθη): staying in life for its sake is itself nature's spell, though one that the good man sees through. |
| IV.4.44.p3 | cheated by the mere track and trace of the Authentic Good | The Greek says "deceived by the traces of the fine" (τοῖς ἴχνεσι τοῦ καλοῦ), the beautiful; the Good comes after, where the not-good is pursued as good. |
| IV.4.45.p1 | in the orders in which each of the parts is also animate | The Greek is a supposition: "if each of the parts were a living being too" (εἰ ... ζῷον ἦν), it would have a living being's works besides a part's. |
| IV.4.45.p2 | thence onwards with those above the Celestials | The Greek says "to what comes next in the daemonic place, and what is beyond them" (τὰ ἐπέκεινα αὐτῶν): beyond the daemons, the gods; nothing of the Celestials. |
| IV.4.45.p3 | transpose the cords | The Greek says the good man's changes are "as if moved by certain drawings of nature, as by cords" (ὥσπερ ἐκ μηρίνθων ὁλκαῖς τισι φύσεως): other cords, not the same ones transposed. |
| IV.4.45.p3 | a wonder of power and wisdom | The Greek says the All is wonderful "in power and order" (δυνάμεως καὶ τάξεως); "wisdom" is MacKenna's. |
| IV.4.45.p4 | taking this sphere in free election | MacKenna has the soul leave a purer place for this one; the Greek says it "took in exchange for this place another, a pure one" (ἄλλον τόπον καὶ καθαρὸν ἠλλάξατο τοῦτον), which it chose: it goes up from here. |
| IV.5.1.p1 | by means of whatever may bridge the gap | The Greek says "through things made like them" (διὰ τῶν προσομοίων): the organs are made like the sensibles, and it is this likeness, not a bridge, that joins the soul to them; the likeness comes back in IV.5.8. |
| IV.5.1.p1 | a certain degree of identity (which is the basis of knowledge) | The Greek says "a kind of like affection" (ὁμοπαθείας τινός): the organ is affected as the thing is, which is likeness of affection, not identity; the bracket is MacKenna's. |
| IV.5.1.p2 | such an intervening material may be a favouring circumstance | The Greek νύττοι is "might prod" or "touch on" (the text is uncertain), but nothing in it says the body between favours seeing: the same sentence says it contributes nothing. |
| IV.5.1.p2 | such as clay | The Greek says "earthy ones" (τὰ γεηρά), bodies of earth, the densest of the elements. |
| IV.5.1.p3 | anyone facing to the object from the side opposite to ourselves sees it equally | The Greek says "if someone is in front of us (ἔμπροσθέν τις ἡμῶν), looking towards the colour": a man standing between us and the colour, in the very space the affection must cross, sees it too; not one on the far side. The bracket before it is MacKenna's. |
| IV.5.2.p1 | this all the more, since as things are | The Greek says "even if" (εἰ καὶ): granted that the air in front of the eyes is in fact changed now, when there is air there, that does not make it needed. The bracket that follows is MacKenna's. |
| IV.5.2.p2 | there will scarcely be less sensation because of the intervening substance | The Greek asks whether it would be affected less, and answers that it would (ἧττον μὲν ἂν πάθοι), but in measure, "as much as nature wanted" (ὅσον ἐβούλετο ἡ φύσις), the between keeping off the excess. |
| IV.5.2.p2 | where the element introduced is of a kind to overleap the bridge | The Greek says "unless what is given were such that what is between is not affected at all" (ὥστε ὅλως τὸ μεταξὺ μὴ πάσχειν); nothing overleaps. |
| IV.5.2.p4 | in trees the upthrust from the root depends on no such external propulsion | The Greek ξύλα are the logs of a fire: the flame rushes up from the wood, though the wood does not push it (οὐκ ὠθούντων); nothing of trees or roots. |
| IV.5.2.p5 | If it were not for this dark substance there would probably be no reason for the existence of light | The Greek says that if the air were not dark, perhaps "there would have been no need of light" (οὐδ’ ἂν ἐδέησε φωτός) for seeing; not that light would have no reason to exist. |
| IV.5.3.p2 | We may hold one thing certain | The Greek says "one might pause at this" (ἐπιστήσειεν): a doubt raised, not something held certain: perhaps with nothing between we could not see, not because nothing is between, but because sympathy would be taken away. |
| IV.5.3.p3 | would our sphere see that other as from a mutually present distance | The Greek asks whether the sight "on the back of the heaven" would see that other "from a measured distance" (ἐκ διαστήματος συμμέτρου), one suited to seeing; not "our sphere", nor a distance "mutually present". |
| IV.5.3.p4 | the greater laws, the spiritual, of a living being one and self-sensitive | The Greek says "greater necessities" (μείζους ἀνάγκας), "of soul" (ψυχικάς), and of one living being "in sympathy" (συμπαθοῦς): necessities, not laws, and sympathy, not self-sensitiveness. |
| IV.5.4.p2 | as it does in its more inward acts such as understanding — which is what vision really is | The Greek compares the light inside the eye: the soul is in the joining light "as with the light within too" (ὥσπερ καὶ ἐπὶ τοῦ ἔνδον), in the grasping "which is seeing" (ὅπερ ἐστὶν ὁρᾶν); nothing is said of understanding. |
| IV.5.4.p3 | if the intervenient is the sole attractive agent | The Greek says "if simply because there is distance" (εἰ δ’ ὅτι διάστημα ἁπλῶς): the sight travels because of the bare interval, not drawn by anything in it. |
| IV.5.4.p5 | the percipient mind or soul remains within itself | The Greek says only "the soul" (ἡ ψυχή); "mind" is MacKenna's, as often in these chapters. The bracket before it is his too. |
| IV.5.4.p5 | but this process of memory and comparison is excluded by the theory of first knowledge through the agency of a medium | The Greek says "but in fact it is not so" (νῦν δὲ οὐχ οὕτως): we do not in fact first come near what we see, and know it later by memory or reasoning; it is a fact, not a theory, that rules this out. |
| IV.5.5.p2 | until (by its distinctive character) it is sent pulsing outwards | The Greek says air sounds when it stands like a solid "before it is poured away" (πρὶν χυθῆναι), before it scatters; the bracket, and the pulsing, are MacKenna's. |
| IV.5.5.p2 | we are brought to the same conclusion as in the case of sight | The Greek says "let this be left in doubt" (ἠπορήσθω), the question here having become the same as for sight: a question left open, not a conclusion. |
| IV.5.6.p1 | The fact is that primarily light is no appanage of air | The Greek says light is not the air's "first, nor its as air" (οὐκ αὐτοῦ πρώτως οὐδ’ ᾗ αὐτός): air may be lit, but light belongs to it neither first nor by its own nature. |
| IV.5.6.p2 | it is powerful, and may very well pass over unhelped | The Greek says it is "taut" (ἀτενές), stretched straight, and asks why it should not cross "riding on nothing" (οὐκ ἐποχούμενον). |
| IV.5.6.p2 | it would vanish when the object or substance disappeared ; but it does not | The Greek argues the other way: if light were the air's affection, it "would have had to stay" once it had come (ἔδει μένειν), as an affection stays in what has it; "but in fact it goes away" (νῦν δὲ ἄπεισιν), with its source; so it is no affection of the air. |
| IV.5.6.p3 | Perhaps ; and (from this untenable consequence) we may gather that the light never was an appanage of anything | The Greek does not agree: that "the light will not be anything's" (οὐδὲ τὸ φῶς τινος ἔσται) is a further absurdity of the objection, which Plotinus answers: the activity is "from a subject" (ἔκ τινος ὑποκειμένου), the sun's, though not into one. |
| IV.5.7.p1 | but in circuit, with more of it | The Greek's bracketed clause tells what would happen if light did flow (περιέρρει γὰρ ἄν): it would flow round and pour inside, more than was being sent from the source; MacKenna makes it a description of light as it is. |
| IV.5.7.p2 | the other lies next to the surface, the outer image of the inner content | The Greek says the second activity is "after the limit of the body" (μετὰ τὸ πέρας τοῦ σώματος): outside it, going out from it, not lying on its surface. |
| IV.5.7.p3 | it gives merely an incipient on which colour might be formed | The Greek says the activity alone "as it were colours over" (οἷον ἐπιχρώννυσιν), a tint on the surface, not a base on which colour might be formed; and the "other" whose activity it is, is the luminous body, not MacKenna's "(primal light)". |
| IV.5.7.p5 | it is clear that the life-principle can no longer remain | The Greek asks it: "how could life still stay?" (πῶς ἂν ἔτι ζωὴ μένοι;), and goes on to answer that it has not perished either. |
| IV.5.8.p1 | which condition being met, it certainly would be | The Greek says "perhaps they would" (τάχα ἄν). |
| IV.5.8.p1 | exhibiting light and colour | The Greek says "a coloured body, having the other qualities too" (σῶμα κεχρωσμένον καὶ τὰς ἄλλας ποιότητας ἔχον); light is MacKenna's. |
| IV.5.8.p2 | What the conditions would be otherwise, remains to be considered | The Greek says "this must be looked into: whether it is for this reason" (εἰ παρὰ τοῦτο), that is, whether the absurdity comes from our being within one living being. |
| IV.5.8.p3 | thus perception is reduced to a mental awareness | The Greek says "the soul's grasping" (ψυχῆς ἀντίληψις) through organs like the things grasped; "mental" is MacKenna's. |
| IV.5.8.p3 | because the mind itself has related them | The Greek says the soul (αὕτη, ἡ ψυχή) has made them "like" (ὅμοια πεποίηκεν), not "related"; it is the soul's making of likenesses, not the mind's relating. |
| IV.5.8.p4 | it presents mind and no mind | The Greek says "soul and not soul" (ψυχὴν καὶ οὐ ψυχήν). |
| IV.6.1.p2 | it is there that we attack it | The Greek says we "direct our sight" there (τῇ ὄψει προσβάλλομεν), casting it on the thing; there is no attack. |
| IV.6.1.p2 | the mind looks outward | The Greek says "the soul" (τῆς ψυχῆς) looks outward; MacKenna has "mind", or "soul or mind", for soul throughout this treatise. |
| IV.6.1.p4 | we cannot see if the living object is in contact with the eye | The Greek says "the visible thing" (τὸ ὁρατόν) put on the pupil; nothing is said of a living object. |
| IV.6.2.p2 | as they reach the point at which they become matter of its knowledge | The Greek says they come to where "they are of a nature to be seen" (πεφύκασιν ὁρᾶσθαι): the image of reading holds, and the soul sees the letters. |
| IV.6.2.p3 | every concept of this Intellectual order is the soul about its Act | The Greek says the soul "is itself each of them in its activity" (ἔστιν αὐτὴ ἐνεργοῦσα ἕκαστον): it does not merely act about them; it is them. |
| IV.6.3.p1 | its own essential Nature is one of the Beings of the Intellectual Realm | The Greek says "the nature of soul" is the last reason-principle "of the intelligibles and of what is in the intelligible" (ἔσχατος μὲν τῶν νοητῶν καὶ τῶν ἐν τῷ νοητῷ): one statement, of which MacKenna makes a separate clause between dashes. |
| IV.6.3.p1 | its knowledge is not attained by besetting them | The Greek says it knows them "not by their settling in it" (οὐ τῷ ἐνιζάνειν αὐτά): sitting in the soul as imprints, the theory just refuted. |
| IV.6.3.p1 | they are itself in a more radiant mode | The Greek says the soul is them "more dimly" (ἀμυδρότερον), and becomes clearer as it wakes; MacKenna makes them the brighter. |
| IV.6.3.p2 | This is why, it is agreed, children have long memory | The Greek says children "are said to remember better" (μνημονεύειν λέγεται μᾶλλον): more, not longer; the length is of the soul's disposition to what it holds strongly. |
| IV.6.3.p4 | It is no answer to say that the parts present themselves sooner than the entire imprint | The Greek says it is not "because he had parts of the imprint before" (τῷ μέρη ἐσχηκέναι πρότερον τοῦ τύπου): the answer of the imprint theory, that the earlier hearings left part of the stamp, which Plotinus refutes: then those parts should be remembered. |
| IV.6.3.p5 | memory follows upon attention ; those who have memorised much, by dint of their training in the use of leading indications (suggestive words and the like) | The Greek has no "memory follows upon attention"; it says memory comes not only for what we have practised, and that those used to "reports" (ἀπαγγελίαις), to telling things over, recover others easily; MacKenna's "leading indications" and his bracket are not there. |
| IV.6.3.p6 | in no pursuit do technical exercises tend to make a man less the master of his acts and states | The Greek says "nowhere does training for anything make what has trained easily affected" (εὐπαθές): the point is that training makes it less passive, which is the opposite of what imprints would need. |
| IV.6.3.p7 | the dominant idea differs from man to man | The Greek says "different forms prevail in different men" (ἐπικρατοῦσι γὰρ ἄλλαι ἐν ἄλλῳ ἰδέαι): kinds of make or constitution, as a boxer's and a runner's, not ideas. |
| IV.8.1.p1 | Lifted out of the body into myself | The Greek says "waking up" (ἐγειρόμενος) out of the body into myself: the life in the body is the sleep, and the vision is the waking. |
| IV.8.1.p2 | always beginning again | The Greek ἄρχεσθαι is most naturally "to be ruled" (toiling for the same masters), not "to begin": Heraclitus' fragment speaks of the weariness of serving. |
| IV.8.1.p2 | in slavery to a raving discord | The Greek says "trusting in raging strife" (πίσυνος μαινομένῳ νείκει): Empedocles put his faith in Strife; there is no slavery. |
| IV.8.2.p1 | though in all cases appropriately placed within the universe | The Greek says each particular body, left to itself, would scatter and be carried to its own place, while "in the All everything lies by nature in its own place": a contrast between the two, not a concession about the particular body. |
| IV.8.2.p3 | in the second kind of care the agent absorbs much of the nature of its object | The Greek says the doer is "filled" (ἀναπιμπλᾶσα), even tainted, with the nature of what it does, by contact with it: not much of it, but its nature. |
| IV.8.2.p4 | bringing about a veritable descent | The Greek says no business "makes it incline downward" (νεῦσιν ποιοῦσα κάτω): an inclination, not a descent. |
| IV.8.3.p3 | remaining, therefore, an Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says the opposite: having taken on something else, "it did not remain intellect" (νοῦς οὐκ ἔμεινεν). |
| IV.8.4.p1 | the appetite for the divine Intel lect urges them to return to their source | The Greek says they "use an intellectual longing" (ὀρέξει νοερᾷ) in turning back to their source: the longing is intellectual, not a longing for intellect. |
| IV.8.4.p2 | by an actual presence, it administers the particular | The Greek says it governs the particular "with difficulty" (μετὰ περιστάσεως), amid troubles; its presence comes after, "present to it, and sunk into it". |
| IV.8.4.p3 | the soul has lost that innocency of conducting the higher | The Greek says it has missed "the freedom from harm" (τοῦ ἀβλαβοῦς) that it had in governing the better: harmlessness, not innocence. |
| IV.8.4.p3 | when only it makes its memories the starting point of a new vision | The Greek says "recollection" (ἀναμνήσεως), Plato's recollection of the forms, not the soul's memories. |
| IV.8.4.p4 | have place in both spheres | The Greek says they become "amphibians, as it were" (οἷον ἀμφίβιοι), living in two elements, like a frog in water and on land. |
| IV.8.5.p1 | a voluntary descent aiming at the completion of the universe | The Greek speaks only of "the descent for the completion of the All" (ἡ εἰς τελείωσιν κάθοδος τοῦ παντός); that it is voluntary is MacKenna's. |
| IV.8.5.p2 | determined by an external law of nature | The Greek says "an everlasting law" (ἀιδίῳ νόμῳ), not an external one. |
| IV.8.5.p3 | its audacity, its Tolma | Not in the Greek of this chapter, which says only "the cause of its coming down"; the soul's "audacity" (τόλμα) is in V.1.1, and MacKenna brings it here. |
| IV.8.5.p4 | coming to understand what sin is | The Greek says "the nature of wickedness" (φύσιν κακίας), vice, not sin. |
| IV.8.6.p1 | native to soul as it exists in the Supreme | The Greek says the power is "as great as was in that one" (ὅση ἦν ἐν ἐκείνῳ), in the prior; nothing is said here of soul in the Supreme. |
| IV.8.7.p1 | the greatness of rest in the Supreme | The Greek says it learns "what it is to be there" (οἷον ἄρα ἐστὶν ἐκεῖ εἶναι); "rest" is MacKenna's. |
| IV.8.7.p2 | Where the faculty is incapable of knowing without contact | The Greek says "too weak to know evil by knowledge before experience" (ἐπιστήμῃ πρὸ πείρας). |
| IV.8.7.p3 | has the property of knowing its lower by inspection | The Greek says it grasps what is below it "by contemplation" (θεωρίᾳ), the word for the soul's beholding of intellect. |
| IV.8.8.p1 | or by the individual choice or by both at once | The Greek says "by the power of discursive thought" (διανοητικῇ): the reasoning power, not choice. |
| IV.8.8.p2 | as in the execution of an artistic conception | The Greek says "as art does not deliberate" (ὡς ἡ τέχνη οὐ βουλεύεται): the point is that it acts without deliberating. |
| IV.8.8.p3 | that pleasure which is its lure | The Greek says "pleasure deceives it" (ἡδονὴ ἠπάτησε): a deception, not only a lure. |
| IV.9.1.p1 | That the Soul of every individual is one thing we deduce from the fact | The Greek is one long question ("Is it the case that, as we say..."), which MacKenna turns into statements. |
| IV.9.1.p2 | and yours, and mine, belongs to it | The Greek says ours "come from" (ἐκ) the soul of the All. |
| IV.9.2.p1 | the distress is felt not in the other but in the hand as represented in the centralising unity | The Greek says the other hand does not perceive it, "but the soul in the whole" does (ἀλλ’ ἡ ἐν τῷ ὅλῳ); MacKenna's "centralising unity" stands for this. |
| IV.9.2.p2 | some transmission of the experience may be expected | The Greek says that "to be affected together" (συμπάσχειν), sympathy, is not absurd; Plotinus' word for the bond of the All. |
| IV.9.2.p3 | an identical thing may be active here and inactive there | The Greek says "moving in one and standing in another" (κινεῖσθαι ... ἑστάναι), as with the man in me and in you above. |
| IV.9.3.p1 | and all this can be due only to some unity among us | The Greek says "perhaps that is why there is love" (μήποτε γὰρ τὸ φιλεῖν διὰ τοῦτο): feeling with one another may be the cause of love. |
| IV.9.3.p3 | which itself has sensation passively | The Greek says it is the part that is nourished (ὃ καὶ παθητικῶς αἰσθητικόν), the body, that perceives passively, not the All. |
| IV.9.4.p2 | Let us suppose, even, the first soul to be corporeal | The Greek says "if it were a body" (εἰ μὲν οὖν σῶμα εἴη): a hypothesis to be refuted, which MacKenna turns into a supposition to grant. |
| IV.9.4.p4 | the soul is a unit broken up into a variety of points | The Greek says it "would be spent" or used up (ἀνηλίσκετο) into many marks: consumed by being divided, as a body is. |
| IV.9.5.p1 | includes the one-many in virtue of giving itself | The Greek says the many go back "to it" (εἰς ταύτην) as to one that has given itself to multitude and not given itself; there is no "one-many" that it includes. |
| IV.9.5.p3 | its value consists in its approach to the whole | The Greek says it "is empowered" (ἐνδυναμοῦται) by coming near the whole: it gains power, not value. |
| IV.9.5.p4 | There all stands out clear and separate | The Greek says "all things are clear, and each" (φανὰ πάντα καὶ ἕκαστον); "separate" is MacKenna's. |
| V.4.1.p2 | it will debar all telling and knowing except that it may be described as | The Greek says that it is false even to say of it that it is one (καθ’ οὗ ψεῦδος καὶ τὸ ἓν εἶναι), and that there is no account or knowledge of it; "beyond being" is what is said of it, not an exception to it, as MacKenna's "except" makes it. |
| V.4.1.p2 | nothing authentically one, there would be no Source | The Greek says that the first, if it were not simple and really one, would not be a principle: a step about the first, not about whether such a thing exists. |
| V.4.1.p4 | tending to eternity and to service | The Greek says "towards everlastingness and goodness" (εἰς ἀιδιότητά τε καὶ ἀγαθότητα): things imitate the first by lasting and by being good, not by "service". |
| V.4.1.p4 | the Power towards all | The Greek ἡ πάντων δύναμις is "the power of all things", as in V.4.2 (δύναμις πάντων): the power that is all things before they are, not a power directed towards them. |
| V.4.2.p1 | for the dyad is the Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says "for this is intellect" (τοῦτο γὰρ ὁ νοῦς): the whole that comes from the dyad and the one, the forms and numbers, not the dyad alone. |
| V.4.2.p1 | and it possesses besides another object of intellection in the Order following upon itself | The Greek says there is another intelligible "after which" intellect comes (τὸ μετ’ αὐτὸ νοητόν): the first, which the next sentence calls "this intelligible", from which intellect comes. MacKenna puts the other intelligible below intellect. |
| V.4.2.p2 | and especially within a being which is the sum of being | The Greek says that something comes to be from the first "when it is most of all what it is" (ὅταν ἐκεῖνο μάλιστα ᾖ ὃ ἔστι), while it stays in itself; it does not call the first "the sum of being", which it is beyond, nor say that the thing comes to be within it. |
| V.4.2.p3 | That transcendent was the potentiality of the All | The Greek says "power of all things" (δύναμις πάντων): the power that makes all things, not a "potentiality" in Aristotle's sense, a matter waiting to be made actual, which Plotinus denies of the first. |
| V.4.2.p4 | it exists here together with the things of the universe | The Greek says "here", in intellect's case, it is with the things (ἐνταῦθα μετὰ τῶν πραγμάτων): with the things it knows, not with "the things of the universe". |
| V.4.2.p4 | and the collective knowledge (in the divine mind) of the immaterial is the universe of things | The Greek says that all knowledge of things without matter is the things (ἡ ἐπιστήμη δὲ ὅλη τῶν ἄνευ ὕλης τὰ πράγματα): the knowledge is the same as what it knows, not "the universe of things". |
| V.5.1.p3 | the intellections would be impressions, that is to say not native act but violence from without | The Greek says the thoughts would be imprints "brought in from outside, and blows" (ἐπακτοὶ καὶ πληγαί), the words of the theories of perception Plotinus is turning against intellect; "not native act" and "violence" are MacKenna's gloss. |
| V.5.1.p5 | They cannot be premises, axioms or predicates | The Greek λεκτά are the Stoics' "sayables", what a sentence says, not "predicates"; and Plotinus' reason is that they would say something about other things, not be the beings themselves. |
| V.5.1.p5 | the work of some unknown sculptor or graver | The Greek says "by some sculptor or painter" (ὑπό τινος πλάστου ἢ γραφέως); "unknown" is MacKenna's, and a painter is not a graver. |
| V.5.1.p6 | belief is our lief | The Greek gives a reason, not a pun: opinion is "receptive" (παραδεχομένη), and receives one thing while the thing it comes from is another. |
| V.5.2.p3 | Thus veritable truth is not accordance with an external ; it is selfaccordance | The Greek says it "agrees not with another, but with itself" (οὐ συμφωνοῦσα ἄλλῳ, ἀλλ’ ἑαυτῇ); a fair sense, but MacKenna's "it affirms and is nothing other than itself and is nothing other" doubles the Greek, which says it "says nothing besides itself, and is, and says what it is". |
| V.5.3.p1 | but the Godhead entire | The Greek says it is "not some god, but thinks fit to be all these" (οὐ τίς, ἀλλὰ πᾶς ἀξιοῖ ταῦτα εἶναι): all the beings, the truth, intellect, not "the Godhead". |
| V.5.3.p1 | it will be heralded by some ineffable beauty | The Greek says an immense beauty (κάλλος ἀμήχανον) must "go before him"; "ineffable" belongs to the One, not to the beauty of intellect that goes before it. |
| V.5.3.p2 | holding sway by all reason over a dense offspring his own | The Greek says he is "by nature lord" (κατὰ φύσιν κύριος) of his own offspring gathered together; "by all reason" and "dense" are not in it. |
| V.5.3.p2 | [Interpolation : —Zeus | MacKenna sets the sentence on Zeus apart as an interpolation; Volkmann's Greek, the Workshop's, prints it as part of Plotinus' sentence, and the modern version keeps it there. |
| V.5.4.p2 | for if we fail of the centre | The Greek says only "if not, you have two" (εἰ δὲ μή, ἔσχες δύο); the centre is MacKenna's image. |
| V.5.4.p3 | essential number first appears as providing duration to the divine Intellection | The Greek phrase (ὁ τῷ νοεῖν εἶναι ἀεὶ παρέχων) is hard, and MacKenna's "first appears" and "divine" are his; it says that substantial number always gives being to thinking, or to the intelligibles in intellect's thinking. |
| V.5.4.p4 | the dyad, as a thing of extension, is not strictly a unit | The Greek compares the dyad with the continuous (πρὸς τὸ συνεχές) and says it is not one as the continuous is; it does not make the dyad a thing of extension. |
| V.5.5.p3 | sounds which labour to express the essential nature of the universe | The Greek says the sounds signify "the existence of what was begotten" (τὴν ὑπόστασιν τοῦ γεννηθέντος), being, not "the universe". |
| V.5.5.p3 | that Being (the weaker) is that which proceeds from (the stronger), The One | "The weaker" and "the stronger" are MacKenna's; the Greek's point is the breathing: on, pressed hard with the rough breathing, sounds hen, so that being shows it came from the one. |
| V.5.6.p2 | but never seeking to define it | The Greek says one learns "what it is like" (οἷον δὲ ἔστι) by letting the intelligible go: MacKenna makes this a refusal to define it, where the Greek gives the way to see it. |
| V.5.6.p3 | we are talking of the untenable | The Greek says "we speak of what cannot be spoken" (λέγομεν περὶ οὐ ῥητοῦ): what is beyond speech, not what cannot be held. |
| V.5.6.p4 | there would be more truth in silence | The Greek says it "would be less clear than if one gave it no name" (ἀσαφέστερον ... τοῦ εἰ μή τις ὄνομα ἔλεγεν αὐτοῦ): a comparison of clearness, not of truth. |
| V.5.7.p2 | declining towards the lower Nature, that upon which the light from above rests | The Greek says intellect inclines "to the nature of the things lit up" (πρὸς τὴν τῶν καταλαμπομένων φύσιν): the intelligibles it sees, not a "lower Nature". |
| V.5.8.p1 | It was here. Yet no | The Greek says "so it was within, and again not within" (ἔνδον ἄρα ἦν καὶ οὐκ ἔνδον αὖ): the question was whether the light came from outside or within; MacKenna's "here" and "beyond" lose it. |
| V.5.8.p2 | gleaming in the light of that presence | The Greek says intellect "saw itself" become more beautiful and shining (εἶδε ... καλλίω γενόμενον ἑαυτὸν καὶ ἐπιστίλβοντα); MacKenna drops that it sees itself. |
| V.5.8.p3 | (by its divinest power) | The Greek says only that intellect sees "by what of it is not intellect" (τῷ ἑαυτοῦ μὴ νῷ); MacKenna's bracket names it, and "(having a sphere of its own)" before it is his too. |
| V.5.9.p1 | otherwise it would not be contained at all | The Greek says that what needed another to come to be "needs another everywhere" (ἄλλου δεῖται πανταχοῦ), and so is in another; MacKenna's "otherwise" is not in it. |
| V.5.9.p2 | but so as not to be pointed off to hold them part for part | The Greek says it "was not scattered into them" (οὔτ’ ἐσκεδάσθη εἰς αὐτά): it holds them without being dispersed among them. |
| V.5.9.p3 | God is sovranly present through all | The Greek says "it will be everywhere by itself" (πανταχοῦ ἔσται ἐφ’ ἑαυτοῦ); "God", here and in the next sentence, is MacKenna's, as is "sovranly". |
| V.5.9.p4 | its linked members form and occupy the whole | The Greek says the cosmos' parts "hang from it and are in it" (ἀνηρτημένα εἰς αὐτὸν καὶ ἐν ἐκείνῳ): they depend on the whole. |
| V.5.10.p2 | for that, you must yourself be the divine Intellect in Act | The Greek says the opposite of this: "else", if you try to report the whole, "you will be an intellect thinking" (νοῦς νοῶν ἔσῃ), and it will escape you. Thinking it is what loses it. |
| V.5.10.p2 | In ordinary vision you may think to see the object entire | The Greek is a command: "when you see, look at the whole" (ὅταν μὲν ὁρᾷς, ὅλον βλέπε), of the seeing of the One, not ordinary vision. |
| V.5.10.p3 | Yet its Being is not limited | The Greek says "he is not limited" (οὐδὲ πεπερασμένος εἶναι); "its Being" gives being to what the treatise has just put beyond being. |
| V.5.11.p2 | the thing of extension least real of all | The Greek quotes a saying, "and the great is less" (τὸ δὲ μέγα ἧττον ἐστί), of what is large in bulk: MacKenna turns it into his own words. |
| V.5.12.p2 | This love of Beauty then is later than the love of Good and comes with a more sophisticated understanding | The Greek says this love is "second" and of those "who already understand more" (ἤδη συνιέντων μᾶλλον); "sophisticated" is MacKenna's. |
| V.5.12.p3 | they struggle venomously for beauty as something secondary like themselves | The Greek says they "rival and quarrel with" beauty (φιλονεικοῦσι καὶ ἐρίζουσι τῷ καλῷ), as a rival that they think has come to be as they have, not that they fight "for" it; "venomously" is MacKenna's. |
| V.5.12.p4 | Beauty is all violence and stupefaction | The Greek says beauty has "amazement and shock" (θάμβος ... καὶ ἔκπληξιν); "violence" and "all" are MacKenna's. |
| V.5.12.p5 | as some attraction will lure the child from the father's side | The Greek says "as the beloved" (τὸ ἐρώμενον) draws one from the father: love of another draws the son from his father's house; MacKenna's "some attraction" and "the child" lose the beloved. |
| V.5.12.p6 | exactly as He would not have grudged existence to any other universe | The Greek says "if it had been possible for something else (ἄλλῳ) to come to be from it": not "any other universe". |
| V.5.13.p2 | existence or intellect or goodness | The Greek says "substance or intellect or beauty" (οὐσίαν ἢ νοῦν ἢ καλόν): Plotinus' point is that even beauty must not be added to the Good, as V.5.12 put beauty second. |
| V.5.13.p2 | philosophy must guard against attaching to the Supreme what is later and lower | The Greek says "we too must add nothing" (καὶ ἡμεῖς μηδὲν ... προστιθῶμεν); "philosophy" is MacKenna's. |
| V.5.13.p3 | the primarily good must be that principle in which the better element has more effectively participated | The Greek says that what is first good is "that by sharing in which", beside the common element, the compound has become good: the compound shares in the first, the first does not share. |
| V.5.13.p4 | Thus is revealed to us the Primarily existent | The Greek says "the first, the Good" (τὸ ἄρα πρώτως καὶ τἀγαθόν) has appeared "above all beings": not "the Primarily existent". |
| V.7.1.p4 | The entire soul-period conveys with it all the requisite Reason-Principles | The Greek says "the whole period" (ἡ πᾶσα περίοδος), the world's whole cycle, holds all the principles; "soul-period" is MacKenna's. |
| V.7.1.p4 | it is an infinitude having nothing to do with number or part | The Greek says that all the infinity is "in what has no parts" (ἐν ἀμερεῖ), in intellect, which holds it undivided; it says nothing of number. |
| V.7.2.p1 | one of the parents —the male let us say — is the source | The Greek says that each of the two (ἑκάτερος) begetting, the male for instance, would make by one principle only; it does not make one parent the only source. |
| V.7.2.p3 | Difference everywhere is a good, and so there must be differing archetypes | The Greek says that what differs is "beautiful in many places" (πολλαχοῦ καλόν), and so the form is not one: an observation, which MacKenna makes a rule ("everywhere a good"). |
| V.7.2.p3 | though only to evil could we attribute any power in Matter to thwart nature | The Greek says "ugliness" (αἶσχος): only where a thing is ugly is the matter to be blamed for mastering the principles; it does not speak of evil. |
| V.7.3.p2 | A craftsman even in constructing an object identical with a model | The Greek says the craftsman makes things that do not differ from each other (ἀδιάφορα), two alike; there is no model in it. |
| V.7.3.p3 | If production is undetermined in regard to Quantity, each thing has its distinct Reason-Principle | The Greek says that if the making leaves the number to chance (τὸ εἰκῆ τοῦ ὁποσαοῦν), "that is another account" (ἄλλος λόγος): another explanation would be needed. It does not say that each thing then has its own principle. |
| V.7.3.p4 | the Beings there are unfailing | The Greek ends with the principles "ready to hand there" (τῶν ἐκεῖ προχείρων), the word V.7.2 uses of the principles a parent has ready at different times; "unfailing" is not in it. |
| V.8.1.p1 | will be able also to come to understand the Father and Transcendent of that Divine Being | The Greek says he will be able "to bring into mind" (εἰς ἔννοιαν βαλέσθαι) the father, "who is beyond intellect" (τὸν ἐπέκεινα νοῦ): to form some notion of him, not to understand him, since he is beyond what intellect grasps. |
| V.8.1.p2 | Let us go to the realm of magnitudes | The Greek has only "two stones in bulk" (δύο λίθων ἐν ὄγκῳ), two blocks of stone; MacKenna makes the phrase a heading of its own. |
| V.8.1.p2 | the other has been minutely wrought by the craftsman's hands | The Greek says the stone has been "mastered by art" (τέχνῃ κεκρατημένου); the craftsman's hands are what the next sentences say the form does not belong to. |
| V.8.1.p3 | and so the art exhibited in the material work derives from an art yet higher | The Greek speaks still of music: the music in the perceptible (τὴν ἐν αἰσθητῷ) is made by the music before it; MacKenna widens it to all art. |
| V.8.2.p1 | as it were the mere Matter of beauty | The Greek says that the shape of blood and menses is "like what holds something simple, as matter does" (οἷα ὕλη); "of beauty" is MacKenna's. |
| V.8.2.p3 | but by an elaboration upon the presented form | The Greek says the size comes in "having become large in form" (εἴδει γενόμενον μέγα): the size itself becomes form; there is no "elaboration" upon it. |
| V.8.2.p5 | catching only at the inner comeliness, the truly personal | The Greek says "his inner beauty" (τὸ εἴσω κάλλος αὐτοῦ); "the truly personal" is MacKenna's. |
| V.8.2.p5 | you, if you are conscious of beauty within, remember | The Greek says "if you too have seen yourself beautiful, recollect" (εἰ δὲ καὶ σὺ εἶδες σαυτὸν καλόν, ἀναμνήσθητι): a beauty once seen, to be recollected, as Plato's souls recollect the forms. |
| V.8.3.p1 | In the proficient soul this is brighter | The Greek says "a good soul" (σπουδαία ψυχή), the word of Aristotle and the Stoics for the good man; "proficient" makes it a soul's skill. |
| V.8.3.p3 | Intellect operating within them (the divine sun and stars) to visibility | The Greek speaks of all the gods, and says intellect is more active in them "so that it is seen" (ὥστε ὁρᾶσθαι); MacKenna's parenthesis makes them the heavenly bodies, though the next sentence tells apart the gods who have bodies from those who do not. |
| V.8.3.p4 | see and know in virtue of their omnipresence to it | The Greek says only that they dwell in all of that heaven (ἐν παντὶ οἰκοῦντες τῷ ἐκεῖ οὐρανῷ); "see and know" is MacKenna's. |
| V.8.4.p1 | every being is lucid to every other, in breadth and depth | The Greek says each is clear to each "to the inside" (εἰς τὸ εἴσω), seen through and through; "in breadth and depth" is MacKenna's. |
| V.8.4.p2 | though of course this is really produced by the stars | The Greek has only the image: as if one were to think of the visible heaven, which is like light, as the light that grows from it, so that ground and what stands on it are one; the correction about the stars is MacKenna's own. |
| V.8.4.p4 | and of course all There are unchangeable | The Greek says the things there are "unwearying" (ἄτρυτα), they do not wear out; "unchangeable" says something else. |
| V.8.4.p6 | if we boast ourselves worthy of the discussion | The Greek says "if we say we are worthy of the name" (εἴπερ ἄξιοι τῆς προσηγορίας φαμὲν εἶναι), of the name of philosophers who seek; "the discussion" is MacKenna's. |
| V.8.5.p1 | to that wisdom in Nature which is embodied in himself | The Greek says the craftsman goes back to the wisdom of nature "by which he has come to be" (καθ’ ἣν γεγένηται): the wisdom that made him, not one he embodies. |
| V.8.5.p2 | but if (as we assume) we are referred to the Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says "if they come to intellect" (εἰ δὲ εἰς νοῦν ἥξουσιν): a step of those who answer, not "as we assume". |
| V.8.5.p4 | need look to our apparatus of science | The Greek says the gods there do not look at "propositions" (ἀξιώματα), statements such as our sciences set out one after another; MacKenna widens it. |
| V.8.6.p1 | not an aggregate of discursive reasoning and detailed willing | The Greek says each image is "not discursive thought or deliberation" (οὐ διανόησις οὐδὲ βούλευσις); deliberation is the weighing of what to do, not "willing", and there is no "aggregate". |
| V.8.6.p2 | This excellence, whose necessity is scarcely or not at all manifest to search, exists, if we could but find it out | In the Greek the condition "if one were to find it" (εἴπερ τις ἐξεύροι) belongs to the search: what search would find with difficulty, if at all, is there before search; MacKenna puts the condition on its existing. |
| V.8.7.p1 | how could the plan for a universe come to one that had never looked outward | The Greek asks where the plan would come from to one "who had never yet seen" (οὐπώποτε ἑωρακότι) anything; "looked outward" is MacKenna's. |
| V.8.7.p2 | since there is no obstacle, all being continuous within the realm of reality | The Greek says that nothing is between (οὐδενὸς μεταξὺ ὄντος) and speaks of nearness (γειτονεία); "no obstacle" and "all being continuous" are MacKenna's. |
| V.8.7.p4 | as man now stands, he does not produce in his work a true image of himself | The Greek says the human being does make a form (δημιουργεῖ εἶδος), but one other than what he has become; MacKenna turns the sentence into a denial. |
| V.8.7.p5 | we can say only that because the All is what it is, therefore there is a total of good | The Greek says that because it is as it is, "these things" (ταῦτα), the things here, are well; MacKenna's "total of good" is not in it. |
| V.8.8.p1 | Certainly it cannot be anything (be, for example, Beauty) without being wholly that thing | The Greek says that what falls short of beauty is what has only a part of it, or none; MacKenna's parenthesis makes a general rule about being anything wholly. |
| V.8.8.p2 | the Beauty sprung from this world is, itself, a copy from That | The Greek says that what came to be from the pattern is beautiful, and is itself an image of it (καὶ αὐτὸ ὡς εἰκόνα ἐκείνου): the world is the image; there is no "Beauty sprung from this world". |
| V.8.9.p1 | cast out your inborn sense of Matter | The Greek says "take away the picture of matter in you" (τὸ τῆς ὕλης ἐν σοὶ φάντασμα): the image of matter in your imagining, not an inborn sense. |
| V.8.9.p2 | It is distinction by state without interval | The Greek says each is apart "in a rest without distance" (ἐν στάσει ἀδιαστάτῳ): they stand still, apart without being spread out; "distinction by state" takes rest (στάσις) for a state. |
| V.8.9.p3 | it is through their failure in the true power | The Greek ἀπειρίᾳ δυνάμεως ἀληθινῆς is most naturally "through inexperience of true power": we picture their powers as great because we do not know the true one; MacKenna makes it the bodies' own failure. |
| V.8.9.p4 | The very figment of Being needs some imposed image of Beauty to make it passable | The Greek says the false substance needs a brought-in image of beauty "so that it may look beautiful" (ἵνα καὶ καλὸν φαίνηται) and be at all; "passable" weakens it. |
| V.8.10.p1 | followed by gods and demigods | The Greek says "daemons" (δαίμονες), as Plato's Phaedrus (246e) has it; "demigods" are heroes, another order. |
| V.8.10.p2 | another is filled with the sight of Moral Wisdom | The Greek has σωφροσύνη, self-control, which Plato's Phaedrus (247d) names beside justice; "Moral Wisdom" hides it. |
| V.8.10.p4 | or rather all There is light and beauty, through and through | The Greek says "all is colour and beauty from the depth" (πᾶν χρόα καὶ κάλλος ἐκ βάθους); "light" is MacKenna's, in place of colour. |
| V.8.10.p5 | it is but finding the strength to see divinity within | The Greek puts it as a condition, "if he had the power to see the god in himself" (εἰ δύναμιν ἔχοι ἐν αὑτῷ θεὸν βλέπειν); MacKenna's "it is but" makes it easy. |
| V.8.11.p1 | at once he forms a multiple unity with the God silently present | The Greek says he is "one, all together" (ἓν ὁμοῦ πάντα) with the god; "multiple unity" is MacKenna's. |
| V.8.11.p1 | still he is pure and remains very near to the God | The Greek makes it a condition: "being pure" (καθαρὸς μὲν ὤν), he stays next to the god; MacKenna's "still he is pure" says it of whoever turns back. |
| V.8.11.p2 | he puts sense away behind him in dread of the separated life | The Greek says he leaves perception behind "for fear of being other" (τοῦ ἕτερος εἶναι φόβῳ), of being other than what he sees; "the separated life" is MacKenna's. |
| V.8.11.p3 | be no longer the seer, but, as that place has made him, the seen | The Greek says he becomes the sight "of another who contemplates" (ἑτέρου θεωμένου), shining with the thoughts that come from there; MacKenna leaves out the other. |
| V.8.11.p4 | Still, we will be told, one cannot be in beauty and yet fail to see it | The Greek asks a question, "how will one be in beauty without seeing it?" (πῶς οὖν ἔσται τις ἐν καλῷ μὴ ὁρῶν αὐτό;), which Plotinus answers by saying that seeing it as other is not yet being in it; MacKenna makes it an objection put to him. |
| V.8.11.p5 | we are most completely aware of ourselves when we are most completely identified with the object of our knowledge | The Greek says we understand ourselves best when we have made "our knowledge and ourselves" one (τὴν ἐπιστήμην ἡμῶν καὶ ἡμᾶς ἓν πεποιηκότες): knowledge and knower, not knower and object. |
| V.8.11.p6 | by intellection, we are aware of none | The Greek says "we seem not to know" (ἀγνοεῖν δοκοῦμεν): a seeming, because perception has nothing to report, not an awareness of nothing. |
| V.8.12.p1 | God engendering a universe within himself in a painless labour | The Greek says he begot "all things" (πάντα) in himself; "a universe" is MacKenna's. |
| V.8.12.p2 | the Supreme has existed for ever and for ever will exist | The Greek's "it was always and will be" (ἦν δ’ ἀεὶ καὶ ἔσται) may go on with "these" (ταῦτα), the things here, whose plural takes a singular verb in Greek: the world always was and will be. MacKenna makes it the Supreme. |
| V.8.13.p1 | The God fettered (as in the Kronos Myth) to an unchanging identity | MacKenna's chapter 12 runs on here without a break: the Greek's chapter 13 begins with these words (the OCR lost his number 13). |
| V.8.13.p1 | by that which keeps him apart from the link between himself and the lower | The Greek says he is held up, away from what comes after him, by "the bond" (τῷ ἀνέχοντι ... δεσμοῦ): the bond itself holds him apart; MacKenna keeps him apart from the link. |
| V.8.13.p2 | Soul also has beauty, but is less beautiful than Intellect as being its image | The Greek says soul is "a trace" (ἴχνος) of intellect, a weaker word than image. |
| VI.1.1.p1 | Philosophy at a very early stage investigated | The Greek says that "the very ancient" thinkers (οἱ πάνυ παλαιοί) inquired into it; the next sentences turn to the later ones who examined them. |
| VI.1.1.p2 | others less, others no doubt more | The Greek only allows that "there might be some" (εἶεν δ’ ἄν τινες) who said more; it names no one. |
| VI.1.1.p4 | the Supreme they overlooked | The Greek says they "have left out the things that most are" (τὰ μάλιστα ὄντα), in the plural: the intelligibles, not the One alone. |
| VI.1.2.p1 | But are we really obliged to posit the existence of such genera | The Greek begins "again, then" (πάλιν οὖν): the question goes back to the start, whether there are kinds at all. |
| VI.1.4.p1 | number together with all continuous magnitude, space and time | The Greek has "place" (τόπον), Aristotle's word for a quantity, and the word the next chapters keep. |
| VI.1.4.p2 | they become known as quantities only when they are rated by number—two yards, three yards | The Greek has "two cubits, three cubits" (δίπηχυ ἢ τρίπηχυ), Aristotle's own example of quantity (Categories 4, 1b28-29); the cubit is about a foot and a half. |
| VI.1.4.p4 | yet as standing outside the objects they have no measuring power, as do rulers and scales | The Greek finds it strange that such numbers measure "by being in" the things (ἐνυπάρχουσιν) "and not by being outside, as rulers and measures measure"; Page turns the comparison round. |
| VI.1.4.p5 | the objects will be quantities only to the extent of participating in Quantity | The Greek says the things measured will not be quantities at all (οὔτε ἐκεῖνα ποσά), "since they do not share in quantity". |
| VI.1.4.p5 | they find their place under the influence of verbal suggestion | The Greek says only that "they will be what they are called" (τοῦτο, ὃ λέγονται, ἔσονται), quantities; there is no "verbal suggestion". |
| VI.1.4.p6 | the mind measures by availing itself of the total figure | The Greek says "the soul" (ἡ ψυχή) "measures the multitude" (τὸ πλῆθος) by using number; "the total figure" is Page's. |
| VI.1.5.p1 | we may define speech as an impact (made upon the outer air by the breath) | The Greek says only that "the blow is rather speech" (ἡ δὲ πληγὴ μᾶλλον λόγος); the breath and the outer air are Page's. |
| VI.1.6.p1 | Has it, perhaps, actuality in some cases only, as for instance in what is termed | The Greek asks whether the relation is real "in those named second" (ἐπὶ τοῦ ὕστερον λεχθέντος), double and half, but "in those named first" (right and left) is nothing; Page reads the two as the relatives "posterior" and "prior". |
| VI.1.6.p3 | this is superior to that | The Greek says "this one rules that one" (οὗτος κρατεῖ τούτου), the relation of master and slave in the list above. |
| VI.1.7.p1 | but are victims of words | The Greek says "we speak falsely" (ψευδόμεθα) when we speak of relations. |
| VI.1.7.p3 | Our task, thus, is to give full value to this elusive character of Relation | The Greek says only "having noted this" (τοῦτ’ οὖν ἐπισημηναμένους), the three cases just listed. |
| VI.1.8.p3 | though here both producer and product have a sort of actuality, which we call life | The Greek says this of what acts and what is acted on (τὸ ποιητικὸν δὲ καὶ τὸ παθητικόν), the next pair: they have "as it were a life and an act"; the father and son are the example before. |
| VI.1.8.p4 | we are overlooking the equivocation involved | The Greek proposes that we "must call homonymous" (λεκτέον ὁμώνυμον) the relation that makes and the one that does not: the two share only the name. Nothing is said of overlooking. |
| VI.1.9.p1 | the Act and the Reason-Principle in the Act must be assumed to be real | "Reason-Principle", the term of MacKenna's volumes, which Page kept, renders λόγος, the forming principle, as the Workshop calls it throughout. |
| VI.1.9.p2 | we are bound to seek their causes in that higher sphere | The Greek speaks of "sharings in forms" as "causes" (εἰδῶν μεταλήψεις αἰτίας), and gives the double itself as the cause of being double; there is no "higher sphere" in the sentence. |
| VI.1.9.p3 | whether sharing in the same Form could make one man depraved and another more depraved | The Greek says "ugly" and "uglier" (αἰσχρός, αἰσχίων): the example is of looks, a form that has not mastered the matter, not of vice. |
| VI.1.10.p3 | but a natural possession of the mature human being | The Greek says that what is a quality is rather "what one might acquire from virtue" (ὃ ἐξ ἀρετῆς κτήσαιτο ἄν τις), being reasonable, as against the rational that belongs to man as man. |
| VI.1.10.p4 | that is, excellences and imperfections whether of soul or of body | The Greek says "beauties and uglinesses" (κάλλη καὶ αἴσχη), the words of the next sentences. |
| VI.1.10.p6 | restricting the term Quantities to Ideal-Forms and powers | The Greek speaks of "qualities" (ποιότητας) in forms and powers; "Quantities" is a slip. |
| VI.1.10.p7 | Whatever is seen to apply alike to Being and to non-Being | The Greek says "to substance and to what is not substance" (εἰς οὐσίαν καὶ εἰς μὴ οὐσίαν): the heat that is part of what fire is, and the heat it gives to other things. |
| VI.1.10.p8 | modified in proportion to its success in shaping that object | The Greek says the triangle is a quality "in so far as it has shaped" its subject (καθ’ ὅσον ἐμόρφωσεν); and Page leaves out the last sentence, that humanity too shapes, or rather makes a substance, and so is no quality. |
| VI.1.11.p3 | Whether some qualities as distinguished from others are derived from experience is immaterial | The Greek says "from an affection" (ἐκ πάθους), Aristotle's affective qualities, such as pallor from fear; not from experience. |
| VI.1.12.p2 | allotted to appetite, emotion, reason | The Greek names Plato's three parts of the soul: the desiring, the spirited (θυμοειδοῦς) and the reasoning; "emotion" is Page's word for the spirited part. |
| VI.1.13.p1 | With regard to Date | The Greek names the category "the when" (τὸ ποτέ); Aristotle's examples are "yesterday, last year" (Categories 4, 2a2). |
| VI.1.13.p6 | as in this case something acts in time | The Greek speaks of "making one thing be in another" (τῷ ἕτερον ἐν ἑτέρῳ ποιεῖν), putting it there, not of acting: the next chapter lists the many ways of being "in". |
| VI.1.14.p1 | The Academy and the Lyceum are places, and parts of Place | Page leaves out the chapter's first words, "where, as in the Lyceum and in the Academy", the category and its example as the school gives them. |
| VI.1.15.p2 | But Motion is a distinct genus | The Greek says "motion is one kind of the things that are" (ἓν γένος ἡ κίνησις τῶν ὄντων), one of the kinds of being, as in Plato's Sophist. |
| VI.1.16.p2 | a moving man has motion and a cutter cuts before there is any question of Quantity | The Greek says "the one moving has already moved, and the one cutting has already cut" (ἤδη κεκίνηται ... ἤδη ἔτεμε); the question of quantity is Page's. |
| VI.1.16.p3 | Our critic, it is true, may find support in that principle of proportion | The Greek says "the analogy too bears witness to this" (μαρτυρεῖ δὲ τούτῳ καὶ ἡ ἀναλογία) and names no critic: what follows from it, as the next paragraph says, is an absurdity for those who set act apart from motion. |
| VI.1.17.p3 | Why not bisect the unity, Motion | The Greek divides "being moved" (τὸ κινεῖσθαι), the kind that would be set beside motion as acting is set beside action, not motion itself. |
| VI.1.18.p2 | together with the faculty of thought | The Greek has "as thought (νόησις) is", the thinking, not a faculty; so too the perception set beside the perceptible is perception (αἴσθησις). |
| VI.1.19.p4 | the distinct emotion supervening | The Greek says "another movement (κίνημα) that comes upon it"; pain is its example, not an emotion. |
| VI.1.19.p9 | Suppose this Passion to be treated as of itself producing pain | The Greek supposes someone who acts "so as to cause pain" (ὥστε λυπεῖν), an agent who means to hurt, not a passion. |
| VI.1.20.p4 | produced by its Reason-Principle | "Reason-Principle", the term of MacKenna's volumes, which Page kept, renders λόγος, the forming principle, as the Workshop calls it throughout. In the Greek the forming principle whitens, and the swan is whitened as it comes to be. |
| VI.1.20.p6 | seeing that the Act of the agent passes into him (and becomes his Act) | The words in brackets are Page's: the Greek says only that the agent's act "goes into him" (εἰς αὐτὸν ἰούσης). |
| VI.1.21.p1 | Surely we must look for it in cases where the patient remains without Act | The Greek asks it as a question (ἀλλ’ ἆρα ... ;), which the cases that follow answer against. |
| VI.1.21.p1 | Imagine a case where an agent improves | In the Greek it is the thing acted on that "became better" while the act "had the worse": being acted on can be better than acting. |
| VI.1.21.p5 | there is the passivity which tends to alteration for better or for worse | The Greek's two kinds are becoming worse and becoming better, with "or neither" (ἢ οὐδέτερον) added after; Page joins the two into one and makes "neither" the second. |
| VI.1.22.p1 | whether the motion be aimless | The Greek's word is "free-standing" (ἀπόλυτον), a motion that does not go into another, as walking does not; nothing is said of an aim. |
| VI.1.22.p3 | involve the possession of the powers respectively to walk and to think | The Greek says they "have walking" and "have thought" (ἔχει βάδισιν ... νόησιν), the motions themselves, not powers. |
| VI.1.22.p4 | but remains selfgathered | The Greek says only that thinking goes "not into the thing thought, but about it" (οὐ ... εἰς αὐτὸ τὸ νοούμενον, ἀλλὰ περὶ αὐτοῦ); that it "remains selfgathered" is Page's. |
| VI.1.22.p5 | if a man as he wails produces footprints | "Wails" is the scan's misreading of Page's "walks": the Greek is "walking" (βαδίζων). |
| VI.1.23.p1 | As for Possession | The Greek names the category "having" (τὸ ἔχειν); Aristotle's examples are "has shoes on, is armed" (Categories 4, 2a3). |
| VI.1.24.p1 | There remains Situation | The Greek's category is "lying" (τὸ κεῖσθαι); Aristotle's examples are "is lying, is sitting" (Categories 4, 2a2-3). |
| VI.1.25.p1 | Substrates, Qualities, States, and Relative States | The Greek names "qualified things" (ποιά), not qualities, and things "disposed in some way" (πὼς ἔχοντα) and "disposed in some way relative to something" (πρός τί πως ἔχοντα): the Stoics' four are ways a body is, not abstractions. |
| VI.1.26.p2 | a principle and a rational law (logos) | "Rational law" is Page's for λόγος, the forming principle, as the Workshop calls it throughout. |
| VI.1.26.p4 | by participation and in an accidental mode | The Greek says "by a coming together" (κατὰ σύμβασιν), the joining of parts into one; "by accident" (κατὰ συμβεβηκός) is the word Plotinus uses after, and explains. |
| VI.1.27.p5 | the living form | The Greek has "the living being" (τὸ ζῷον), the animal, not its form. |
| VI.1.29.p3 | even their Seminal Principles (Logoi) | The Greek says only "the forming principles" (οἱ λόγοι); "Seminal" is Page's, after the Stoics' seminal principles (σπερματικοὶ λόγοι). |
| VI.1.30.p5 | or even supposed the Composite to be the Authentic Existent | The Greek has only "or both together" (τὸ συναμφότερον), principles both bodies and bodiless; "the Authentic Existent" is Page's. |
| VI.2.1.p2 | And do both the sets of categories we have been examining imply | The Greek has only "on the one side ... on the other" (ἐπὶ μὲν τῶν ἑτέρων ... ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν ἑτέρων), the principles and the kinds; nothing here of the two sets of categories examined in VI.1. |
| VI.2.1.p3 | these questions become imperative, once we are satisfied as to the number of genera | The Greek says we must look into these questions "first setting out" (πρότερον εἰς μέσον θέντας) what number of kinds we speak of, and how: the number is the first task, not a condition already met. |
| VI.2.1.p5 | implying that the nature of True Being has been quite misapprehended | The Greek says Plato showed "them" (αὐτοῖς) that what truly is is something else; nothing here of a misapprehension. |
| VI.2.2.p1 | the number may well be infinite | The Greek asks "is it some number? Or unlimited?" (ἆρα ἀριθμόν τινα; ἢ ἄπειρον), and leaves it a question; Page answers it. |
| VI.2.2.p4 | that there are genera as distinct from principles of Being | The Greek agrees that there are kinds "and moreover" (καὶ προσέτι) principles of substance: the same things are kinds and principles, as the chapter has argued, not kinds distinct from principles. |
| VI.2.2.p4 | it would be more plausible to dispense with their separate existence | The Greek says it would be much more reasonable "to bring them under one" (ὑφ’ ἕν): the objection is that one kind over them would be the likelier view. |
| VI.2.2.p6 | we are not predicating the Absolute Whiteness | The Greek says only that he does not call it "just what white is" (ὅπερ λευκόν), white in its essence; Page's capitals make it a Platonic form. Being said of each thing would be said by accident, not as its kind. |
| VI.2.3.p5 | for thus we shall also discover their causes | The Greek says "so too the how" (οὕτω γὰρ καὶ τὸ πῶς): knowing how many the kinds are will show how they are one and many; nothing here of their causes. |
| VI.2.4.p1 | the pseudosubstance, the quantity, the quality | The Greek says "something like substance" (οἷον οὐσία): the substrate of a body is called substance only loosely, since perceptible substance is not substance in the proper sense (VI.3.2-8); Page's "pseudo" makes it false. |
| VI.2.4.p3 | single, continuous, without extension | The Greek's words are "one, without extension, without magnitude, most simple" (μίαν, ἀδιάστατον, ἀμεγέθη, ἁπλούστατον); "continuous" is Page's, and a word for bodies. |
| VI.2.5.p3 | the Reason-Principles of the things of process | VI.1 and VI.2 keep λόγος as "the forming principle", the principle in soul that forms what comes to be; "Reason-Principle" is Page's word for it, after MacKenna. |
| VI.2.6.p2 | One, in the sense of being one Reason-Principle | λόγος here too is the forming principle; "Reason-Principle" is Page's. |
| VI.2.6.p3 | its purpose is the Act of the Intellect | The Greek says only "so that it may think" (ἵνα νοήσῃ): thinking needs the many; nothing here of the act of intellect as a purpose. |
| VI.2.7.p3 | Being, then, containing many species, has but one genus | The Greek says being "has many species and is one kind" (εἴδη πολλὰ καὶ ἓν γένος): being is itself the kind, not something that has one. |
| VI.2.7.p3 | in the individual they may be mutually exclusive | The Greek speaks of "the one being" (ἐπὶ τοῦ ἑνὸς ὄντος) of Plato's Parmenides, where each part "had the other" (εἶχε θάτερον); Page makes it the individual, and the having exclusion. |
| VI.2.7.p4 | single Reason-Principle—these are characteristics of the higher sphere | Here λόγος is the account a thing keeps, as what is always the same keeps one, not the forming principle; "Reason-Principle" is Page's. |
| VI.2.8.p2 | how it puts apart things that were together | The Greek says "how it stands apart, being together" (ὡς διέστηκεν ὁμοῦ ὄντα): the things in intellect are distinct while together; Page makes intellect part what was together. "Stands in itself" and "stands apart" (ἕστηκεν, διέστηκεν) are one word, as rest and otherness will be found in it. |
| VI.2.8.p3 | Now in the Act of Intellect there are energy and motion | The Greek says "in its thinking" (ἐν τῷ νοεῖν) are the act (ἐνέργεια) and the motion; Page puts "Act" for the thinking and "energy" for the act. |
| VI.2.8.p3 | Intellect bridges the dualism (of agent and patient) | The two that are joined are the seeing and what is seen, the act and being; "agent and patient" is Page's. |
| VI.2.8.p4 | the Form-Idea has Stability, since it is the goal of Intellect | The Greek says the form, at rest, is "the limit" (πέρας) of intellect, its boundary, not its goal. |
| VI.2.8.p5 | We have caught the radiance of Being | The Greek says "having come into contact" (ἐν προσβολῇ) with the nature of being: a touch or approach of thought; "radiance" is Page's. |
| VI.2.8.p5 | found Being to contain Difference | The kind is ἑτερότης, "otherness", Plato's "the other" (θάτερον) of the Sophist; the modern version keeps "otherness" and "the other" for it, and "difference" for διαφορά, the difference that divides a kind into species. |
| VI.2.8.p5 | do we not see in this amalgamation Identity emerging | ταυτότης is "sameness", Plato's "the same" (ταὐτόν); Page's "Identity". |
| VI.2.8.p6 | the thing of Sense has, it is clear, a particular identity | The Greek says only that "each" (ἕκαστον) of the later things is a certain same and a certain other; "the thing of Sense" is Page's, and the later things are first the intelligible species. |
| VI.2.8.p7 | they do not even attain to its level | The Greek says they "do not stand above it" (οὐδὲ ἐπαναβέβηκεν αὐτῷ), nor are prior to being, as a kind would be over its species; Page reverses it, as if they fell short of being. |
| VI.2.9.p3 | The mind accepts: differences of Being | The Greek says "intellect" (ὁ νοῦς) accepts it; the modern version keeps "intellect" for νοῦς throughout. |
| VI.2.9.p5 | Admitted that not everything suffices to produce a genus | The Greek's objector says the one is not the kind "of all things, but one thing by itself, like the others" (μὴ πάντων ... ἀλλὰ ἕν τι ἐφ’ ἑαυτοῦ, ὥσπερ τὰ ἄλλα): a sixth kind beside the five. |
| VI.2.10.p2 | The unit is prior to number; yet number it must be | The Greek says "the one is not a number" (οὐ γὰρ ἀριθμὸς τὸ ἕν); nothing here of its being prior. |
| VI.2.11.p1 | especially the dividing of the genera Being and unity | The Greek asks about the division "and in general that of the kinds" (καὶ ὅλως ὁ τῶν γενῶν), and whether each division is the same; Page narrows it to being and unity. |
| VI.2.11.p4 | related not so much to Being as to a standard of perfection | The Greek says the one in each thing "looks to the good" (πρὸς ἀγαθὸν βλέπειν); "a standard of perfection" and "perfection" are Page's for the good, here and in what follows. |
| VI.2.11.p5 | Unity is thus identical with Goodness | The Greek says only "so it is with the good too" (οὕτω γὰρ καὶ τὸ ἀγαθόν): the good, like the one, is origin and goal; Page makes them identical. |
| VI.2.11.p7 | not however in the same sense as for things of the physical order | The Greek says "not in the same way, but otherwise" (οὐχ ὡσαύτως δέ, ἀλλὰ ἄλλως), and names no physical order. |
| VI.2.12.p1 | Just as all other inanimates find their perfection in their unity | The Greek says only that the question "is common to the other soulless things too" (κοινὸν τοῦτο), and gives no answer here. |
| VI.2.12.p1 | but perfection does exist in such things, for example in a circle | The Greek's second case is the point "in other things, as in a circle" (εἰ δ’ ἐν ἄλλοις, οἷον ἐν κύκλῳ), the point that is not by itself; Page joins it to the first. |
| VI.2.13.p1 | in number the posterior is divided from the prior | The Greek says "the later comes from the earlier" (τὸ ὕστερον παρὰ τοῦ προτέρου); nothing here of a division. |
| VI.2.13.p2 | for it contains within itself number, line and surface | The Greek says magnitude is "number in this" (ἀριθμὸς ἐν τῷδε), and the line "a sort of two", the plane "three": magnitude is number in something, the line and the plane numbered two and three. |
| VI.2.13.p4 | or that number belongs to the genus Stability | The Greek says number belongs to "what stands still" (ἑστώτων), not to the kind rest; and it joins the two thoughts ("and number ...") rather than setting them against each other. |
| VI.2.14.p2 | the qualities may be detached from the common core around which they are found to group themselves | The Greek says "a certain commonness will be seen in them" (κοινότης τις ἐν αὐταῖς): something common among the qualities themselves, which would make quality a kind; Page makes it a core they gather round. |
| VI.2.14.p3 | are only by analogy called qualities | The Greek says "by a shared name only" (ὁμωνύμως), not by analogy. |
| VI.2.14.p3 | he is, on the contrary, Substance in a higher degree before he arrives at differentiation | The Greek says he is substance "from above" (ἄνωθεν), already, before coming to the difference: higher up the division, from the kind down, not in a higher degree. |
| VI.2.15.p2 | If Motion is the Act of Substance, and Being and the Primaries in general are its Act | The Greek says being and the first things "are act" (ἐνέργεια δὲ τὸ ὂν), not the act of substance: motion is the act of being, and being is itself act. |
| VI.2.16.p1 | Apparently it has less claim than even Place | The Greek says "more likely, perhaps, not" (μᾶλλον δὲ ἴσως οὔ): time is probably not there at all; Page compares it with place. |
| VI.2.17.p2 | but as a constituent | The Greek says being is good "in itself" (ἐν αὐτῷ), not as a quality; "constituent" is Page's. |
| VI.2.17.p3 | If however we must make goodness a genus as well (as a transcendent source) | Page's parenthesis is not in the Greek: the good set down as a kind would be the good in being, later than substance, not the good beyond. |
| VI.2.18.p1 | none the less Motion by being directed towards Absolute Beauty | The Greek says "if the act is toward that" (πρὸς ἐκεῖνο), the beautiful thing seen, which moves us; "Absolute Beauty" is Page's. |
| VI.2.18.p2 | or perhaps more suitably under Stability | The Greek says "if you like" (εἰ δὲ βούλει), not "more suitably": either will do. |
| VI.2.18.p3 | Intelligence, since it connotes intelligent Being and comprises the total of existence | The Greek's νοῦς is "intellect", as Page himself has it in the same sentence ("the true Intelligence (or Intellect)"), and it is "being that thinks" (ὂν νοοῦν), composite of all the kinds. |
| VI.2.19.p3 | the particular intellect, Intellect as such being found identical with Being | The Greek says "each intellect" (νοῦν ... ἕκαστον) is what is from all the beings, and that being and substance, set down as intellect, are before all of them as their species and parts; "Intellect as such" is Page's. |
| VI.2.20.p2 | we find that what subsists in the particulars must be maintained from the totality | The Greek says the particular intellects are "filled from all" (ἐκ πάντων πληρωθέντας): completed from all the kinds. |
| VI.2.20.p3 | all are latent in it | The Greek says all are "at rest" (ἥσυχα) in it: still, not hidden. |
| VI.2.21.p1 | while, in virtue of its Reason-Principle, remaining a unity | The modern version keeps λόγος as "forming principle"; "Reason-Principle" is Page's. |
| VI.2.21.p2 | so that they are infinite, infinity, Magnitude-Absolute | The Greek says "the great" (τὸ μέγα); Page's capitals make it a form. The great is the first of the things Plotinus will see coming forth, as magnitude. |
| VI.2.21.p3 | Identity; coming in with Difference, creates equality | The Greek says sameness, "being present" (συνοῦσα), makes equality; Page has it come in "with Difference", the kind that makes inequality. |
| VI.2.21.p4 | may itself be regarded as one great and perfect Reason-Principle | λόγος is "forming principle" here too; "Reason-Principle" is Page's, for the forming principles and for the one great one alike. |
| VI.2.21.p5 | we conclude that Being has so created Intellect | The Greek says one would think "being, an intellect that had reasoned" (τὸ ὂν νοῦν λελογισμένον), had made things so: being is the intellect that seems to have reasoned, not the maker of intellect. |
| VI.2.21.p6 | it is obvious that Being is best served by the domination of Intellect | The Greek says it would be at its best "without toil" (ἀμογητί), lying in accord with intellect; nothing here of domination. |
| VI.2.22.p1 | There is Intellect in our intellect also | The Greek says "our intellect, having them in itself" (ὁ νοῦς ἡμῶν ἔχων ἐν αὑτῷ), the forms, sees them better in what is before it; nothing here of an intellect within our intellect. |
| VI.2.22.p2 | has bound up with it the principle of Difference | The Greek says "the nature of the other" (τὴν τοῦ θατέρου φύσιν), Plato's phrase in the Sophist (258d-e) and the Timaeus (35a); the kind is otherness, as in chapter 8. |
| VI.2.22.p5 | the other powers imposed by the particular Reason-Principle | The Greek says only "according to the forming principle" (κατὰ λόγον), or "in due proportion"; "particular", and the parenthesis on the being ensouled, are Page's. |
| VI.3.1.p1 | We have still to investigate the opposed principle (the principle of Becoming) | The Greek has "the other nature" (τῆς ἑτέρας φύσεως), the perceptible over against the intelligible; "opposed principle" and "Becoming" are Page's, though Plotinus will call substance here becoming (chapter 2). |
| VI.3.1.p2 | Suppose that we were making a division of speech | The Greek's φωνή is vocal sound, the unlimited range of the voice, divided into letters (στοιχεῖον, "letter", which Page makes "element" below), as in the Philebus; not speech. |
| VI.3.1.p3 | analogous indeed but never identical, a mere image of the higher | The Greek says this world is "not synonymous, but homonymous, and an image" (οὐ συνώνυμον, ὁμώνυμον δὲ καὶ εἰκών): it shares that world's name but not its account; Page puts "analogous" for the shared name. |
| VI.3.1.p3 | taken in the interests of commerce and taxation | The Greek ranks the citizens "by property ratings or by crafts" (κατὰ τιμήσεις ἢ τέχνας); there is no census for commerce and taxation. |
| VI.3.2.p1 | only by analogy that the nature manifested in bodies is designated as Substance | The Greek says "by a shared name" (ὁμωνύμως), not by analogy; chapter 1 kept the two apart (ἀναλογίᾳ καὶ ὁμωνυμίᾳ). |
| VI.3.2.p2 | our first-principles will be compounds | The Greek says "we would be beginning from syllables" (ἀπὸ συλλαβῶν): bodies are to matter and form as syllables to letters, the image of chapter 1; Page drops it. |
| VI.3.2.p3 | The inertia of Matter would correspond with Stability | The Greek sets matter's "not going out of itself" (τὴν οὐκ ἔκστασιν) for rest (στάσις), the third of the five kinds of VI.2; "inertia" and "Stability" are Page's. |
| VI.3.2.p3 | while the Identity and Difference of the Intellectual | The Greek has "the same and the other" (τὸ ταὐτὸν καὶ θάτερον), and here "otherness" (ἑτερότης) and "unlikeness"; "Identity" and "Difference" are Page's names for the kinds. The modern version keeps "sameness" and "otherness", and "difference" only for διαφορά. |
| VI.3.2.p4 | As for Stability, how can it belong to Matter | The Greek asks how there can be "rest" (στάσις) of matter; "Stability" is Page's name for the kind. |
| VI.3.3.p2 | Matter separates that Form from other Forms | In the Greek it is form itself, with the words "about matter" or "in matter" added, that is separated from the other forms (or separates them); matter is not the subject. |
| VI.3.3.p2 | make the Reason-Principle of Substance dependent upon Form | The Greek has "the substantial forming principle according to the form" (λόγον τὸν οὐσιώδη κατὰ τὸ εἶδος); "Reason-Principle" is Page's name for the forming principle (λόγος), and "dependent upon" his. |
| VI.3.3.p4 | the Sensible counterpart of Substance | The Greek has "the substance here, which shares the name" (τὴν ἐνταῦθα ὁμώνυμον οὐσίαν): substance only by a shared name, not a counterpart. |
| VI.3.3.p4 | with Time and Space already included (under Relation) | The Greek says only "once place and time are taken" (ληφθέντος τόπου καὶ χρόνου), as kinds in the list; "(under Relation)" is Page's. |
| VI.3.4.p2 | we may think Substance validly defined as that which is not predicated of anything else | The Greek says "what is not said to be of another" (τὸ μὴ ἑτέρου ὃ λέγεται): belonging to another, as white belongs to the whitened, not being predicated of it. Page keeps "predicated" through the chapter. |
| VI.3.4.p3 | or, in so far as it is a part of the differentiated object | The Greek has "being part of such a thing" (μέρος ὂν τοιούτου), of a thing that is just what it is, of itself; "differentiated object" is Page's. |
| VI.3.5.p1 | These are incontrovertible facts in regard to the pseudo-substance | The Greek says only that "this must be heard as said of what is called substance here" (ἀκουστέον δὲ ταῦτα περὶ τῆς ἐνθάδε οὐσίας λεγομένης); "incontrovertible facts" and "pseudo-substance" are Page's. |
| VI.3.5.p4 | Place is the limit of the surrounding space | The Greek says "the limit of what contains" (πέρας τοῦ περιέχοντος), the containing body, as in Aristotle; not of a space. |
| VI.3.6.p3 | Being is regarded as comprising whiteness in its own extension | The Greek says the white is "taken together with being" (συνειλημμένον τῷ ὄντι); there is no extension of being. |
| VI.3.6.p4 | dependent for its whiteness upon participation in that Supreme Being whose existence is underived | The Greek says the really white has its being (τὸ εἶναι), not its whiteness, by participation in the being there; "Supreme" and "underived" are Page's. |
| VI.3.7.p1 | These are Reason-Principles and more directly derived from Being | The Greek calls the things upon matter "forming principles" (λόγοι); "Reason-Principles" is Page's name for them. |
| VI.3.7.p1 | a vain attempt to achieve a Principle | The Greek says matter is "a falling-away from forming principle" (ἔκπτωσις λόγου), not an attempt to reach one. |
| VI.3.7.p4 | they are clearly in some sense different | The Greek says "for there is difference among those too" (διαφορὰ γὰρ καὶ ἐν ἐκείνοις), probably among the things there from which matter and form come, not between matter and form. |
| VI.3.8.p1 | nor predicated of any other thing | The Greek says "nor of another" (οὐδὲ ἄλλου): not belonging to another, as in chapter 4, rather than not predicated. |
| VI.3.8.p3 | while Sensible Substance as a whole is merely the sum of these coagulations | The Greek draws no line between a particular substance and the whole: it asks whether all these, "fixed together upon one matter" (συμπαγέντα ἐπὶ ὕλης μιᾶς), are one substance. |
| VI.3.9.p3 | between whiteness and the white object | The Greek has "a certain white and white" (τὶ λευκὸν καὶ λευκόν), the particular and the universal, as this fire and fire; not whiteness against the white thing. |
| VI.3.9.p4 | this particularity can have no effect whatever in adding to his essential manhood | The Greek asks what "of such a sort" would do "toward his being more substance" (πρὸς τὸ μᾶλλον οὐσίαν εἶναι), not toward his manhood. |
| VI.3.9.p4 | for the Reason-Principle will be less effectual in Matter | The Greek says that in matter the forming principle (λόγος) is "worse" (χείρων); "Reason-Principle" and "less effectual" are Page's. |
| VI.3.9.p5 | hence, the Form-Idea is prior to the individual | The Greek's εἶδος here is the species, over the individual; "Form-Idea" is Page's. |
| VI.3.9.p5 | would be incompatible with a single Reason-Principle of Substance | The Greek's λόγος here is the account, the definition, of substance; "Reason-Principle" is Page's. |
| VI.3.11.p1 | number and magnitude are to be regarded as the only true qualities | The Greek says these alone are to be called "quantities" (ποσὰ μόνον); "qualities" is a slip. |
| VI.3.11.p1 | to be three yards long | The Greek has "three cubits" (τρίπηχυ), the example of quantity in Aristotle's list. |
| VI.3.11.p3 | its greatness is due, not to any external, but to its own participation in the Absolute Great | The Greek says only that a thing is "great in itself, by its magnitude, and in relation to another not so" (ἐφ’ ἑαυτοῦ μὲν μέγα μεγέθους, πρὸς ἄλλο δὲ οὐ τοιοῦτον); "the Absolute Great" is Page's. |
| VI.3.12.p2 | so there must be a Reason-Principle of greatness | The Greek has a "forming principle" (λόγος) of the great, as of the beautiful; "Reason-Principle" is Page's name for it. |
| VI.3.12.p3 | could only be a contrary if there were something in the universe | The Greek says that up "would not be contrary to anything, since there is no down in the All" (μὴ ὄντος τοῦ κάτω ἐν τῷ παντί): there is none, and Page makes it a condition. |
| VI.3.13.p1 | The same principle gives rise to the numerical distinction | The Greek says only "from here on, for number, odd and even" (τὸ δ’ ἐντεῦθεν ἤδη ἐπὶ μὲν ἀριθμοῦ περιττῷ, ἀρτίῳ); there is no "same principle". |
| VI.3.13.p1 | they are either to be abandoned to the objects numbered | The Greek leaves the differences "to those who deal with number" (τοῖς περὶ ἀριθμὸν ἔχουσιν), the arithmeticians, not to the things numbered. |
| VI.3.13.p2 | there is no common basis in first, second and third dimensions | The Greek has the first, second and third "increase" (αὔξη), the growth of a line into a plane and of a plane into a solid, as in Plato; "dimensions" is Page's here. |
| VI.3.13.p4 | the last also referred to as | The Greek says "which he has called body" (ὃ δὴ κέκληκε σῶμα): Aristotle, whose list of the continuous quantities is line, surface and body. |
| VI.3.14.p4 | disposed to see in figures or shapes (qualitative) varieties of Quantity | The Greek has "differences of quantity" (ποσότητος διαφοράς), the same word as the differences of substance; "(qualitative)" and "varieties" are Page's. |
| VI.3.15.p1 | Triangles, it is significant, are said to be similar rather than equal | The Greek says only that "triangles are said to be like" (ὅμοια γὰρ τρίγωνα λέγεται), as an objection to Aristotle's rule; "rather than equal" is Page's. |
| VI.3.15.p1 | if we are told that equality and inequality are characteristic of Quantity | The Greek has "if he said" (εἰ ἴδιον εἶπε), Aristotle, whose two rules are being weighed; Page leaves him out. |
| VI.3.15.p2 | similarity is predicable of Quantity only in so far as Quantity possesses (qualitative) differences | The Greek says "in so far as the differences are in it" (καθόσον αἱ διαφοραὶ ἐν αὐτῷ), the differences of quantity, the figures of chapter 14; "(qualitative)" is Page's. |
| VI.3.15.p3 | we predicate equality of triangles, rectangles, and figures generally | The Greek has "triangles and squares" (τρίγωνα καὶ τετράγωνα), the four-angled figures; "rectangles" is Page's. |
| VI.3.15.p4 | Thus, for the essence of fire we must look to the Reason-Principle | The Greek has "the forming principle" (ὁ λόγος), which "signifies rather the something" (τὸ τί); "Reason-Principle" is Page's name for it, here and below. |
| VI.3.15.p4 | but we apply the name Socrates to the Socrates we see | The Greek says the reverse: it is "not the perceptible Socrates" (οὐ τὸν αἰσθητὸν Σωκράτην) but colours and shapes, imitations of those in the forming principle, that the name is given to, as to the portrait. |
| VI.3.16.p1 | This beauty—need we say ?—is identical in name only with Intellectual Beauty | The Greek says that beauty here and there is "homonymous" (ὁμώνυμον), sharing a name, as the kinds of chapter 1 did; Page's words agree, but "identical" is his. |
