| VI.3.16.p2 | But the beauty in the germ, in the particular Reason-Principle | The Greek has "in such a forming principle" (τῷ τοιούτῳ λόγῳ), the seed's forming principle; "Reason-Principle" is Page's name for it. |
| VI.3.16.p3 | It may even be doubted whether the arts, as Reason-Principles | The Greek calls the arts "forming principles" (λόγους οὔσας); "Reason-Principles" is Page's name for them. |
| VI.3.16.p3 | Take the case of lyrical music | The Greek's κιθαρῳδία is singing to the lyre (the cithara), whose song is perceptible sound about the strings; not lyric poetry. |
| VI.3.16.p4 | they necessarily belong to that other world in close proximity to the Intellectual | The Greek ranks them "there, towards the intelligible" (πρὸς τὸ νοητὸν ἐκεῖ), with it; "in close proximity" is Page's. |
| VI.3.16.p5 | attendant upon its apostasy | The Greek says they belong to a soul "bending downward" (κάτω νευούσης); "apostasy" is Page's, here and below. |
| VI.3.16.p6 | their manifestation makes for beauty in this world, a beauty regarded not as necessary but as desirable | The Greek says that these virtues "act well here" (ἐνταῦθα τὸ καλῶς ἐνεργοῦσι), setting this acting well before them as their first aim, "not as necessary"; Page makes it a beauty in the world. |
| VI.3.16.p7 | on the ground that they are connected with body and are activities of body | The Greek calls the forming principles "makings of bodies" (σωμάτων ποιήσεις), what makes bodies; not activities of body. |
| VI.3.16.p8 | being affections arising within Soul | The Greek calls them affections "that have bent down here" (πάθη ὄντα νενευκότα τῇδε); they are not said to arise within soul. |
| VI.3.16.p8 | torn between two worlds by their objects and their abode | The Greek divides the affection into "what it is about and what it is in" (εἴς τε τὸ περὶ ὅ ἐστι καὶ ἐν ᾧ ἐστιν); "torn between two worlds" is Page's. |
| VI.3.17.p1 | sounds may also be classified qualitatively as sweet, harsh, soft | The Greek's third quality of sound is "smooth" (λεῖον), set against "rough" (τραχύ); not "soft". |
| VI.3.17.p3 | some colours integrate, others disintegrate the vision | The Greek speaks of what "draws together" (συγκριτικόν) and what "parts" (διακριτικόν) the sight, Plato's and Aristotle's words for black and white; "integrate" and "disintegrate" are Page's, here and in chapter 25. |
| VI.3.17.p4 | but also to their Reason-Principles | The Greek has "their forming principles" (λόγους αὐτῶν), here and in the next sentence; "Reason-Principles" is Page's. |
| VI.3.18.p1 | as in distinguishing dark-green from pale-green | The Greek has "leek-green against pale yellow" (πράσινον ὠχροῦ), two colours said to come from white and black; not two greens. |
| VI.3.18.p2 | there is in the movements of intelligence a diversity which separates one object from another, making further differentiation unnecessary | The Greek has "otherness" (ἑτερότης), the kind of VI.2, which "does not itself need otherness"; "diversity" and "differentiation" are Page's. |
| VI.3.18.p4 | Virtue and vice are two states differing in quality | The Greek says that virtue is "a state of such a sort" and vice "of such a sort" (ἡ μὲν ἕξις τοιάδε, ἡ δὲ τοιάδε): the "such" is the difference that would be a quality. |
| VI.3.18.p5 | hardly reveals the essence but merely the cause of sweetness | The Greek says that "thick" names not what the sweet is (τὸ τί ἦν) but "that in which the sweetness is" (ᾧ ἡ γλυκύτης), its subject; "cause" is Page's. |
| VI.3.18.p6 | if we proceed on the principle that virtue is bound up with pleasure, vice with lust | The Greek divides the virtues among themselves, "one about pleasures, another about angers, one about getting in the harvest, another about spending" (τὴν μὲν περὶ ἡδονάς, τὴν δὲ περὶ ὀργάς); no vice is named. |
| VI.3.19.p2 | if a name or definition, it is a relative | The Greek's λόγος here is an account, spoken words that signify, beside the sound and the name; "definition" is Page's. |
| VI.3.19.p2 | it would be better, in view of their composite nature, not to include the negations in the same genus as the affirmations | The Greek says it is better not to count the negations, "as we do not count the affirmations either" (τάς τε καταφάσεις μὴ συναριθμοῦντας), both being compound; Page sets the one against the other. |
| VI.3.19.p3 | on the other hand, are neither of them qualities but states | The Greek says they are "rather in some way disposed" (μᾶλλόν πως ἔχων), in relation to another; "states" is Page's word, which he gives to the ἕξεις at the end of the chapter. |
| VI.3.19.p4 | Such is the theory | No such words are in the Greek: Plotinus' own conclusion follows at once, that only the motion must be thought of, without the time. |
| VI.3.19.p5 | We shall accordingly maintain that states alone, and not dispositions, constitute qualities | The Greek offers this as a consequence to be weighed: "or else, in this way, we shall call only the states qualities" (ἢ οὕτως τὰς ἕξεις μόνας ποιότητας ἐροῦμεν); Plotinus does not assert it. |
| VI.3.20.p2 | Contrariety however demands that there be some one distinct quality in the intermediates | The Greek gives a reason for setting the intermediates against one another, "because we see some one other colour in each" (τῷ μίαν τινὰ ἄλλην... ἀντιτίθεμεν); contrariety makes no demand. |
| VI.3.20.p2 | taste may be our judge when we have hot, cold and no intermediate | The Greek has "what is neither, between them" (τὸ μηδέτερον μεταξύ): the lukewarm is the intermediate. |
| VI.3.20.p3 | White and yellow are entirely different from each other | The Greek gives this as what someone might say against the usual view, "perhaps someone might grant us this, and yet say" (τάχα δ’ ἄν τις ἡμῖν συγχωροῖ ταῦτα); Page makes it the argument's own conclusion. |
| VI.3.20.p5 | they differ in degree but are not contraries | The Greek's "more or less" (ἢ πλείονα ἢ ἐλάττονα) belongs to the mixing that is ruled out, "by being mixed with other things, their shapes, so to speak, more or less"; Page makes such things differ in degree. |
| VI.3.20.p6 | If indeed these qualities possess an extension quite apart from their participants | The Greek says "if each of them has a latitude" (πλάτος), a range within which it can be more or less; "quite apart from their participants" is Page's. |
| VI.3.21.p1 | These conditions satisfied, we may consider the nature of the genus to which we shall refer it | The Greek's question "into what kind could one bring it?" (εἰς ποῖόν τις γένος αὐτὴν ἀνάξει;) belongs to the first test, whether motion goes back into another kind; Page makes it a further step. |
| VI.3.21.p2 | takes birth and actuality in juxtaposition with a yard | The Greek has "the cubit's length" (τὸ πηχυαῖον), Aristotle's example of a quantity; Page makes it a yard. |
| VI.3.21.p4 | the antecedents of becoming are being confused with becoming itself | The Greek says he "takes what comes before the coming to be" (τὰ πρὸ τῆς γενέσεως λαμβάνει), the alteration and growth before it; Page's words agree, but "confused" is his. |
| VI.3.21.p5 | we must accept as types of Motion knowledge and musical performance | The Greek has "learning and playing the lyre" (ἡ μάθησις καὶ ἡ κιθάρισις), motions from a state (ἀφ’ ἕξεως); knowledge is the state, not the motion. |
| VI.3.22.p1 | on the ground that Motion itself results in difference | The Greek says that "something other" (τὸ ἄλλο) follows on motion; "difference" is kept in the modern version for διαφορά. |
| VI.3.22.p2 | regarded Motion as a dynamic Form in opposition to the other Forms which are static | The Greek calls motion "a waking form" (εἶδος ἐγρηγορός), set against forms that "stand" (ἑστηκόσιν), in that they stay and it does not; "dynamic" and "static" are Page's. |
| VI.3.22.p2 | To proceed to identify this bodily motion with life would however be unwarrantable | The Greek says the reverse: if one were to call this motion "the life of bodies", "he would not be absurd either" (οὐκ ἂν ἄτοπος εἴη); the next words, that it shares only a name with the motions of intellect and soul, give the reason. |
| VI.3.22.p5 | never acquiescing in Identity but always courting Difference | The Greek has "the other, by not staying in the same" (τὸ ἕτερον τῷ οὐκ ἐν τῷ αὐτῷ μένειν); "Identity" and "Difference" are Page's names for the same and the other. |
| VI.3.22.p5 | in the sense of being itself perpetual difference | The Greek has "always otherness" (ἀεὶ ἑτερότης), the kind of VI.2; the modern version keeps "difference" for διαφορά. |
| VI.3.22.p5 | Time is the measure of unceasing Motion | The Greek says that time "is motion measured, not staying" (μεμετρημένη γὰρ κίνησις οὐ μένουσα); Page makes it Aristotle's measure of motion, which Plotinus rejects in III.7. |
| VI.3.23.p1 | that they may neither rest nor preserve their identity | The Greek says "that they do not sleep, nor be in sameness" (μὴ εὕδειν μηδ’ ἐν ταυτότητι εἶναι); "identity" is Page's word for ταυτότης. |
| VI.3.23.p3 | when there is one thing that moves and another that passes from an inherent potentiality to actuality | The Greek asks about two cases, "when another moves it, and when it goes from the potency within it into act" (ὅταν ἄλλο κινῇ, καὶ ὅταν δὲ ἐκ τῆς ἐνούσης δυνάμεως εἰς ἐνέργειαν ἴῃ); Page makes them two parties to one motion. |
| VI.3.23.p4 | when another potentiality demolishes the structure, the result is decay | The Greek has "diminution" (μείωσις), the contrary of growth (αὔξησις), "of what can undergo taking away"; not decay. |
| VI.3.23.p4 | not the destruction of what already exists, but that which intervenes upon the road to existence | The Greek has destruction "not that in what has already come about, but that in what is under way" (οὐχ ἡ ἐν τῷ ἤδη γεγονότι, ἀλλ’ ἡ ἐν τῷ πορευομένῳ): the motion is the destroying, still going on. |
| VI.3.24.p1 | in which case the up-motion constitutes lightness, the down-motion heaviness | The Greek says "if the one is lightness and the other heaviness" (εἰ ἡ μὲν κουφότης εἴη, ἡ δὲ βαρύτης): the powers that move are lightness and heaviness, not the motions. |
| VI.3.24.p2 | in this tendency we seem to have the differentia which separates locomotion from the other species | The Greek says that moving to its own place is common to these motions, "so that here the difference perhaps comes from outside things" (ὥστε ἐνταῦθα κινδυνεύειν παρὰ τὰ ἔξω τὴν διαφορὰν γίνεσθαι); Page makes it the difference of change of place. |
| VI.3.24.p2 | not involving a complete surrender of identity | The Greek says "nor displacing altogether" (οὐδὲ πάντη ἐξισταμένην), the displacing of chapter 21; nothing of sameness is named. |
| VI.3.25.p1 | The nature of integration and disintegration calls for scrutiny | The Greek has "combination and separation" (σύγκρισις, διάκρισις), the gathering and parting of the elements; "integration" and "disintegration" are Page's. |
| VI.3.25.p4 | Are we then to posit a new species for these two motions, adding to them, perhaps, alteration | The Greek asks whether we are to take these by themselves "and bring alteration back to them" (τὴν δὲ ἀλλοίωσιν εἰς ταύτας ἀνακτέον), making alteration a combining or separating; not adding it to them. |
| VI.3.26.p1 | whether the organic motion should be distinguished from the inorganic | The Greek has the motion "of the ensouled and the soulless" (ἐμψύχων καὶ ἀψύχων); "organic" is Page's. |
| VI.3.26.p2 | It may indeed be the case that motions create these differences | The Greek has it the other way: "these produce them" (ποιητικαὶ τούτων αὗται), the differences, according to nature and against it, produce the motions, which could not be without them. |
| VI.3.27.p1 | whether it be Stability or Rest | The Greek has "rest" (στάσις), the kind of VI.2, and "stillness" (ἠρεμία); "Stability" is Page's name for rest, and his "Rest" is the stillness. |
| VI.3.27.p2 | the apparently stable has merely a less strenuous motion | The Greek says that what seems to stand is moving with "a slower motion" (σχολαιοτέρᾳ τῇ κινήσει); "less strenuous" is Page's. |
| VI.3.27.p4 | why not regard Motion as the negation of Stability | The Greek has "a negation of rest" (ἀπόφασιν τῆς στάσεως); "Stability" is Page's name for rest. |
| VI.3.27.p4 | giving it a thousand forms and destroying them all | The Greek says that motion is "working countless things in it and destroying it" (μυρία ἐργαζόμενον αὐτὸ καὶ φθεῖρον), the subject; "forms" is Page's. |
| VI.3.27.p5 | what is of a nature to move is not stationary because the Stability of that realm is motionless | The Greek says that what stands there is not "a thing of a nature to be moved, standing in so far as it is not moved", but stands "in so far as rest has taken hold of it" (καθ’ ὃ στάσις κατείληφεν); Page has rest itself motionless, and calls it "Stability". |
| VI.3.27.p6 | What we have to observe is the essential character of this Sensible counterpart of Stability | The Greek has "the rest itself that is called rest here" (ἡ στάσις αὐτὴ ἡ ἐνταῦθα λεγομένη): Plotinus uses the word "rest" here himself; "Sensible counterpart" and "Stability" are Page's. |
| VI.3.27.p6 | It may be declared that health or sickness is indeed some form of Stability | The Greek has "a sort of rest" (τινὰ στάσιν); "Stability" is Page's, here and in the next sentence. |
| VI.3.28.p2 | We may also adopt the ancient division of relatives into creative principles | The Greek has "as productive" (ὡς ποιητικά), the agent relative to what it acts on; "creative principles" is Page's. |
| VI.3.28.p2 | Our investigation into the kinds of Being is now complete | The Greek says only "and about these kinds, so much" (καὶ περὶ μὲν τούτων τῶν γενῶν ταῦτα), the kinds of the perceptible; Page closes the whole of the three treatises. |
| VI.4.1.p1 | coupled with some native tendency in soul to distribute itself over material mass | The Greek says soul has "a nature to be divided about bodies" (περὶ τὰ σώματα φύσιν ἔχουσα μερίζεσθαι), the Timaeus' phrase, not a tendency to spread itself. |
| VI.4.1.p1 | We may perhaps be told that, though extended over the corporeal, it does not itself become so | The Greek says soul is "stretched out along with the body, not being body" (συνεκτείνεσθαι μὴ σῶμα οὖσα); the magnitude would be soul's by accident, which is what the next words weigh. |
| VI.4.1.p2 | no doubt the Form, White, is the same all over, but there is not arithmetical identity | The Greek says the white on one part is "the same in form" (τὸ αὐτὸ μὲν εἴδει) as that on another, "but not the same in number" (οὐ μὴν ταὐτὸν ἀριθμῷ): two whites of one kind, not the Form White itself. |
| VI.4.1.p3 | whether before material masses exist or as enveloping them | The Greek has "before bodies or in bodies" (εἴτε πρὸ τῶν σωμάτων εἴτ᾽ ἐν τοῖς σώμασι); "enveloping" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.2.p1 | Side by side exist the Authentic All and its counterpart, the visible universe | The Greek says there is the true All and "the imitation of the All" (τοῦ παντὸς μίμημα), this visible nature: an image after its original, not two side by side. |
| VI.4.2.p1 | the boundaries of some surrounding extension considered as an envelope | The Greek has "the limit of the surrounding body, by which it surrounds" (πέρας σώματος τοῦ περιέχοντος καθ᾽ ὃ περιέχει), Aristotle's definition of place; the "extension" is the other view, named next. |
| VI.4.2.p1 | but this objection is merely verbal and will disappear if our meaning is grasped | The Greek raises no objection: whoever says the world is "in" the true All should "drop the predication of the name" (τὴν τοῦ ὀνόματος ἀφεὶς κατηγορίαν) and take the meaning with his thought. |
| VI.4.2.p2 | the omnipresence should be at once self-abiding and universal | The Greek says it is no wonder if "the everywhere" is "in being and in itself" (ἐν τῷ ὄντι καὶ ἐν αὑτῷ): the everywhere is in being, not being in the everywhere. |
| VI.4.2.p3 | meets it everywhere as the omnipresent All ever stretching beyond | The Greek says the world finds it "everywhere all, and greater than itself" (πανταχοῦ πᾶν καὶ μεῖζον ἑαυτοῦ), greater than the world, not stretching beyond it. |
| VI.4.2.p4 | it is content to accept place and subordination | The Greek has "a place and an order" (τόπον καὶ τάξιν), a rank where the world is kept safe as the All's neighbour; "subordination" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.2.p4 | in that movement its every part embraces the All | The Greek says that with every part of itself the world "enjoys the whole of that" (ἀπολαύει ὅλου ἐκείνου); it cannot embrace it, as the paragraph has just said. |
| VI.4.3.p1 | any presence is presence of an emanant power | The Greek says "a power of it" (δύναμιν αὐτοῦ) is present; "emanant" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.3.p2 | becoming the informing principle of some definite thing | The Greek has "becoming the form of this" (τοῦδε εἶδος): the form of one body, which would then belong to it. |
| VI.4.3.p2 | in the same accidental way, we may reasonably put it, soul concurs with body | The Greek says soul "shares the body's affections" (συμπαθεῖν τῷ σώματι) by accident; "concurs" loses the affections. |
| VI.4.3.p4 | do we not thus sunder Intelligence, one intelligence in this man, another in that | The Greek says only "so that one is in one, another in another" (ἵν᾽ ὁ μὲν ᾖ ἐν ἄλλῳ, ὁ δὲ ἐν ἄλλῳ); "this man" is MacKenna's, and the modern version keeps "intellect" for νοῦς. |
| VI.4.4.p2 | would come into being to produce the multiple total of the universe | The Greek says only that what came from it, "as it were parts" (οἷον μέρη), "fill up all things" (συμπληροῦν ἤδη τὰ πάντα); MacKenna's "the separate souls" before it is his gloss. |
| VI.4.4.p3 | No doubt the beings proceed from the Priors in the mode only of numerical distinction and not as concrete masses | The Greek says the things that go forth "are numbers, not magnitudes" (ἀριθμοὶ ὄντα, ἀλλ᾽ οὐ μεγέθη), and so raise the same difficulty; "numerical distinction" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.4.p4 | diversity within the Authentic depends not upon spatial separation but sheerly upon differentiation | The Greek says being is many "by otherness, not by place" (ἑτερότητι, οὐ τόπῳ). The modern version keeps "otherness" for ἑτερότης, one of the five kinds of VI.2. |
| VI.4.4.p4 | thus the Intellectual-Principle (which is Being and the Beings) remains an integral, multiple by differentiation | The parenthesis is MacKenna's; the Greek says "intellect is many by otherness, not by place, but all together" (νοῦς πολὺς ἑτερότητι, οὐ τόπῳ, ὁμοῦ δὲ πᾶς). |
| VI.4.4.p5 | present each to all as never having been set in opposition | The Greek says the souls are present to one another "without being made strangers" (οὐκ ἀλλοτριωθεῖσαι), not without opposition. |
| VI.4.4.p5 | the nature of such a principle must be utterly free of boundary | The Greek says "in this way such a nature is unlimited" (οὕτως ἐστὶν ἄπειρος): it is unlimited because it holds all in itself, not by a demand of the argument. |
| VI.4.5.p1 | it lies spread over the universe | The Greek says "it has taken hold of the All" (τὸ πᾶν κατείληφε), "or rather it is itself the All"; it is not spread. |
| VI.4.5.p2 | too great to be held by body, giving, therefore, to the material universe but little of itself | The Greek says it is "greater than the nature of body" and so would reasonably be thought (νομίζοιτο) to give little; "too great to be held by body" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.6.p1 | Are we to think that this second body, in keeping its soul with a like care | The Greek asks only whether the other body, "having itself too the soul it has" (ἔχον καὶ αὐτὸ τὴν ἣν ἔχει ψυχήν), has the same soul; "with a like care" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.6.p1 | Merely some additions (from the experiences of life, none in the soul itself) | The Greek says only "by the additions" (ταῖς προσθήκαις); the parenthesis is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.6.p2 | it is only as a man pronounces simultaneously upon a pleasant sensation in his finger and a pain in his head | The Greek has no "simultaneously": it is as if "the same one of us" (ὁ αὐτὸς ἡμῶν) judged both. |
| VI.4.6.p3 | Often, as it happens, reason does become aware of a verdict formed in another reason | The Greek says reasoning sees "the judgement in another" (τὸ ἐν ἑτέρῳ κρίμα), another person, and understands his affection; not a verdict in another reason. |
| VI.4.7.p2 | though the hand is only hand-long, not taking the extension of the mass it wields | The Greek says the power seems bounded "as far as it touches" (καθ᾽ ὅσον ἐφάπτεται), while the hand is bounded "by its own quantity" (τῷ αὑτῆς ποσῷ); "hand-long" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.7.p3 | which illuminates not as body but as body lit | The Greek says it had the light "as luminous body" (ᾗ φωτεινὸν σῶμα), a body that gives light, "by another power, which is not bodily"; not a body lit. |
| VI.4.7.p4 | Consider too the refraction of light by which it is thrown away from the line of incidence | The Greek has "the cuttings-off" (αἱ ἀποτομαί), the bodies that block the light, which keep it from the far side and yet do not divide it; there is no refraction. |
| VI.4.8.p2 | passive and partible in view of its close relationship with the body | The Greek says such a thing is "something of body, like an affection or a form" (σώματός ἐστί τι οἷον πάθος ἢ εἶδος); "close relationship" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.8.p3 | we link the variety in the multiples with the unity which we discern | The Greek says we "fasten the affection of the many on that one" (τῶν πολλῶν τὸ πάθος περιάπτεις τῷ ἑνί): the many-ness is the many's affection, laid on the one. |
| VI.4.8.p3 | cannot fail within its own scope (and therefore does reach over the multiple) | The parenthesis is MacKenna's; the Greek says only that, being itself, it "does not fall short of itself" (οὐκ ἀπολείπεσθαι ἑαυτοῦ). |
| VI.4.9.p1 | those firsts cannot be present in the material masses as their Forms if they are to remain identical with the First | The Greek says "if they too are like that first" (εἴπερ ὅμοια καὶ ταῦτα ἐκείνῳ τῷ πρώτῳ): like it, not identical. |
| VI.4.9.p1 | they certainly have not moved away with no purpose in their movement | The Greek says "if they left it, plainly they left it going somewhere" (κατέλιπόν ποι ἰοῦσαι): leaving is going to a place, which powers of what has no place cannot do; there is no purpose in it. |
| VI.4.9.p2 | for There power is Being or something greater than Being | The Greek has "reality and substance, or greater than substance" (ὑπόστασις καὶ οὐσία ἢ μεῖζον οὐσίας); the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία. |
| VI.4.9.p3 | such secondary powers would be perfectly uniform | The Greek says the powers are "of one kind with one another" (ὁμοειδῶν πρὸς ἀλλήλας), not uniform in themselves. |
| VI.4.9.p4 | the conditions of consciousness cease | The Greek has "perceiving together" (συναίσθησις), the one body's feeling of itself as one; "consciousness" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.9.p4 | for example, a reflected light | The Greek has "the image of something, like the weaker light" (τὸ ἴνδαλμά τινος, οἷον καὶ τὸ ἀσθενέστερον φῶς), the dim light of the paragraph before; there is no reflection. |
| VI.4.10.p2 | Nor is there strictly any such making of image as we see in water or in mirrors or in a shadow | The Greek says the painting is not properly the making of an image "as in water and mirrors or in shadows" (οἷον ἐν ὕδασι καὶ κατόπτροις ἢ ἐν σκιαῖς): those are the images made properly, which hang on their originals; MacKenna's "Nor is there" denies them. |
| VI.4.10.p2 | it is in this sense that we are to understand the weaker powers to be images of the Priors | The Greek says this is how "they will claim" (ἀξιώσουσι) that the weaker powers come from the prior: those who hold the powers are cut off must grant it. |
| VI.4.10.p3 | unless we think of warmth as containing fire so that the two are separate things | The Greek says that if there were fire in the warmth, one "will make the warmth apart from fire" (χωρὶς πυρὸς ποιήσει τὴν θερμότητα), which is absurd; the separate things are MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.10.p4 | then since Being (identical with the Intellectual-Principle) becomes transitory, so also must the Beings, its productions | The Greek says they "will make what comes from a flowing substance flowing" (ἐκ ῥεούσης οὐσίας ῥέοντα τὰ ἐξ αὐτῆς); the parenthesis and "the Beings" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.11.p1 | why do not all things participate in the Intellectual Order in its entirety | The Greek has "the intelligible" (τοῦ νοητοῦ); the modern version keeps "intelligible" for νοητός. |
| VI.4.11.p1 | the transparent object and the opaque answer very differently to the light | The Greek has "the clouded" (τῷ τεθολωμένῳ), the troubled or muddied, which takes the light otherwise, not the opaque, which takes none. |
| VI.4.11.p3 | for it is a Reason-Principle, which is to say a unity in variety | The Greek says "the forming principle (λόγος) is one and many" (λόγος γὰρ εἷς καὶ πολύς); "Reason-Principle" is MacKenna's name, and "which is to say a unity in variety" his gloss. |
| VI.4.11.p3 | the differentiation is within Being, obviously not within non-Being | The Greek says "the other is in it and the otherness is its own" (τὸ ἕτερον ἐν αὐτῷ καὶ ἡ ἑτερότης αὐτοῦ). The modern version keeps "otherness" for ἑτερότης. |
| VI.4.11.p3 | for presence in the sensible does not abrogate independence | The Greek says "it is possible to be present while being separate" (ἔστι γὰρ καὶ παρεῖναι χωρὶς ὄν), nothing of the sensible. |
| VI.4.12.p1 | from a great distance many eyes look to the one object and all take it fully | The Greek has no great distance: many eyes saw the same, "though what is seen lies in a bounded place" (ἐν ἀφωρισμένῳ τοῦ θεάματος κειμένου). |
| VI.4.12.p1 | while from the one identical presence another will derive something else | The Greek says "another again, and another, from the same" (καὶ ἄλλο αὖ καὶ ἕτερον ἀπὸ τοῦ αὐτοῦ) will have soul; not something else. |
| VI.4.12.p4 | though even embodied it remains at once the speaker and the silent | The Greek says it remains as in "the one uttering, who in uttering both has the voice and gives it" (ὅστις φωνῶν καὶ ἔχει τὴν φωνὴν καὶ δίδωσι); there is no silent one. |
| VI.4.12.p4 | the soul belongs to that other Kind | The Greek has "the other nature" (φύσεως τῆς ἑτέρας), here the intelligible over against body; MacKenna's "Kind" suggests the kinds of VI.2. |
| VI.4.12.p5 | receives mysteriously that same principle | The Greek says the newcomer has it "from the invisible" (ἐξ ἀφανοῦς): from what cannot be seen, not by a mystery. |
| VI.4.13.p1 | the extended has participated in that kosmos of life which itself has no extension | The Greek says the extended "has shared in all of it" (πᾶν αὐτοῦ μετειληφέναι), the soul's, "which is without extension"; the world of life was chapter 12's. |
| VI.4.13.p2 | the actual two cubits must themselves become the new three (which cannot occur) | The parenthesis is MacKenna's; the Greek says only "since in that way the two themselves will be three" (ἐπεὶ οὕτω γε αὐτὰ τὰ δύο τρία ἔσται). |
| VI.4.13.p4 | the universe must hold that one soul present at its every point | The Greek says "the whole bulk will have it in every part of itself" (ἕξει αὐτὸ κατὰ πᾶν ἑαυτοῦ): it is the bulk, not the universe, and the Greek does not name soul. |
| VI.4.14.p1 | It was to hold life not single but infinite and yet one life, one in the sense not of an aggregate built up | The Greek says all the lives are together "not heaped together into one" (οὐ συμφορηθείσας εἰς ἕν), but beginning from one; "aggregate built up" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.14.p2 | Intelligence inbound with the entire of reality | The Greek has "intellect joined to the whole of substance" (νοῦς συνημμένος τῇ ἁπάσῃ οὐσίᾳ); the modern version keeps "intellect" for νοῦς and "substance" for οὐσία. |
| VI.4.14.p3 | now we have lost that first simplicity | The Greek has no loss here: the voice and its hearers are an image of how the second man adds himself to the first, and "we have become the pair" (γεγενήμεθα τὸ συνάμφω); MacKenna makes the image our first state. |
| VI.4.15.p2 | but that it is warmed and lit by soul entire | The Greek says the body takes "some trace of soul" (τι ἴχνος ψυχῆς), "not a part of that, but like a sort of warmth or illumination that came"; not soul entire. |
| VI.4.15.p3 | This is the evil of state and of council | The Greek has "the vice (κακία) of a city and an assembly" (πόλεως καὶ ἐκκλησίας κακία), the city's badness; "council" is MacKenna's for the assembly. |
| VI.4.15.p4 | and these become masters when the man, the manifold, gives them play | The Greek says they master him when "such a man gives himself over to such a people" (συνδόντος ἑαυτὸν τοῦ τοιούτου ἀνθρώπου δήμῳ τῷ τοιούτῳ); "the manifold" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.16.p1 | the banishment into animal forms | The Greek has "the entries into the bodies of other living beings" (αἱ εἰς ἄλλων ζῴων σώματα εἰσκρίσεις); "banishment" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.4.16.p2 | will give forth freely to body | The Greek says soul at the last place of the intelligible gives of itself "more often" (πλεονάκις), "at shorter intervals" (ἐν βραχυτέροις διαστήμασι), not freely. |
| VI.4.16.p3 | a soul in any way to be described as attached to this universe | The Greek says "a soul called this one's" (ψυχὴ τοῦδε λεγομένη), the soul of one body; not of this universe, whose soul does not become partial. |
| VI.4.16.p4 | it is perfectly the All-Soul, the partial in it latent | The Greek says it is "wholly all" (πάντη πᾶσα), "being a part then as it were potentially"; "All-Soul" is MacKenna's, and the soul of the All is not what is meant. |
| VI.4.16.p5 | the authentic passing uncontaminated into the Intellectual | The Greek says soul is "purely in the intelligible" (καθαρῶς ἐν τῷ νοητῷ); the modern version keeps "intelligible" for νοητός. |
| VI.4.16.p6 | chooses the higher and by that choice blends into the All | The Greek has "inclination" (νεῦσις) "to the other side", and the image is "drawn together toward the whole" (πρὸς τὸ ὅλον συνέσταλται); there is no choice. |
| VI.5.1.p1 | It would be taken as certain if no one asked How | The Greek says that if no one demanded the how of them (τὸν τρόπον), they would lay it down so and rest in it: what is in question is how they hold it, not whether it would be certain. |
| VI.5.1.p1 | for this universal quest of good depends on the fact that all aim at unity | The Greek says the principle that all things desire the good "would be true if" (οὕτω γὰρ ἂν αὕτη ἀληθὴς εἴη, εἰ) all things hasten to one: a condition of its truth, not a fact it depends on. |
| VI.5.1.p2 | towards the Other Order must become manifest as multiplicity | The Greek has "to the other side" (ἐπὶ θάτερα), toward what is other; "the Other Order" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.1.p2 | to this every form of Being is urged in a movement towards its own reality | The Greek says "every nature hastens to this, to itself" (ἐφ' ἑαυτήν): the good of each nature is itself, and "form of Being" and "reality" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.1.p3 | In virtue of that unity the Good may be regarded as truly inherent | The Greek says the good would rightly be called "one's own" (οἰκεῖον): what belongs to each, not what is inherent in it. |
| VI.5.1.p3 | yet that Good is not exclusively ours | The Greek says "nor again has it [stood away] from us" (οὐδ' αὖ ἐκεῖνο ἡμῶν): we are not apart from being, and it is not apart from us; nothing is said of its not being ours alone. |
| VI.5.2.p1 | thus it thinks of the Essential Existence as corporeal and as a thing of parts | The Greek says reasoning "divided substance" (ἐμέρισε τὴν οὐσίαν), thinking it such as bodies are; "Essential Existence" is MacKenna's for οὐσία, and the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.5.2.p1 | we must hold to the Intellectual principles which alone apply to the Intellectual Order | The Greek has "intelligible principles of intelligibles" (νοητὰς νοητῶν); the modern version keeps "intelligible" for νοητός, and "which alone apply" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.2.p2 | On the one hand there is the unstable | The Greek has "what is borne about" (πεφορημένον), carried this way and that, the world of becoming in motion. |
| VI.5.2.p2 | not so much Being as Becoming | The Greek says it would be fitting to call it "becoming, not substance" (γένεσιν ... ἀλλ' οὐκ οὐσίαν): not a matter of degree. |
| VI.5.2.p3 | In every entity the essential nature is the governing principle | The Greek says "the what-it-is (τὸ τί ἐστιν) is the principle" (ἀρχή), the starting point of the argument; "governing" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.2.p3 | where the essential nature is the entire being | The Greek says "those in whose what-it-is everything belongs" (οἷς πάντα ἐν τῷ τί ἐστιν ὑπάρχει): things that have no accidents, whose all is their essence. |
| VI.5.3.p1 | then it must be always self-gathered, never in separation | The Greek says it must "always be with itself" (σὺν αὑτῷ) and "not stand apart from itself" (μὴ διεστάναι ἀφ' αὑτοῦ). |
| VI.5.3.p1 | all that are apt and in the measure of their aptitude | The Greek says the other things share in it, "as many as can be present to it, and as far as it is possible for them to be present to it" (ὅσα δύναται αὐτῷ παρεῖναι): they are present to it, not it to them. |
| VI.5.3.p2 | something produced and projected from it enter at various points into that other order | The Greek says "something else that has come from it, leaving it behind" (γεγονὸς καταλελοιπὸς αὐτὸ), comes to "the other things" (εἰς τὰ ἄλλα); "projected" and "that other order" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.3.p3 | Either would imply something of it remaining there while the emanant is elsewhere | The Greek has "what is from it" (τὸ ἀπ' αὐτοῦ) elsewhere; "emanant" is MacKenna's, a word Plotinus does not use here. |
| VI.5.3.p3 | anything belonging to the Other Order | The Greek has "from the other nature" (ἐκ τῆς ἑτέρας φύσεως), the nature of bodies of chapter 2. |
| VI.5.4.p1 | Then consider this god (in man) | The words "in man" are MacKenna's: the Greek has only "this god" (τόνδε τὸν θεόν), which may be the god in each of us of chapter 1, or the god of this visible world. |
| VI.5.4.p1 | All that have insight into the nature of the divine beings | The Greek says "all who have a notion (ἔννοιαν) of gods": the common notion of chapter 1 again, not an insight into their nature. |
| VI.5.4.p2 | just as a mass cut up ceases to be a mass | The Greek says a magnitude cut into many "will perish" (ἀπολλύμενον), and its parts are "no longer that whole": what is lost is the whole, not the mass. |
| VI.5.4.p3 | holding the god to pervade the Being of man, we hold the omnipresence of an integral identity | The Greek says the disbelieved doctrine "has shown itself in every human nature, together with thinking there is a god" (ἐν πάσῃ φύσει ἀνθρώπου ὁμοῦ τῷ θεὸν νομίζειν): every man who believes in a god already holds it; nothing is said of the god pervading man's being. |
| VI.5.4.p4 | any third will be about that second and move towards it | The Greek speaks of the second only: what comes after the one is "about it and toward it" (περὶ ἐκεῖνο καὶ εἰς ἐκεῖνο), and is like its offspring; the thirds come only in the next sentence. |
| VI.5.4.p4 | where, therefore, the Intellectual tertiaries are present | The Greek has "wherever the thirds are present" (τὰ τρίτα), the thirds of the intelligible; the modern version keeps "intelligible", and "tertiaries" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.5.p1 | but while we provide for individualisation we must carefully preserve mutual presence | The Greek says that, "keeping them all together" (τηροῦντας ὁμοῦ πάντα), we must say that the things called many "have come to be"; "individualisation" and "mutual presence" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.5.p1 | all is power and reality undifferentiated | The Greek has "powers and substances without extension" (ἀδιάστατοι δυνάμεις καὶ οὐσίαι): what they lack is extension, not difference. |
| VI.5.5.p2 | once more we have lines, each touching a generating centre of its own | The Greek says the lines "will fasten on the centres that each left" (ἐξάψονται τῶν κέντρων αὑτῶν ἃ κατέλιπον ἑκάστη); "generating" is MacKenna's, and the next paragraph denies that the lines generate the centres. |
| VI.5.5.p3 | The radii, thus, afford a serviceable illustration for the mode of contact by which the Intellectual Unity manifests itself | The Greek says the lines are analogous to "the things which the intelligible nature touches" (ὧν ἐφαπτομένη ἡ νοητὴ φύσις): the lines stand for the bodies that receive it, not for a mode of contact. |
| VI.5.6.p1 | Thus, when Man enters into human form there exists a particular man | The Greek says Man comes "into some man" (εἰς τόν τινα ἄνθρωπον), the particular man, and becomes some man (τὶς ἄνθρωπος): there is no human form for it to enter. |
| VI.5.6.p2 | material man has come to constitute many individual men | The Greek says the man in matter, from the one Man of the idea, "made many men the same" (πολλοὺς ἐποίησε τοὺς αὐτοὺς ἀνθρώπους): the form in matter makes them the same, it does not compose them. |
| VI.5.6.p3 | This does not mean that Man Absolute, or any Absolute, or the Universe in the sense of a Whole, is absorbed by multiplicity | The Greek says they are "not in many in this way" (οὐχ οὕτως ἐν πολλοῖς), but the many are "in it, or rather about it" (περὶ αὐτό); nothing is absorbed. |
| VI.5.6.p3 | There is a difference between the mode in which a colour may be absorbed by a substance entire | The Greek has "the white is everywhere" (τὸ λευκὸν πανταχοῦ) in one way: the white in each part is the same in form, as in VI.4.1, not absorbed. |
| VI.5.7.p1 | to that we return as from that we came | The Greek says "we go up to it, and to what first comes from it" (τὸ πρῶτον ἀπ' ἐκείνου): nothing is said here of whence we came. |
| VI.5.7.p1 | and to know without image is to be | The Greek says only "and if not this, being those things" (εἰ δὲ μὴ τοῦτο, ὄντες ἐκεῖνα): if we think them without images, it is because we are them. |
| VI.5.7.p2 | looking outward we see many faces ; look inward and all is the one head | The Greek says we are "like many faces" (οἷον πρόσωπα πολλά), outward, "having one crown inward": the faces are ours, a head with many faces, not what we see. |
| VI.5.7.p2 | we cease to rule ourselves out from the total of reality | The Greek says he will stop "drawing a line round himself" (περιγράφειν αὑτόν) to part himself "from all being" (ἀπὸ τοῦ ὄντος ἅπαντος). |
| VI.5.8.p1 | a meaningless conception, for what have distance and separation to do here | The Greek says it is reasonable and necessary that the illumination has come into matter, "the forms not lying apart" (μὴ κειμένων τῶν εἰδῶν χωρίς), lest the word be said emptily: the illumination is kept, without forms lying apart; it is not called meaningless. |
| VI.5.8.p2 | This participation cannot be thought of as elusive or very perplexing ; on the contrary, it is obvious, accessible in many examples. | The Greek is unreal: if the forms lay apart, sharing "would not have been" called the hardest thing to tell and most perplexing, but would have been said readily, known from examples (παραδείγμασιν). It was called so, and it is hard: MacKenna has turned it into the claim that it is obvious. |
| VI.5.8.p3 | the Idea itself not penetrating, not approaching, the Matter | The Greek says the idea is "not going through all of it and running over it" (οὐ ... διεξελθούσης καὶ ἐπιδραμούσης); "not approaching" is MacKenna's, and matter takes what it can "by its nearness" (τῷ πλησιασμῷ). |
| VI.5.8.p4 | The Ideal Fire, then, remaining apart | The Greek says "fire itself, not coming to be in the matter" (αὐτὸ δὴ πῦρ οὐκ ἐγγενόμενον αὐτὸ τῇ ὕλῃ); "remaining apart" is what the chapter denies, since the form is not apart in place. |
| VI.5.8.p5 | supposing that it were a whole of parts (as the analogy would necessitate) | The parenthesis is MacKenna's; the Greek says only "if all of it were many of itself" (εἰ πᾶν αὐτὸ πολλὰ εἴη ἑαυτοῦ). |
| VI.5.8.p6 | The Idea, impartible, gives nothing of itself to the Matter | The Greek has "unscattered" (ἀσκέδαστος): the idea is not dispersed over matter; "impartible" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.8.p6 | bringing it to pattern not by acting part upon part | The Greek has "to shape" (μορφῶσαι) "each and all with all of itself" (παντὶ ἕκαστον καὶ πᾶν): it shapes each with the whole of itself. |
| VI.5.9.p1 | or, more precisely, the making principle must be a partless unity not permeating the sphere | The Greek says what makes the sphere is "not poured out into the sphere" (οὐκ αὐτοῦ χυθέντος εἰς τὴν σφαῖραν), but the sphere is "hung from" it (ἀνηρτημένης): it is not spread through it. |
| VI.5.9.p2 | while others think of it as in its nature a selfincreasing number | The Greek says others called its nature "a number increasing itself" (αὑτὸν αὔξοντα): Xenocrates' "number that moves itself", with "increasing" for "moving", which the next sentences say is not to be taken as the word says. |
| VI.5.9.p2 | the virtue of soul would still compass it | The Greek has "power" (δύναμις), not virtue; MacKenna's "virtue" is the older English sense of power. |
| VI.5.9.p3 | dismembered, it no longer performs any one single function | The Greek says it would be "unable to go to any one completion" (εἰς μίαν τινὰ συντέλειαν), one whole brought together, not a function. |
| VI.5.9.p4 | this, as encircled by a single self-embracing Reason-Principle | The Greek says it has "one forming principle (λόγον) holding itself in" and is "itself what holds it in" (τὸν περιέχοντα αὐτὸν εἶναι): it is not encircled by something else. "Reason-Principle" is MacKenna's for λόγος. |
| VI.5.9.p4 | but over all the universe remaining what it must be | The Greek says "being in itself everywhere" (ἐν αὑτῷ πανταχοῦ ὄντα); "the universe" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.9.p5 | The unity is in this way saved from the local division of the things in which it appears | The Greek says only "it is not, then, parted in this way by the place of another" (ἄλλου τόπῳ διειλημμένον). |
| VI.5.10.p1 | It remains, then, poised in wisdom within itself | The Greek has "self-controlled" (σωφρονοῦν), in its right mind, against the senseless (ἀνόητον) of the last sentence; "wisdom" is the φρονεῖν of the next lines. |
| VI.5.10.p1 | happy when to the utmost of its power it attains | The Greek says Love is "always content" (ἀγαπῶν ἀεί) to share "as far as he can"; nothing is said of attaining. |
| VI.5.10.p2 | because it is thus entire for all it can be The Good to all | The Greek says "and beautiful, because it is whole to all" (καὶ καλόν, ὅτι πᾶσιν ὅλον): it is the beautiful, not the Good. |
| VI.5.10.p2 | So must it be in our participation in the One | The Greek says only "and so from there" (καὶ οὕτως ἐκεῖθεν): the One is not named. |
| VI.5.10.p3 | the road to sense is the road to unity | MacKenna's; the Greek says only that assemblies imitate it, "as men who go to one in wisdom" (ὡς εἰς ἓν τῷ φρονεῖν ἰόντων). |
| VI.5.10.p3 | as they begin to grow together, each, in that true growth, generates wisdom while he recognises it | The Greek says each, "putting it together into one" (συμβάλλων δὲ εἰς ἕν), in "the gathering and the true understanding" (τῇ συνόδῳ καὶ τῇ ὡς ἀληθῶς συνέσει), has begotten and found wisdom: the word plays on coming together (σύνοδος) and understanding (σύνεσις), and there is no growth. |
| VI.5.10.p4 | in any real receiving of good, giver is in contact with taker | The Greek says "the giver gives to those who take, so that they really take" (ἵνα ὄντως λαμβάνωσι); nothing is said of contact. |
| VI.5.10.p4 | Now if in body, whose very nature is partition | The Greek says body by nature "as it were flees from itself" (οἷον φεύγοντος ἑαυτό), its parts outside one another. |
| VI.5.10.p5 | It is therefore by identification that we see the good | The Greek says "being in the same" (ἐν τῷ αὐτῷ ὄντες) we see the good and touch it. |
| VI.5.10.p5 | brought to it by becoming identical with what is of the Intellectual within ourselves | The Greek says "being together with our own intelligibles" (ὁμοῦ ὄντες τοῖς ἡμετέροις νοητοῖς); the modern version keeps "intelligible". |
| VI.5.10.p5 | except, indeed, that it will be less worthy of respect | The Greek has "more absurd" (γελοιοτέρα): an extended intelligible sphere would be laughable, not less respected. |
| VI.5.11.p1 | The fact stands abundantly evident but there is still the need of intellectual satisfaction | The Greek says "it has been shown in many ways that it is so; but some encouragements (παραμυθιῶν) are needed": consolations to persuade, not an intellectual satisfaction. |
| VI.5.11.p1 | It is a First Principle, measureless | The Greek has "a first nature" (πρώτη φύσις), not measured. |
| VI.5.11.p2 | Time is like a radial line running out apparently to infinity | The Greek says time is like a line that seems to go to infinity, "hung from a point and running about it" (περὶ αὐτὸ θεούσης) and "circling about it" (κυκλουμένης): a circle round a centre, not a radius running out. |
| VI.5.11.p2 | as it goes it tells of that centre | The Greek says the point "appears in it" (ἐμφανταζομένου) wherever it runs. |
| VI.5.11.p3 | This secondary of Power, participating as far as it may in that higher, must be identified. | The Greek asks a question, "what, then, is this nature that shares...?" (τίς οὖν αὕτη ἐστὶν ἡ μεταλαμβάνουσα), which the next sentence answers with what is in matter. |
| VI.5.11.p4 | though these, too, are omnipresent | The Greek says "yet it was present to all" (παρῆν μέντοι παντί): the immaterial is present to all matter, whatever it takes; it is not that the kinds are everywhere. |
| VI.5.12.p1 | To return : How is that Power present to the universe ? | The Greek says only "How, then, is it present?" (πάρεστιν οὖν πῶς;): "to return" and "the universe" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.12.p1 | we appeal to the character of this power | The Greek says "let him remember the power" (ἀναμνησθήτω τῆς δυνάμεως), what has been said of it in chapter 11. |
| VI.5.12.p2 | The contrary is your only way | The Greek says "the contrary would happen to you" (τοὐναντίον δ' ἄν σοι γένοιτο): instead of finding it in one place, you would find it everywhere; MacKenna makes it a way to take. |
| VI.5.12.p2 | denying it, you have strayed away to something of another order and you fall | The Greek has "giving up" (ἀπειπών), growing weary of the chase, and turning aside "to something else" (εἰς ἄλλο). |
| VI.5.12.p3 | how do you experience it | The Greek asks "how will you be persuaded of this?" (πῶς ποτε τοῦτο πείσῃ;): the question is of conviction, not of experience. |
| VI.5.12.p3 | you cease to think of yourself as under limit | The Greek says you did not say "I am so much" (τοσοῦτός εἰμι), and let go of "the so much" (τὸ τοσοῦτον). |
| VI.5.12.p4 | By the lessening of the alien in you, you increase | The Greek says "you increase yourself by letting go of the other things" (ἀφεὶς τὰ ἄλλα): the increase is the self-increasing number of chapter 9, and "the alien" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.12.p5 | It is so with the lesser gods | The Greek has "the other gods" (οἱ ἄλλοι θεοί); "lesser" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.5.12.p5 | everywhere with God and in Him | The Greek says "on him and in him" (ἐπ' αὐτοῦ καὶ ἐν αὐτῷ), "that one" (ἐκεῖνον); the name "God" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.1.p1 | It is suggested that multiplicity is a falling away from The Unity | The Greek asks a question (Ἆρ’ ἐστὶ ... ;), "Is multitude a standing away (ἀπόστασις) from the one?", which the treatise goes on to answer; "it is suggested" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.1.p1 | Given consciousness, there will be, since the thing must feel its exile | The Greek says "if it perceived, it would be" (εἰ ᾐσθάνετο, ἦν ἄν), terrible: a condition contrary to fact. Magnitude does not perceive, so nothing is lost by it; MacKenna's "there will be" makes the grief real. |
| VI.6.1.p1 | in that outward moving there is frustration or compulsion | The Greek says the journey outward is "vain or forced" (μάταιος ἢ ἀναγκαία): idle, or under necessity, as the soul's going into body may be. |
| VI.6.1.p2 | in other words, a thing is itself not by being extended but by remaining, in its degree, a unity | The Greek says it is itself "when it is in some way one, not great" (ὅταν ἀμηγέπη ἕν, μὴ μέγα, ᾖ); "remaining, in its degree" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.1.p3 | it needed Beauty to parry that magnitude | The Greek says it "needed the beautiful because it had become great" (ἐδεήθη τοῦ καλοῦ, ὅτι ἐγένετο μέγα): beauty is needed, not to parry magnitude, but to order it. |
| VI.6.1.p3 | in the degree of its extension it was void of beauty and to that degree ugly | The Greek is a condition: bare of beauty, the greater it was, "the uglier it would have shown itself" (ἂν κατεφάνη αἰσχρόν). The All is not ugly, since it has beauty. |
| VI.6.1.p3 | The greater the expansion, the greater the disorder and ugliness | The Greek says only that the great as such is "rather without order and rather ugly" (μᾶλλον ἄκοσμον ... καὶ μᾶλλον αἰσχρόν); "the greater ... the greater" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.2.p1 | Nor is an enumerator able to number to infinity | The Greek says one who counts does not count "the unlimited" (τὴν ἀπειρίαν), the unlimitedness itself, as an object: whatever he counts, he bounds. |
| VI.6.2.p2 | Are we then to dismiss absolute limitlessness and think merely that there is always something beyond ? | The Greek asks whether number is "not unlimited simply, but so that one can always take more" (ὥστε ἀεὶ ἐξεῖναι λαμβάνειν): an unlimited only in this sense, which the answer then denies. |
| VI.6.2.p2 | we multiply a non-existent in and so produce multiple numbers | The Greek has "as we multiply a town (ἄστυ) that does not exist": a city repeated in thought, as the man and the beautiful are; MacKenna leaves out the town. |
| VI.6.3.p1 | it is evil in comparison with unity pure | The Greek says it is "worse" (χεῖρον) in so far as it is compared with the one; it does not say "evil". The answer to the question is that multitude among the beings is not evil. |
| VI.6.3.p1 | multiplicity has value in so far as it tends to return to unity | The Greek says the multitude "turned back to one and stayed" (ἀνέστρεψε ... εἰς ἓν καὶ ἔμεινεν): a turning that has happened and holds, not a tendency. |
| VI.6.3.p2 | To be brought to limit it must be unlimited | The Greek says that even when it is bounded "it is unlimited by this very fact" (τούτῳ γε ἄπειρος), because only the unlimited can be bounded; MacKenna makes it a condition. |
| VI.6.3.p2 | the recoil, of course, is not from one place to another | The Greek has "it flees" (φεύγει): the unlimited runs from the form of limit as a quarry runs from the hunter; "recoil" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.3.p2 | Hence what is known as the flux of the unlimited | The Greek has "its so-called motion" (τὴν λεγομένην κίνησιν αὐτῆς); "flux" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.3.p3 | could be known only by place (which Matter precedes) | The Greek says both are "judged by the same place" (πρὸς τὸν αὐτὸν τόπον κρίνεται); "which Matter precedes" is MacKenna's, who speaks of matter where Plotinus speaks of the unlimited. |
| VI.6.3.p3 | We must fasten on the bare notion and take what that gives us | The Greek says one thinks of it "by separating the form in thought" (χωρίσας τὸ εἶδος τῇ διανοίᾳ): taking the form away, as with matter in II.4.10, not fastening on a notion. |
| VI.6.3.p4 | And there is movement in its lack of consciousness | The Greek says "its not being able to see itself through itself" (τὸ μὴ δύνασθαι δι’ αὑτῆς αὐτὴν ἰδεῖν) is motion away from intellect; "lack of consciousness" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.3.p4 | Thus it is not true to speak of Matter as being solely in flux | The Greek says only "so that one may not say that it only moves" (μὴ μόνον ... κινεῖσθαι): its subject is the unlimited (ἡ ἀπειρία) of the whole chapter; "Matter" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.4.p1 | Are they Ideas added to the other Ideas? | The Greek asks whether the numbers are "coming upon" (ἐπιγινομένων) the other forms, coming to them from outside and after, or "always following" them; MacKenna's "added" weakens the first, and "Ideas" is his word for the forms (εἴδεσιν). |
| VI.6.4.p1 | Number is a direct production of the Intellectual-Principle (an Idea in itself) | The Greek says only that number "was thought by itself" (αὐτὸς ἐφ’ ἑαυτοῦ ... ἐνοήθη): intellect is not named, nor said to produce it. |
| VI.6.4.p2 | thus making the concept depend upon variation among things | The Greek says Plato gives the thought of number "to the otherness (ἑτερότητι) of things", one of the greatest kinds; the modern version keeps "otherness", and "variation" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.4.p2 | observing pure identity unbroken by difference, it says One | The Greek says "as long as it thinks something the same (ταὐτόν) and not another after it", the soul says one; the modern version keeps "the same", and "pure identity" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.4.p2 | the veritable Number has Being, is a Being | The Greek says Plato speaks of "substance in true number, and number in substance" (ἐν τῷ ἀληθινῷ ἀριθμῷ οὐσίαν καὶ τὸν ἀριθμὸν ἐν οὐσίᾳ); the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία. |
| VI.6.5.p1 | Is it an accompaniment upon each substance | The Greek has "something that follows" (παρακολούθημα), and "as it were is seen in addition upon" (ἐπιθεωρούμενον) each substance: number would be what we see upon things over and above them. The modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία. |
| VI.6.5.p1 | in their symbolic system in which Justice, for example, is a Tetrad | The Greek says the Pythagoreans "seemed to speak of numbers from proportion" (ἐκ τοῦ ἀνὰ λόγον), by analogy; "symbolic system" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.5.p1 | This would apply in the Intellectual as in the sensible | The Greek says only "as it is there too" (ὡς κἀκεῖ οὕτως): the intelligible counts in the same way; "the sensible" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.5.p2 | But how then can number, observed upon: things, rank among Real Beings ? | The Greek asks whether there will still be "an existence" (ὑπόστασις) of number, if it is seen upon things; "rank among Real Beings" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.5.p2 | Besides, the kind of real existence thus implied annuls the reality of number | The Greek says such an existence "takes number away from being a substance" (ἀφίστησι τὸν ἀριθμὸν τοῦ οὐσίαν εἶναι) and makes it an accident; it does not annul its reality. |
| VI.6.5.p3 | the oneness being different from the manness and common to all things | The Greek says "the one is other (ἕτερον) than the man", and the one is common; "manness" is MacKenna's coinage. |
| VI.6.5.p4 | moreover if it were compounded with an object brought into being by its agency | The Greek says the one, mixed with another "that came to be through it" (τῷ γενομένῳ δι’ αὐτὸ), would make it falsely one; the compound is not brought into being by the one's agency, but the other comes to be through the one. |
| VI.6.5.p4 | The need may be like that of Form to Matter | The Greek says "if it is to give it form (εἰδοποιήσει) as if it were matter": a condition, which the decad meets only if it is first by itself; nothing is said of a need. |
| VI.6.6.p1 | Henads, Dyads, Triads | The Greek has "units" (ἑνάδες), "dyads and triads"; "Henads" is MacKenna's transliteration, and the modern version keeps "units". |
| VI.6.6.p1 | We cannot but think that some reason accounts for their origin | The Greek says "we must think that their generation is made in thought" (λόγῳ ... τὴν γένεσιν αὐτῶν ποιεῖσθαι): in the account, not in time, since there is no time there; it is not that some reason accounts for them. |
| VI.6.6.p2 | It is not that the thinking principle thought of each Idea | The Greek has "the one who thinks" (τοῦ νενοηκότος), who has thought each; "thinking principle" is MacKenna's, and the modern version keeps "forms" for the εἴδη he calls "Ideas". |
| VI.6.6.p3 | but we must not misapply that statement | The Greek says "we must take what is said in this way" (ἐκείνως χρὴ νοεῖν τὸ λεγόμενον): it is read rightly, not guarded against misuse. |
| VI.6.6.p3 | it is that the thing in the Intellectual transmutes the knowledge | The Greek says "the thing there made the knowledge ... other" (ἑτέραν ἐποίησεν εἶναι) than the knowledge of things in matter, which does not stay; "transmutes" is MacKenna's, and the modern version keeps "intelligible". |
| VI.6.6.p4 | for movement as it exists There, bound up with Being, is a concept | The Greek says "the motion there is also the thought of that one" (κἀκείνου νόησις), of being; "bound up with Being" and "a concept" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.6.p4 | Thus it is a real existent though the notion of Being is different | The Greek says "so it is substance too" (ὥστε αὖ καὶ οὐσία); the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία. |
| VI.6.6.p5 | an Intellectual manifestation self-rising, self-seen | The Greek has "like a statue (ἄγαλμα) in intellect, as if standing out of itself and shown forth in itself" (οἷον ἐξ αὑτοῦ ἑστηκὸς καὶ προφανὲν ἐν αὑτῷ); MacKenna leaves out the statue. |
| VI.6.7.p1 | This is the very nature of the Intellectual-Principle as we may know from soul which reproduces it | The Greek says "this is the nature of intellect, for soul too imitates it so" (οὕτω μιμεῖται); "as we may know" is MacKenna's, and the modern version keeps "intellect" for νοῦς. |
| VI.6.7.p1 | while that Nature itself remains indissolubly one | The Greek says nature is "together with itself" (ὁμοῦ ἑαυτῇ οὔσης), while what it begets is one thing here and another there; "indissolubly" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.7.p2 | the all-embracing Intellect sees what is in it, what is within Being | The Greek says intellect sees what is "in intellect and in substance" (ἐν τῷ νῷ καὶ τῇ οὐσίᾳ); the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία, and "all-embracing" is MacKenna's for "that has them". |
| VI.6.7.p3 | Against doubters we cite the fact of participation | The Greek says "we find assurance, for those who wonder (τεθαυμακότας), from the things that have shared": they wonder at it, not doubt it; and "the fact of participation" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.7.p3 | the longing of the rest towards soul is set up by its likeness to its higher | The Greek says the others love soul "because of such a nature", and because, by having it in some way, soul "has been made like" (ὡμοίωται) it: soul is like intellect, and the things below love it for that. |
| VI.6.7.p3 | It is surely inconceivable that any living thing be beautiful | The Greek says "it is absurd" (ἄτοπον) that a living being be beautiful while the living being itself is not; "inconceivable" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.8.p1 | there is Intellectual-Principle or Being, Authentic Being | The Greek says "there is intellect and real substance" (νοῦς ἐστι καὶ οὐσία ἡ ὄντως), both, not one as another name for the other; the modern version keeps "intellect" and "substance". |
| VI.6.8.p1 | for we ascribe an existence of their own to Absolute Man, Absolute Number, Absolute Justice | The Greek says we speak of man himself, number itself and the just itself "in another way" (ἄλλως): otherwise than of the many men and the many just things; "an existence of their own" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.8.p2 | issue of Authentic Being which is power self-existing | The Greek says true substance "has stripped these off" (ἐκδεδυκέναι ταῦτα), bulk and magnitude, and is "a power standing by itself"; life and intellect in us are not called its issue. |
| VI.6.8.p2 | and producing its effect by contact and in the ratio of the contact | The Greek says "what touches it has these" (οὗ τὸ ἐφαψάμενον ἔχει ταῦτα), life and intellect, according to the measure of touching: it is the one who touches that has them, not substance that produces an effect. |
| VI.6.8.p2 | If Being is to be sought, then most to be sought is Being at its intensest | The Greek says "if being is desired (ἐφετόν), what most is is desired more"; "at its intensest" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.8.p3 | Intellectual-Principle, as the Act of Real Being, is a second | The Greek says intellect is second because it is "the activity (ἐνέργεια) of substance"; the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία. |
| VI.6.8.p3 | nor does it rise in the Intellectual-Principle since before that there existed Real Being which is both one and numerous | The Greek says number is not "according to intellect", for before it "substance was, being one and many" (ἡ οὐσία ἓν οὖσα καὶ πολλὰ ἦν); the modern version keeps "intellect" and "substance". |
| VI.6.9.p1 | whether Being by its distinction produced Number or Number produced that distinction | The Greek asks whether "substance (οὐσία) begot number by its own dividing, or number divided substance"; the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.6.9.p1 | It is certain that either Number was the cause of Being, movement, rest, identity and difference, or these the cause of Number | The Greek asks it, and does not say "it is certain": whether these "themselves (begot) number, or number these". The modern version keeps "sameness" and "otherness" for ταὐτόν and ἕτερον, where MacKenna has "identity and difference". |
| VI.6.9.p1 | For the present we must take it that Being precedes Number, is its source | The Greek says we must "leave this aside for the present" (ἐατέον ... ἐν τῷ παρόντι), whether number is before being, "and grant" that number comes to be from being; it is granted for the argument, not taken as true, and the next sentence overturns it. |
| VI.6.9.p2 | that unity is certainly thought of as subsequent to a precedent duality | The Greek says one "surely thinks the one later, upon each" (ὕστερον δήπου τὸ ἓν νοεῖ ἐφ’ ἑκατέρῳ): the one is thought after the man and after the beautiful, not after a duality. |
| VI.6.9.p2 | with full intention from where they lie latent within itself | The Greek says "or bring them forth when they were in himself" (ἢ ἐν αὑτῷ ὄντας προφέροι), and "not as it happened" (μὴ κατὰ τὸ ἐπελθόν); "lie latent" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.9.p2 | counting myself there are three | The Greek has "and make another with me" (καὶ μετ’ ἐμοῦ καὶ ἄλλο ποιητέον): the maker goes on counting, with himself; "three" is MacKenna's reckoning. |
| VI.6.9.p3 | for Being was still unparted | The Greek says "being was still one" (ἓν γὰρ ἦν ἔτι τὸ ὄν); "unparted" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.9.p3 | Clearly Being is to be thought of as Number Collective | The Greek asks it: "Is being, then, number united (ἡνωμένος)?" (ἆρ’ οὖν ...;); "clearly" is MacKenna's, and "united" is not "collective". |
| VI.6.9.p3 | while the Living-Form is Number container of the universe | The Greek says the living being is "number that holds" (ἀριθμὸς περιέχων); "of the universe" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.9.p3 | Hence it is that the Forms have been described as Henads and Numbers | The Greek says "they called the forms both units (ἑνάδας) and numbers"; the modern version keeps "units". |
| VI.6.9.p4 | This is the authentic Number; the other, the ‘‘ monadic ” is its image | The Greek calls it "the substantial number" (ὁ οὐσιώδης ἀριθμός), and the other the number "of units" (ὁ μοναδικός), the number we count with; "authentic" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.9.p4 | serving to them as root, fount, first principle | The Greek says the beings have in it "their base and spring and root and principle" (βάσιν ... καὶ πηγὴν καὶ ῥίζαν καὶ ἀρχήν): MacKenna leaves out the base. |
| VI.6.10.p1 | When it takes lot with multiplicity, Being becomes Number by the fact of awakening to manifoldness | The Greek says being "standing in multitude is number, when it was roused to be much" (ὅτε πολὺ μὲν ἠγείρετο): number is being as it stands in multitude, and "takes lot with" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.10.p1 | a total of henads offering a stay for what was to be based upon them | The Greek has "units holding a place" (ἑνάδες τόπον ἔχουσαι) for what was to be set on them; the modern version keeps "units", and "a stay" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.10.p2 | Number then preexists and is the cause by which produced things participate in quantity | The Greek says the things that came to be shared in "being so many" (τοῦ τοσαῦτα), not in quantity in general; "quantity" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.10.p2 | a unity is a unity in virtue of Being | The Greek says each is "one from the one" (ἓν δὲ παρ’ αὐτοῦ ἕν), as it is being from being; MacKenna makes unity come from being. |
| VI.6.10.p3 | it is he that produces the number | The Greek says the ten thousand are not called out by the things but by "his thought saying so many" (τῆς διανοίας λεγούσης τοσαῦτα); he does not produce the number, but says it, as one who knows how to count. |
| VI.6.10.p3 | that that Principle should not know its own total content is absurd, impossible | The Greek has "that nature" (τὴν φύσιν ἐκείνην), the intelligible, not knowing "how great its multitude is"; "Principle" and "total content" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.10.p4 | the goodness we have in mind is that First Hypostasis | The Greek says "if he speaks of the first things, he speaks of the first existence" (ὑπόστασιν ... τὴν πρώτην): the good of the first things is their first existence, not a hypostasis set over them; the capitals are MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.10.p5 | the characteristic Act of that subject | The Greek says it "is its activity" (ἐνέργειά ἐστιν ἐκείνου); "characteristic" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.10.p5 | then, if the subject is a Real Being so also is the accidental in an equal degree | The Greek says "if that thing is a substance (οὐσία), it too is a substance, and that thing is no more substance than it"; the modern version keeps "substance", and Plotinus does not call it an accident here. |
| VI.6.11.p1 | We are certainly not compelled to attach that one henad te some one thing | The Greek says the one unit "need not be yoked (συνεζεῦχθαι) to some one of the beings"; the modern version keeps "units" for ἑνάδες, where MacKenna has "henads". |
| VI.6.11.p1 | the principle whose existence within itself we affirmed to be presupposed by its manifestation outside | The Greek says this one nature "must exist by itself even before it is seen in many" (πρὸ τοῦ ἐν πολλοῖς θεωρεῖσθαι), as chapter 5 said; "manifestation outside" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.11.p2 | or else Number becomes a collection of unlike monads and there are differences among monads (an impossibility) | The Greek says number would be "of unlike monads" and there would be "differences of the monads in so far as they are monads" (καθ’ ὅσον μονάδες); "(an impossibility)" is MacKenna's, though the next sentence counts it among the impossible things. |
| VI.6.11.p2 | there must exist a unity bare, unrelated by very essence | The Greek says the one is "bare" (ψιλόν), "left bare (ἀπηρημωμένον) of being by its own substance": a one that is nothing but one; "unrelated" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.11.p3 | Each particular, considered in itself, would be a manifold of monads, totalling to a collective unity | The Greek says only "each apart (χωρὶς), there are many monads, which are also many ones"; "totalling to a collective unity" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.11.p3 | something outside, at once, of both Number and Reason | The Greek says it would be "without number and without forming principle" (ἀνάριθμον καὶ ἄλογον); the modern version keeps "forming principle" for λόγος, and "Reason" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.12.p1 | so that all comes to an attitude of the mind towards things considered singly | The Greek says the one is "an affection of the soul" (πάθημα ... τῆς ψυχῆς) toward each of the beings: something the soul undergoes, and the modern version keeps "soul" for ψυχή, where MacKenna has "mind". |
| VI.6.12.p1 | something latent in our minds independently of any concrete one thing | The Greek says the one "is in the soul (ἐν ψυχῇ) without its being some one thing"; "latent" and "minds" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.12.p2 | [An objector speaks :—] | The Greek gives the objection without naming a speaker; the brackets are MacKenna's, and the modern version marks it only with quotation marks. |
| VI.6.12.p2 | Those that take the notion of numbers and of unity to be but one species of the notions held to be inherent in the mind | The Greek speaks of "what they call concepts" (τῶν λεγομένων παρ’ αὐτοῖς ἐννοημάτων), the Stoics' word; "inherent in the mind" is MacKenna's, and the Stoics held that concepts are not in the mind from birth. |
| VI.6.12.p3 | and have no significance outside of this sphere | The Greek says one thing is in that part "of the All" (τοῦ παντός) we call above, and another in what is called below; "no significance outside of this sphere" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.12.p3 | there does exist an actuality implicit in each one of the relations cited | The Greek says there is "some existence" (ὑπόστασίς τις) of each of the things spoken of; "actuality" is MacKenna's, and "relations" is his word for them. |
| VI.6.13.p1 | a presentation of that order is something very different from unity (which must be a thing of the Intellectual) | The Greek says only that "what appeared is one thing, and the one is another" (ἄλλου μὲν ὄντος τοῦ φανέντος, ἄλλου δὲ ... τοῦ ἕν); the bracketed words are MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.13.p1 | the mind does not affirm in some caprice but from observation of contrasted position | The Greek says thought "is not moved idly" (οὐ μάτην κινουμένη), but, "seeing a different position", says "here"; "caprice" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.13.p1 | Besides Otherness and Difference are later | The Greek has "the other and the different" (τὸ ἄλλο καὶ τὸ ἕτερον), the words just used, "another" and "not another"; "otherness" belongs to ἑτερότης, the kind, and the capitals are MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.13.p2 | the object is a unity before any outside affirmation or conception | The Greek says what it speaks of "is one before anyone says or thinks anything about it" (πρὶν εἰπεῖν ἢ νοῆσαί τινα περὶ αὐτοῦ); "outside" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.13.p2 | In refusing to allow the manifold to remain manifold, the mind makes the truth clear | The Greek says "if thought does not let it be a multitude, though it is (one), plainly here too it does" one of two things: it gives the one, or sees it in the order; the clearness is in the argument, not truth made clear by the mind. |
| VI.6.13.p3 | something which is a Nature or Principle of Unity | The Greek says the one is "a nature that is and exists" (ὄντος τινὸς φύσεως τοῦ ἑνὸς καὶ ὑφεστώσης); "Principle of Unity" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.13.p3 | we must similarly admit that Unity exists under all forms though still by reference, only, to that primal Unity | The Greek says the one is "in all the ways" (κατὰ πάντας τοὺς τρόπους), but "in reference to one" (εἰς ἀναφορὰν ... ἑνός); "that primal Unity" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.13.p4 | The mind, from a thing present to it, comes to knowledge of something else | The Greek says thought "from one thing thinks another, which it does not see" (ἀπ’ ἄλλου ἄλλο νοεῖ, ὃ οὐχ ὁρᾷ); "comes to knowledge" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.13.p5 | Unity must precede Reality and be its author | The Greek says it would be "before substance, and begetting substance" (πρὸ οὐσίας ... καὶ γεννῶν τὴν οὐσίαν); the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.6.13.p5 | It is, therefore, an existent Unity, not an existent that develops Unity | The Greek says "it is one being, but not being and then one" (ἓν ὄν, ἀλλ’ οὐκ ὄν, εἶτα ἕν): the one comes first in the name; "develops" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.13.p6 | the This shows a thing present, as much as if we used the strict name of the object | The Greek says "there is a thing underlying" (ἔστι πρᾶγμα ὑποκείμενον), as if one spoke "its own name" (τὸ ἴδιον αὐτοῦ ... ὄνομα); "strict" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.14.p1 | The Unity is not of a nature to lose its own manner of being only because something else stands in a state which it does not itself share | The Greek says the one does not lose its nature "when another is affected (ἄλλου παθόντος) and it is affected in nothing"; to go out of one it must itself undergo "the privation of the one" (τὴν τοῦ ἑνὸς στέρησιν). The modern version keeps "privation". |
| VI.6.14.p1 | must be classed among Real-Beings, regardless of position | The Greek asks "how shall we not rank it among the beings, wherever it is?" (ὅπου ἂν ᾖ); "regardless of position" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.14.p1 | especially in the First in its aspect of Unity developing into Being | The Greek says it is by itself in the intelligible "in the first, when it is one, and then being" (τῷ πρώτῳ, ὅταν ἕν, εἶτα ὄν): the first of the intelligibles, which is one before it is being; "developing" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.14.p2 | If one configuration produces a certain thing it is impossible that the opposite should produce the same | The Greek has "when it is a relation (σχέσις) that begets something, the contrary relation cannot beget the same"; "configuration" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.14.p3 | Unity is due to the presence of Unity ; duality to that of Duality | The Greek says a thing is "one by the presence (παρουσίᾳ) of the one, and two by the dyad": the language of the Phaedo; the capitals are MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.14.p3 | Beauty from a certain pattern of the person | The Greek says a thing would be beautiful "because we are disposed so" (ὅτι οὕτω διατιθέμεθα): the beauty would be in our being affected, not in a pattern of the person. |
| VI.6.14.p4 | that thing possesses also size, form, and a host of other characteristics | The Greek says it is "great too, and beautiful" (καὶ μέγα καὶ καλόν), and ten thousand things could be said of it; "form" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.14.p4 | quantity (Number) has not | The Greek has only "quantity" (ποσότης); the bracketed "(Number)" is MacKenna's gloss, though the argument is about the discrete quantity, which is number. |
| VI.6.14.p5 | in the looser totals is different from its presence in the continuous | The Greek has "in things that are divided" (ἐν ... τοῖς διῃρημένοις), things apart from one another; "looser totals" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.14.p5 | Dekad-Absolute not Dekad of some particular Intellectual group | The Greek has "the decad itself (αὐτοδεκάδα), not a decad of some intelligibles"; the modern version keeps "intelligible". |
| VI.6.15.p1 | for the sense-nature as such cannot compass that transcendental unity | The Greek says "the nature of the perceptible fled (ἔφυγε) the one there, if it was to be perceptible": the perceptible had to escape the one to be perceptible at all; "transcendental" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.15.p1 | thus that Living-All is inevitably Number-Entire | The Greek says "it", the whole true being of the first sentence, "must be all number" (ἀριθμὸν δὴ δεῖ αὐτὸ εἶναι σύμπαντα), not the living All, this world, which only imitates it. |
| VI.6.15.p2 | and man is There also, in the Intellectual, in so far as he is intellect | The Greek says "in intellect, in so far as it is intellect, all the intellects are, each, as parts" (ὡς μὲν μέρη οἱ νοῖ πάντες καθέκαστον); it is the many intellects that are in intellect, not man as intellect. |
| VI.6.15.p2 | its presence There is the reckoning of the Acts of Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says number is in intellect "as are all the activities of intellect" (ὅσα νοῦ ἐνέργειαι), among them, not as their reckoning. |
| VI.6.15.p2 | its moral wisdom, its virtues, its knowledge | The Greek has σωφροσύνη, "self-control", and "the other virtues" (αἱ ἄλλαι ἀρεταί); "moral wisdom" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.15.p3 | where these virtues are mainly potential becoming actual by the intention towards Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says they are actual "when it turns toward intellect and is with it" (ὅταν πρὸς νοῦν καὶ συνῇ); "intention" is MacKenna's, and the modern version keeps "virtues" and "intellect". |
| VI.6.15.p4 | Next we come to Being, fully realised, and this is the seat of Number | The Greek says "after this, then, being, and in it number" (μετὰ δὲ τοῦτο ἤδη τὸ ὂν καὶ ἐν τούτῳ ὁ ἀριθμός); "fully realised" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.15.p4 | its movement is planned to Number | The Greek says being begets the beings "moving according to number" (κινούμενον κατ’ ἀριθμόν); "planned" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.15.p5 | to suppose another number than the actual is to suppose the production of something else or of nothing | The Greek says that if something "takes another number" (ἄλλον ἀριθμὸν λάβῃ), it begets another thing or comes to be nothing: a thing, not a supposition, takes the number. |
| VI.6.15.p5 | These then are the primal numbers; they are numerable | The Greek says they are the first numbers "as numbered" (ὡς ἀριθμητοί): numbers that are counted, the things numbered, not numbers that count; the others both count and are counted. |
| VI.6.16.p1 | but you go on to say that there are the numbers belonging to the Firsts and then talk of other numbers quite distinct, those of reckoning | The Greek says these numbers "belong to the first beings" (πρώτων ὄντων), and there are other numbers "which count" (ἀριθμοῦντας); "reckoning" is MacKenna's, and "the Firsts" with a capital makes them the gods of VI.5. |
| VI.6.16.p1 | or is quantity here just so many units brought together | The Greek asks whether "the one is many times quantity" (πολλάκις μὲν τὸ ἓν ποσόν): the one taken many times makes quantity, while the one itself is only its principle; "brought together" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.16.p2 | the number affirmed is not a Reality, even as Reality goes in the sphere of sense | The Greek says this number is "not a substance (οὐσία), not even as (there is) in perceptible things"; the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.6.16.p2 | since the single individual is not a unity absolute | The Greek says "one of the ten is not one by itself" (οὐχ ἓν καθ’ αὑτό); "individual" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.16.p3 | in that he is at once living and reasoning | The Greek has "living being and rational" (ζῷον καὶ λογικόν), the two parts of the old definition of man, not two things he does. |
| VI.6.16.p3 | even the duality so formed is no posterior | The Greek says this dyad is "not later" (οὐχ ὕστερον) than the thing, and does not "only say how much from outside the thing"; it is not formed by our counting, as "so formed" suggests. |
| VI.6.16.p4 | in the ten of your reckoning there cannot be a dekad without a unitary basis | The Greek says in the ten you count, "not ranked together into one" (μὴ συντεταγμένοις εἰς ἕν), "it would not even be called a decad"; "unitary basis" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.16.p4 | is either an Act of these inherent numbers or an Act in accordance with them | The Greek says it is "an activity (ἐνέργεια), either of those numbers or according to them": the modern version keeps "activity". |
| VI.6.16.p5 | It is the Number of our essence | The Greek says "the number of our substance" (ὁ τῆς οὐσίας ἡμῶν); the modern version keeps "substance", where MacKenna has "essence". |
| VI.6.16.p5 | soul, then, is Number since it is essence | The Greek says "so the soul is number, if (εἴπερ) it is substance": a condition, and "substance" again. |
| VI.6.16.p6 | But when you declare virtue to be a Tetrad, you are affirming a Tetrad which does actually exist | The Greek says "it is also some tetrad" (καὶ τετράς ἐστί τις), as its parts are into one: the virtue is four in its substance, as man is two; the modern version keeps "virtue" for ἀρετή. |
