| VI.6.17.p1 | does not all this reasoning set it under limit ? | The Greek says "these arguments give it a limit" (πέρας ... διδόασιν), as a statement, not a question. |
| VI.6.17.p1 | that of the (path of the) universe | The Greek has only "the line of the All" (τοῦ παντός), the greatest line there is, its diameter; "(path of the)" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.17.p1 | though not of course to produce the doubled quantity | The Greek says one doubles it in thought "not joining it to that" (οὐκ ἐκείνῳ συνάψαντα), the number known: the double is not added to the things. |
| VI.6.17.p2 | It is our view that there does exist an infinite line, among the Intellectual Beings | The Greek asks it, "or shall we say (ἢ φήσομεν) that there is an unlimited line in the intelligibles?", and at once objects; it is not given as Plotinus' view. |
| VI.6.17.p2 | for There a line would not be quantitative | The Greek says the contrary: "the line there would be of some quantity" (ποσὴ γὰρ ἂν εἴη ἡ ἐκεῖ γραμμή), which is the objection; then "if it is not of some quantity", it would be unlimited in number. MacKenna reverses the first clause. |
| VI.6.17.p2 | In that the conception of the Absolute Line does not include the conception of limit | The Greek says "in the account (λόγῳ) of the line itself there is no limit thought in with it": the definition of a line, "length without breadth", sets no bound on its length. |
| VI.6.17.p3 | There is a position, as well as a manner of being, for all configurations | The Greek asks "where and how" (ποῦ καὶ ὅπως) the figures are, the question of the line put again; "position" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.17.p3 | the configurations of the universe are obviously antecedent to ourselves | The Greek has "the figure of the All" (τὸ τοῦ παντὸς σχῆμα), one figure, the sphere, as a witness that the figures are not our making. |
| VI.6.17.p3 | and so of all the rest as we read in the account of the realm of sense | The Greek says "and the others in proportion (ἀνὰ λόγον), as is said about the things here": the other elements have their figures as fire has its pyramid. |
| VI.6.17.p4 | But does the Life-Form contain the configurations by the mere fact of its life ? | The Greek asks whether they are in the living being "in so far as it is a living being" (καθ’ ὃ ζῷον); "by the mere fact of its life" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.17.p4 | the Act of the Intellectual-Principle possesses itself of bare sphere | The Greek says "thinking (ἡ νόησις) has the bare sphere": the modern version keeps "thinking", and "the Act of the Intellectual-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p1 | no number has been or could be omitted to make addition possible | The Greek says no number "was left out (ἐλήφθη) nor will be" so that one might be added: none was taken out, as one takes from a heap. |
| VI.6.18.p1 | its manner of being is settled for it by itself alone | The Greek says it is "by itself what it is" (ἑαυτῷ ὢν ὃς ἔστι), bounded by itself and not by a limit; "settled" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p1 | There every being is Measure | The Greek says "those things are all measures" (ἐκεῖνα δὲ πάντα μέτρα), many measures, not one capitalized Measure. |
| VI.6.18.p2 | a life not tainted towards death | The Greek says its life is not "mixed with death" (πρὸς θάνατον συμμιγῆ); "tainted" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p2 | Nor is the life of that Absolute Living-Form some feeble flickering | The Greek has "not feeble" (ἀμενηνή), as in chapter 8; "flickering" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p2 | and close above it stands the wisdom of all, the collective Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says the wisdom of all and the whole intellect are "upon it and with it and together with it" (ἐπὼν καὶ συνὼν καὶ ὁμοῦ ὤν); the modern version keeps "wisdom" for φρόνησις and "intellect" for νοῦς, and the dots before these words in MacKenna's text are his. |
| VI.6.18.p3 | Here attention is set for the most part upon the unliving | The Greek has "our striking upon things" (ἡ προσβολή), the impression things make on us; "attention" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p3 | once you have known the Essence that pervades them | The Greek has "the substance (οὐσία) that has gone through them"; the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.6.18.p3 | you can but smile at all the lower nature with its pretention to Reality | The Greek says "you will laugh" (γελάσει) at its pretence "to substance" (εἰς οὐσίαν); the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.6.18.p4 | In virtue of this Essence it is that life endures | The Greek says "from it (παρὰ ... ταύτης) life stays", from substance; "Essence" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p4 | nothing, indeed, is in being beside it to touch it | The Greek says "there is no being after it" (μετ’ αὐτό) that will touch it; "beside" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p4 | Being itself could not make such an opposite into Being | The Greek says the contrary, if it were a being, "would not have made this being" (οὐκ ἂν τοῦτο ἐποίησεν ὄν), but something common before both would be being: it is the contrary, not being, that would fail to make. |
| VI.6.18.p4 | Being is thus beyond contact not because it stands alone but because it is Being | The Greek says "unaffected" (ἀπαθές), as before; "beyond contact" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p5 | As long as it exists it produces | The Greek says "as long as it is, it supplies" (χορηγεῖ), the word for the leader who pays for a chorus; "produces" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p5 | and rejoicing to hold their trace of it and through that to seek their good | The Greek says they love it, "and after this seek the good" (μετὰ τοῦτο τἀγαθὸν ζητεῖν): being is loved first, the good sought after; "through that" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.6.18.p5 | all this world desires life and wisdom in order to Being | The Greek says this world wishes to live "and be wise" (φρονεῖν) so that it may be; the modern version keeps "wise". |
| VI.7.1.p1 | placed eyes in the face to catch the light | The Greek has "light-bearing eyes" (φωσφόρα ... ὄμματα), the Timaeus' word: eyes that bring light out, not eyes that catch it. |
| VI.7.1.p1 | providing thus for the safety which comes by seeing and hearing in time | The Greek has "if it saw ahead and heard ahead" (εἰ προορῷτο καὶ προακούοι), the same prefix as the god's foreseeing (προορώμενος): the senses are the living being's own foresight. |
| VI.7.1.p1 | But what led to this provision ? | The Greek asks "from what (πόθεν) did he foresee these things?": the source of the foresight, which the chapter goes on to seek, not what led to the provision. |
| VI.7.1.p2 | it is of their very nature to belong to process | The Greek says "going into becoming is inborn (σύμφυτον) in them": a going, not a belonging; and "process" is MacKenna's for γένεσις, "becoming". |
| VI.7.1.p2 | unnatural to them to be outside of process and within the Intellectual | The Greek has "in the intelligible" (ἐν τῷ νοητῷ); the modern version keeps "intelligible", and "the Intellectual" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.1.p2 | the divine provision would consist in holding them to their disaster | The Greek says providence is "so that they might be kept safe in evil" (ἵνα σώζοιντο ἐν τῷ κακῷ): the providence saves them in it, as the senses do, and does not hold them to it. |
| VI.7.1.p2 | this is God’s reasoned purpose, this the plan entire | The Greek says "this is god's reasoning, and reasoning altogether" (ὁ λογισμὸς ὁ τοῦ θεοῦ οὗτος καὶ ὅλως λογισμός): the objection ends by making god reason, which the chapter will deny; "purpose" and "plan" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.1.p3 | But sense-perception it cannot in this case be | The Greek says "there was not yet (οὔπω) perception": the senses had not yet been given, which is the point; "it cannot be" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.1.p3 | whether over living beings or over any part of the universe | The Greek says "nor that of this All altogether" (οὔθ’ ὅλως τοῦδε τοῦ παντός): the providence of the whole All, not of any part of it. |
| VI.7.1.p4 | we speak of reasoned purpose in the world of things only to convey that the universe is of the character which in the later order would point to a wise purposing | The Greek says all is "as a wise man (σοφός) would have it from reasoning in the things that come later": the reasoner is a wise man, and "the universe" and "purposing" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.1.p4 | Providence implies that things are as in the later order a competent foreplanning would produce them | The Greek has προόρασις, "foresight", not πρόνοια, "providence"; and it says again "as a wise man would foresee", where MacKenna has "a competent foreplanning would produce". |
| VI.7.1.p4 | The alone and one and utterly simplex cannot involve | The Greek asks how what is alone and one "holds, unfolded" (ἔχει ἀναπτυσσόμενον) "this, so that not that": the unfolding, which the chapter's end takes up again, is not in MacKenna. |
| VI.7.1.p4 | the serviceable thing appeared and at once approved itself so | The Greek has "this appeared useful, and this, once it came to be, saving" (σωτήριον): the safety of the chapter's first sentence; MacKenna leaves out the saving. |
| VI.7.1.p5 | But surely this is foreseeing, deliberating | The Greek is an objector's conclusion, not a question: "he foresaw, then, and reasoned beforehand" (προείδετο ἄρα καὶ προελογίσατο ἄρα). |
| VI.7.1.p6 | No: all turns on the necessary completeness of Act | The Greek says "if each activity (ἑκάστην ἐνέργειαν) must not be incomplete": each activity, not Act in general; and "No" is MacKenna's, where the Greek has "yet" (οὐ μὴν ἀλλ’). |
| VI.7.1.p6 | the All, in its extension and so to speak unfolding, is able to present succession while yet it is simultaneous | The Greek names no subject: what is stretched out and unfolded is what is in god, the all things; "the All" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.1.p6 | this is because it contains the cause of all as inherent to itself | The Greek says "it holds in itself its cause too" (ἔχον ἐν ἑαυτῷ καὶ τὴν αἰτίαν): each thing its own cause, the theme of chapter 2; "the cause of all" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.2.p1 | We know that it exists but its cause we do not see | The Greek says "we grant that it has the that (ὅτι), but no longer the why (διότι)": what we grant intellect holds, the that and the why of things, not our knowing that intellect exists. |
| VI.7.2.p1 | but this is an image or part of an image | The Greek says "as a statue (ἄγαλμα) or a statue's part": the word for an image of a god set up to be seen; "image" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.2.p1 | In the Intellectual all is at one | The Greek says "there" (ἐκεῖ); "the Intellectual" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.2.p2 | and this cause being the essence of the thing | The Greek has "the substance (οὐσίαν) of each"; the modern version keeps "substance", and "essence" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.2.p2 | by this search after the cause the thing’s essence is reached | The Greek has "the what it was to be" (τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι), Aristotle's phrase, grasped rightly by those who try in this way. |
| VI.7.2.p2 | I am not here saying that the informing Idea is the cause of the thing | The Greek has "the form" (τὸ εἶδος); "the informing Idea" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.2.p3 | A thing of inactivity, even though alive, cannot include its own cause | The Greek says that if it were idle (ἀργόν) it would "not altogether" (οὐ πάντως) have the why; "cannot" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.2.p3 | where could a Forming-Idea, a member of the Intellectual-Principle, turn in quest of its cause? | The Greek has "being a form (εἶδος), and intellect's (νοῦ)"; the modern version keeps "form" and "intellect", and "Forming-Idea" and "Intellectual-Principle" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.2.p3 | it is an integral and so includes the excellence bound up with the cause | The Greek says it has "the well (τὸ καλῶς) of its cause": that it is as it should be, which the cause gives with it; "excellence" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.2.p4 | must all the singles exist for the whole and each for itself | The Greek has "toward" (πρὸς τὸ ὅλον ... πρὸς αὑτό): a holding toward the whole and toward itself, not existing for them as for a purpose. |
| VI.7.2.p4 | uncaused, independent and standing apart from cause, they must be self-contained, cause and all | The Greek is a condition: "if, then, they have no cause of their being" (εἰ οὖν μὴ ἔχει αἰτίαν τοῦ εἶναι), they would hold the cause in themselves; MacKenna makes it an assertion. |
| VI.7.2.p5 | not a cause but a fact of the being | The Greek says "not being a why (διότι), but a that (ὅτι)": the pair the chapter began with, now one. |
| VI.7.2.p5 | Take for example the Idea of Man; Man entire is found to contribute to it | The Greek says the whole man "appeared at once, bringing himself together with himself" (συμφέρων ἑαυτὸν αὐτῷ): he brings himself, and does not contribute to an Idea; "the Idea of Man" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.2.p5 | the man born is the derivative | The Greek says "the man who comes to be is a thing come to be" (γενητός): generated, as opposed to the man who always is; "derivative" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.3.p1 | insist on the eternity of the process and planning falls to the ground | The Greek says the account itself, by showing things "always coming to be" (ἀεὶ γινόμενα), took away that he reasons: it is the Timaeus' own account that takes the reasoning away, not our insisting. |
| VI.7.3.p1 | things stood ill at first ; if they stood well, so they must remain | The Greek says "they were not beautiful (καλά) before; but if they were beautiful, they keep the same": the beautiful, which the next paragraph takes up; "ill" and "well" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.3.p2 | Only in conjunction with their causes are things good; even in this sphere a thing is good in virtue of being complete | The Greek has "beautiful" (καλά, καλόν τι) both times: things are beautiful with their cause, and beautiful because they are all; "good" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.3.p2 | you affirm an indwelling safeguard of the essence | The Greek has "of the substance (τῆς οὐσίας)"; the modern version keeps "substance", and "essence" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.3.p2 | the being, the essence, the cause, all are one | The Greek says "the substance (ἡ οὐσία) and the what it was to be (τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι) and the why (τὸ διότι) are one": Aristotle's essence is the second of the three, and MacKenna's "the being" stands for the first. |
| VI.7.3.p3 | sense-perception—even in its particular modes—is involved in the Idea | The Greek says "being perceptive, and perceptive in this way (οὕτως)", is set "in the form" (ἐν τῷ εἴδει); "even in its particular modes" and "the Idea" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.3.p3 | thus Man in the Intellectual was not purely intellect, sense-perception being an addition made upon his entry into birth | The Greek denies both together: "it is not that the man there was intellect only, and the perceptive was added (προσετέθη) when he was sent into becoming". MacKenna keeps the addition as a fact, which the Greek denies. |
| VI.7.4.p2 | whether man as here is a Reason-Principle different to that soul | The Greek has λόγος; the modern version keeps "forming principle", and "Reason-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.4.p2 | or, again, the (yet lower) soul using the human body | The Greek says only "the soul that uses a body of this kind" (ἡ τῷ σώματι τῷ τοιῷδε ψυχὴ προσχρωμένη); "(yet lower)" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.4.p3 | since what is indicated is not the Idea that is to enter Matter but only that of the known thing, the conjoint | The Greek says it is "not of the form in matter" (εἴδους ... τοῦ ἐνύλου), the form already in matter, "but shows the compound, which already is"; "that is to enter" and "the known thing" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.4.p4 | This does not define the principle of either. | The Greek says he "does not think fit to say that according to which each thing is" (καθ’ ὅ ἐστιν ἕκαστον): he leaves out what makes each thing what it is; "either" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.4.p4 | a complete definition must cover the essential manner of being | The Greek has "those who think fit to define the what it was to be (τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι) for each thing, when they define in the strict sense (κυρίως)": Aristotle's school, named by its phrase. |
| VI.7.4.p5 | What, then, is this essential of Man? | The Greek has "being for man" (τὸ εἶναι ἀνθρώπῳ), Aristotle's phrase for the essence of a kind; MacKenna's "this essential" loses the phrase. |
| VI.7.4.p5 | and what is it apart from that act of making ? | The Greek asks only "what is it, itself?" (τίς ὢν αὐτός;); "apart from that act of making" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.4.p5 | and man therefore is not an essence but simply an activity of the soul | The Greek has "not a substance (οὐσία)"; the modern version keeps "substance", and "essence" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.5.p1 | Man, thus, must be some Reason-Principle other than soul. | The Greek has λόγος; the modern version keeps "forming principle", and "Reason-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.5.p1 | there is nothing surprising in such essences being Reason-Principles | The Greek has "such substances (οὐσίας)"; the modern version keeps "substance", and "essences" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.5.p2 | just as an artist may make a reduced image of that again | The Greek has "a painter" (ζωγράφος), who will make of this man "a still lesser man": the image of an image. |
| VI.7.5.p2 | It is soul, then, that holds the pattern and Reason-Principles of Man | The Greek has "the shape" (τὴν μορφήν); the modern version keeps "pattern" for παράδειγμα, and "Reason-Principles" is MacKenna's for the forming principles. |
| VI.7.5.p3 | This must be the Man of Plato’s definition | The Greek says "it would be (εἴη ἂν) this one that Plato defined": a likely reading of Plato, not a certainty; and MacKenna's quotation "Man is Soul" is his own. |
| VI.7.5.p3 | the soul, poised above, which touches body only through that intermediary | The Greek says it "rides upon" (ἐποχεῖται) the soul that uses body first, as on a vehicle, and uses it "second" (δευτέρως); "poised above" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.5.p3 | the higher soul enters to bestow a brighter life | The Greek says this soul "followed" (ἐπηκολούθησεν), and then corrects it: it did not even follow, but added itself. "Enters" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.5.p3 | for soul does not leave the Intellectual | The Greek has "the intelligible" (τοῦ νοητοῦ); the modern version keeps "intelligible", and "the Intellectual" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.5.p3 | blending with it by the natural link of Reason-Principle to Reason-Principle | The Greek says "mingling itself, forming principle with forming principle" (λόγῳ πρὸς λόγον); "natural link" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.6.p1 | it is the source of the soul’s perception of the sense-realm in its correspondence with the Intellectual | The Greek says "this is why it perceives the perceptible harmony (ἁρμονίαν) too in this way": the harmony, which the perceptive man fits to the harmony there, is not in MacKenna. |
| VI.7.6.p1 | proceeding from the fire Intellectual to the fire here | The Greek says the perceptive man "fits fire to the fire there" (πῦρ ἐναρμόσαντος πρὸς τὸ ἐκεῖ πῦρ): from the fire here to the fire there, the other way. |
| VI.7.6.p2 | not in the sense of actually living by them all but as standing in under-parallel to them | The Greek says the last man has them all "not becoming (γινόμενος) them, but lying beside (παρακείμενος) them"; "actually living by them" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.6.p2 | in yet another there is a blending of the third grade with the others | The Greek says another "has activity from the third as well" (παρὰ τοῦ τρίτου ἡ ἐνέργεια); there is no blending. |
| VI.7.6.p3 | No doubt it seems strange that a soul which has been the Reason-Principle of a man | The Greek has λόγος; the modern version keeps "forming principle", and "Reason-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.6.p3 | and will at different times be this and that | The Greek says it "is active (ἐνεργεῖ) at different times by different ones": it was all things, and is active by one or another; MacKenna's "be" loses the activity. |
| VI.7.6.p4 | It produces also the still loftier beings, the Celestials (Daimons) | The Greek has "daemons" (δαίμονας); the modern version keeps "daemon", and "the Celestials" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.6.p4 | higher still stands that Man more entirely of the Celestial rank, almost a god | The Greek says "rather a god" (μᾶλλον δὲ θεός): more truly a god than a daemon, not almost one. |
| VI.7.6.p4 | For that Being into which man develops is not to be called a god | The Greek says "that on which (εἰς ὃν) man hangs is not called a god", answering the daemon "hanging on" (ἀνηρτημένος) a god; nothing develops into anything. |
| VI.7.6.p5 | chooses animal nature and descends to that | The Greek says it "follows along" (συνέπηται), joined to the soul it was joined to as a man; "descends" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.7.p1 | it is like a skilful craftsman competent to create all kinds of works of art but reduced to making what is ordered | The Greek says craftsmen (δημιουργοί) who know how to make many forms "then make this one, either what they were ordered to, or what the matter wants": "reduced" is MacKenna's, and the craftsmen are many. |
| VI.7.7.p1 | the reason-principle of the animal, and the animal itself, must lie outside of the natural plan ? | The Greek says they are "against nature" (παρὰ φύσιν), as the answer repeats ("not against nature"); "the natural plan" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.7.p2 | The power of the All-Soul, as Reason-Principle of the universe, may be considered as laying down a pattern | The Greek asks a question, "what prevents ... from sketching beforehand (προυπογράφειν)?": an outline drawn before the painting, not a pattern; and the soul of the All is "the whole forming principle" (λόγον πάντα οὖσαν). |
| VI.7.7.p2 | this plan would be something like a tentative illumining of Matter | The Greek has "illuminations running ahead" (προδρόμους ἐλλάμψεις) into matter, like runners going before; "tentative" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.7.p2 | the elaborating soul would give minute articulation to these representations of itself | The Greek says the soul follows "such traces" (ἴχνεσιν) and articulates "the traces part by part"; "representations of itself" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.7.p3 | But this is to anticipate | The Greek says "drawn on (ἐπισπώμενοι) by what follows, we came to this": a digression the argument led to, not an anticipation. |
| VI.7.7.p3 | the Intellectual in him is linked to the Intellectual | The Greek says "these perceptibles are yoked (συνεζεῦχθαι) to this man, and those to that one": the two men and their two kinds of perceptibles; "the Intellectual" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.7.p4 | so distinguished as dealing with corporeal objects | MacKenna takes "because it was of bodies" (ὅτι σωμάτων ἦν) with the perception here; in the Greek it goes with the apprehension there, which "we called perception because it was of bodies", the bodies there. |
| VI.7.7.p4 | perceptions here are Intellections of the dimmer order | The Greek says "these perceptions are dim thoughts (ἀμυδρὰς νοήσεις)": dim, as the images in chapter 5 are dim, not of an order. |
| VI.7.8.p1 | the universal horse, must look deliberately towards this sphere | The Greek asks "how does it not want (ἐθέλει) to look toward the things here?" of "the whole horse" (ἵππος ὅλος): a question, not an assertion; "universal" and "deliberately" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.8.p1 | Yet what was that there to present the idea of the horse it was desired to produce? | The Greek asks "how was it possible, wanting to make a horse, to think a horse?": the thought of the horse must be there already, before any wanting. |
| VI.7.8.p1 | the reproduction followed of necessity since the total of things was not to halt at the Intellectual | The Greek says "it was not possible to stop (στῆναι) at the things there"; "the total of things" and "the Intellectual" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.8.p1 | a power capable at once of self-concentration and of outflow | The Greek says "able both to stay (μένειν) and to go forth (προιέναι)": the staying and the going forth of every principle, not concentration and outflow. |
| VI.7.8.p2 | But how come these animals of earth to be There ? | The Greek asks only "why are these living beings (ζῷα) there?"; "of earth" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.8.p2 | The answer is that obviously the unity of our universe must be that of a manifold | The Greek says "this one (τοῦτο τὸ ἕν) must be many", the one being of intellect, after what is altogether one; "our universe" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.8.p3 | and so progressively (in an endless dualisation) | The parenthesis is MacKenna's; the Greek says only "and so again with those" (καὶ ἐκείνων αὖ ὡσαύτως). |
| VI.7.8.p3 | it must be Intellect agglomerate including all the particular intellects | The Greek says "not as one intellect, but all (πᾶς)", holding the particular intellects; "agglomerate" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.8.p3 | it would be the entire living universe containing much besides man | The Greek says "it was a complete living being" (ζῷον παντελὲς ἦν), the Timaeus' pattern of the world; MacKenna makes it a condition ("would be") and the living being "the universe". |
| VI.7.9.p1 | The mean is the unreasoning since value depends upon reason | The Greek is a condition: the cheap belongs to the irrational "if (εἰ) the honourable belongs to the rational"; "since" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.9.p1 | but how can the divine contain the mean, the unreasoning ? | The Greek asks "how were the cheap and the irrational there (ἦν)?"; "the divine" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.9.p2 | here the reasoning, There the pre-reasoning | The Greek says "here, perhaps (ἴσως), he is rational": the "perhaps" is left out. |
| VI.7.9.p2 | Degrees of reasoning here correspond to degrees of Intellection in that other sphere | The Greek says "since thinking (τοῦ νοεῖν) there is different (διαφόρου) in man and the others, reasoning too is different": a difference, not degrees. |
| VI.7.9.p3 | but what we know here as unreasoning was There a Reason-Principle | The Greek says what is called irrational (ἄλογον) "was a forming principle" (λόγος ἦν), and the unintelligent (ἄνουν) "was intellect" (νοῦς): a play on the words; the modern version keeps "forming principle", and "Reason-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.9.p3 | the Thinker of Horse was Intellect and the Thought, Horse, was an Intellect | The Greek says "the thought of a horse (ἡ νόησις ἵππου) was intellect": the thought of a horse, not the Thought named Horse. |
| VI.7.9.p4 | But (it will be objected) if this were a matter of mere thinking we might well admit | The parenthesis is MacKenna's; the Greek raises the question itself, without an objector. |
| VI.7.9.p4 | Intellectual-Principle adapted to some particular living being does not cease to be the Intellectual-Principle of all | The Greek has νοῦς; the modern version keeps "intellect", and "Intellectual-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.9.p4 | every manifestation is the whole, though in some special mode | The Greek says "each part, whatever you take, is all, but perhaps (ἴσως) in another way": a part, not a manifestation, and the "perhaps" is left out. |
| VI.7.9.p5 | Thus the Intellectual-Principle by its very descent is directed towards the perfect sufficiency of the natural constitution | The Greek says that "by the way intellect came down, by that way it might rise up again" (ἀνακύψαι), lifting its head as from water; the rising up again is not in MacKenna. |
| VI.7.9.p5 | the outgoing is by loss and by this loss the powers become one thing or another | The Greek says the powers "go forth letting something go (ἀφιεῖσαι)" and "find out" (ἐξευροῦσαι) something to add: they are not made by the loss, but find a remedy for it. |
| VI.7.10.p1 | Its characteristic difference is in this power of being now this, now that | The Greek says "the difference (ἡ διαφορά) lies in one thing instead of another" (τῷ ἄλλο ἀντὶ ἄλλου): different things in different places, not a power of being now this, now that. |
| VI.7.10.p1 | while yet, over all that multiplicity, unity reigns | The Greek ends with "each complete as what it is" (ἕκαστον δὲ ὡς ἕκαστον τέλειον); the unity over all is MacKenna's, from the next paragraph. |
| VI.7.10.p2 | The Reason-Principle, too, is at once the living form and something else | The Greek has λόγος; the modern version keeps "forming principle", and "Reason-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.10.p2 | and the total is good because the universal is not differentiated | The Greek says "the whole is beautiful" (τὸ ὅλον καλόν); "good" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.11.p1 | The very heavens, patently multiple, cannot be thought to disdain any form of life | The Greek says "it is said" (λέγεται): a saying Plotinus takes up, which MacKenna leaves out. |
| VI.7.11.p1 | But, having fire (warmth) and water | The parenthesis "(warmth)" is MacKenna's; the Greek has "fire" (πῦρ). |
| VI.7.11.p2 | Vegetal life we can well admit, for the plant is a Reason-Principle established in life | The Greek has λόγος; the modern version keeps "forming principle", and "Reason-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.11.p2 | since every Reason-Principle is a unity | The Greek says "the forming principle is something one" (ὁ λόγος ἕν τι): this one, the plant's; "every" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.11.p2 | that primal must have much the truer life | The Greek says that one must "live much before" (πολὺ πρότερον ἐκεῖνο ζῆν): earlier, prior, not truer. |
| VI.7.11.p3 | this, we must take it, is the creative earth-principle corresponding to what we call the specific principle of a tree | The Greek says "as in trees what is called nature" (τὴν λεγομένην φύσιν); "specific principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.11.p3 | except of course that there is no hurt done, the stone remaining a member of the earth as the twig, uncut, of the tree | The Greek sets two stones side by side: the stone cut out is like a twig cut from the tree, and the stone that has not suffered this, still attached, is like the part not cut from the living plant. MacKenna makes the cut stone suffer no hurt. |
| VI.7.11.p4 | that it is a reasoned Earth-Livingness, the earth of Real-Being, earth primally | The Greek says "a life of earth with a forming principle (ζωὴν ἔλλογον γῆς), earth itself (αὐτογῆν) and earth first": earth itself, like man himself, and "the earth of Real-Being" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.11.p5 | Fire, similarly, with other such things, must be a Reason-Principle established in Matter | The Greek is a condition: "if (εἰ) fire too is a forming principle in matter"; MacKenna makes it an assertion, and "Reason-Principle" is his. |
| VI.7.11.p5 | It is with this in mind that Plato says there is soul in everything of this sphere | The Greek says "in each of these" (ἐν ἑκάστῳ τούτων), the elements; "everything of this sphere" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.11.p6 | its nature obliges it to abstain from the soul which nonetheless is indwelling in it | The Greek says "nothing forced happens to it, but it is ready to stand apart (διεστάναι) from the soul that is in it": ready, not obliged. |
| VI.7.11.p6 | the living beings formed from the close conglomeration of air (the stars) are not susceptible to suffering | The parenthesis "(the stars)" is MacKenna's: the living beings of air, in the Epinomis and in Plotinus, are daemons; the stars are of fire. |
| VI.7.11.p6 | so too, by the effect of its circular movement, it eludes soul | The Greek has "round about" (κύκλῳ): the soul is round about the air as the light is; there is no circular movement. |
| VI.7.12.p1 | Since in our view this universe stands to that as copy to original | The Greek says this All is "toward that one as toward a pattern" (πρὸς οἷον παράδειγμα); the modern version keeps "pattern", and "copy to original" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.12.p1 | that is the realm of complete Being and everything must exist There | The Greek is a condition: "if its being is complete (παντελές), it must be all things": the All there, not the realm. |
| VI.7.12.p1 | with the movement of their unending life | The Greek has "in a flowing (ἐν ῥοῇ) and a life that stays (ζωῇ μενούσῃ)": a flowing that stays, not a movement. |
| VI.7.12.p1 | air too must be a member of that universe with the living things of air as here | The Greek says the living beings of air are "in proportion to the air itself" (ἀνὰ λόγον αὐτῷ τῷ ἀέρι); "as here" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.12.p2 | if those lesser forms were not There, that heaven itself would not be | The Greek says "it is not possible that they should not be; or that one (ἐκεῖνος, the heaven) will not be either"; "lesser" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.12.p3 | whence the All-Life, whence the All-soul, whence collective Intellect | The Greek has "all life (ζωὴ πᾶσα) and all soul (ψυχὴ πᾶσα) and the whole intellect": all soul, every soul, not the soul of the All; "the All-soul" and "collective" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.12.p3 | There no indigence or impotence can exist | The Greek has "no poverty (πενίας) nor want (ἀπορίας)": the names of Love's mother, Poverty, and her want of resource in the Symposium. |
| VI.7.13.p1 | Intellectual-Principle is moved in a movement unfailingly true to one course | The Greek says it moves "in the same way, according to the same and alike always" (ὡσαύτως καὶ κατὰ ταὐτὰ καὶ ὅμοια ἀεί), Plato's words for the circle's motion; "one course" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.13.p2 | if no differentiation woke it into life | The Greek has "otherness" (ἑτερότης), one of the greatest kinds; the modern version keeps "otherness", and "differentiation" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.13.p2 | it would not be a Force | The Greek says "it would not even be activity" (οὐδ’ ἂν ἐνέργεια εἴη), and such a state would differ in nothing from not being active (μὴ ἐνεργείας). |
| VI.7.13.p3 | or if there has been advance it still remains a simplex and at once there is a permanent duality | The Greek says "if it has gone forth, another stays (ἄλλο μένον): so that there are two": what goes forth and what stays; MacKenna has it remain a simplex. |
| VI.7.13.p3 | This production, based on Identity and Difference | The Greek has "the same and the other" (ταὐτοῦ καὶ ἑτέρου), the greatest kinds; the modern version keeps "the same", "sameness" and "otherness", and "Identity" and "Difference" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.13.p4 | this wandering permitted to its nature is among real beings which keep pace with its movement | The Greek has "among substances (ἐν οὐσίαις), the substances running along with (συνθεουσῶν) its wanderings"; the modern version keeps "substance", and "real beings" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.13.p4 | a wandering within the Meadow of Truth from which it does not stray | The Greek has "the plain of truth" (τὸ τῆς ἀληθείας πεδίον), Plato's Phaedrus' plain; the meadow (λειμών) is the next word there, but not here. |
| VI.7.13.p5 | its movement is complete, filling Being complete | The Greek has "filling all substance (οὐσίαν πᾶσαν)"; the modern version keeps "substance", and "Being" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.13.p5 | all is dead, activity and actuality exist nowhere | The Greek says "all is idle" (ἀργεῖ πάντη): at rest, without work, not dead. |
| VI.7.13.p6 | If it knew no otherness but was pure identity its essential Being would be the less | The Greek says "if it were not other but the same (ταὐτόν), it would lessen its substance (οὐσίαν)"; the modern version keeps "the same" and "substance", and "identity" and "essential Being" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.14.p1 | On the nature of the Intellectual-Principle we get light from its manifestations | The Greek says one can know what intellect is "by using intellectual patterns (παραδείγμασι νοεροῖς)": examples drawn from intellect, a plant's or a living being's forming principle; "manifestations" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.14.p1 | they show that it demands such diversity as is compatible with its being a monad | The Greek says it "does not endure to be, as it were in the way of a monad (κατὰ μονάδα), not other": intellect refuses to be a monad; MacKenna turns it the other way. |
| VI.7.14.p2 | There is infinity in Intellectual-Principle | The Greek has "the unlimited" (τὸ ἄπειρον); the modern version keeps "the unlimited", as in VI.6, and "infinity" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.14.p2 | all the outlines, all the patterns | The Greek has "figurings" (σχηματισμούς) within the outlines; the modern version keeps "pattern" for παράδειγμα, as in this chapter's first sentence. |
| VI.7.14.p2 | the division is not determined by a boundary but goes ever inward | The Greek says the division is "not in a straight line" (μὴ κατ’ εὐθύ), as a line is cut into pieces side by side, but always inward. |
| VI.7.14.p2 | where there is a halt at the individual form | The Greek has "an indivisible form" (εἶδος ἄτομον), the last species that cannot be divided further, not an individual. |
| VI.7.14.p3 | this is what is known as the Universal Sympathy | The Greek has "the friendship (φιλία) in the All", Empedocles' word; "Sympathy" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.14.p3 | that authentic Sympathy consists in all being a unity and never discriminate | MacKenna ends here; the Greek goes on, "but he says that what is in this heaven is separated" (διακρίνεσθαι δέ φησι τὸ ἐν τῷδε τῷ οὐρανῷ): Empedocles, whose Strife separates in this world. |
| VI.7.15.p1 | All other life is darkness, petty and dim and poor | The Greek says "the others, those below (αἱ κάτω)": the lives here below; MacKenna leaves out "below". |
| VI.7.15.p1 | this life which includes all living | The Greek says you no longer "live all those lives together" (ζῇς ἐκείνας τὰς πάσας ὁμοῦ): the lives there, many and all at once, not one life that includes them. |
| VI.7.15.p2 | There is the archetype, that which is good in the very Idea—we read—as holding The Good in the pure Idea | The Greek says "he calls" (φησιν) the archetype "good-formed" (ἀγαθοειδές), Plato's word, "because it has the good in the forms" (ἐν τοῖς εἴδεσι): in the forms, plural; "the pure Idea" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.15.p2 | it sees also as good the objects of its contemplation because it holds them in its act of contemplating the Principle of Good | The Greek says it acquired (ἐκτήσατο) them "when it was contemplating" (ὅτε ἐθεώρει) the nature of the Good: a past acquiring, not a holding now. |
| VI.7.15.p2 | for it is their source | The Greek says "that one (ἐκεῖνος) is the principle": the Good is the principle, and the forms come from it into intellect; MacKenna makes intellect their source. |
| VI.7.15.p2 | In the very law | The Greek says "it was not lawful" (οὐ ... ἦν θέμις): a thing forbidden, not a law it follows. |
| VI.7.15.p2 | never, on the other hand, could it produce what is There | The Greek says it was not lawful for it to think "what is in that one" (τὰ ἐν ἐκείνῳ): had it thought what is in the Good, it would not have generated; "produce" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.15.p3 | it could not sustain the power poured upon it and therefore broke it up | The Greek says the power "it was bringing away" (ἐκομίζετο), carrying it for itself, and that "it broke it apart" (συνέθραυε); "poured upon it" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.15.p3 | it is good, itself, since it is constituted by these things of good | The Greek says it is good "from good-formed things" (ἐξ ἀγαθοειδῶν): made of things in the form of the good. |
| VI.7.15.p4 | to a unity of souls, all the pure souls, not the faulty but the perfect | The Greek has the pure souls "run together into the same (εἰς τὸ αὐτὸ συνδραμούσας), not lacking (οὐκ ἐνδεεῖς), but having all that is theirs": not lacking, where MacKenna has "not the faulty but the perfect". |
| VI.7.15.p4 | with Intellect enthroned over all | The Greek says the whole intellect is "set on their summits" (ἐπ’ ἄκραις αὐταῖς ἱδρυμένον): at the top of the souls themselves, not over all. |
| VI.7.15.p4 | the true way is to become Intellectual-Principle and be, our very selves, what we are to see | The Greek says "one must become that and make oneself the sight" (τὴν θέαν ἑαυτὸν ποιήσασθαι): the seer becomes the sight; "Intellectual-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.16.p1 | we must make haste yet higher, above this heaven of ours and even that | The Greek says "darting up (ἀίξαντα) and letting this go, not from this heaven but from that one": one goes up from the heaven there, not above it. |
| VI.7.16.p1 | Everything There is a single Idea in an individual impression | The Greek says "each form is each, and its own (ἴδιος), as a stamp": each form its own, not a single Idea. |
| VI.7.16.p1 | possesses also the total Living-Form in virtue of that transcendent life | The Greek says each has "the living being" (τὸ ζῷον), life being common "over all" (ἐπὶ πᾶσιν); "transcendent" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.16.p1 | But what is the Nature of this Transcendent in view of which and by way of which the Ideas are good ? | The Greek asks what would be common to them "in so far as they are goods, and because they are goods": what in them makes them good, not the nature of something transcendent. |
| VI.7.16.p2 | applied its Act by breaking into parts what it was too feeble to know as a whole | The Greek has it "dividing (μερίζων) it in itself" because it "could not" (μὴ ... δύνασθαι) think it whole at once; "too feeble" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.16.p2 | No: that would not be Intellection looking upon the Good; it would be a looking void of Intellection | The Greek says "it was not yet (οὔπω) intellect when it looked at that one, but looked without thinking": a stage before intellect, not a hypothesis set aside. |
| VI.7.16.p2 | all the tendance taking place There and upon That must be a movement teeming with life | The Greek says "this movement, filled by moving there and around (περί) that one, filled it": a movement around the Good; "teeming with life" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.16.p2 | there is no longer bare Act, there is a filling to saturation | The Greek says it "was no longer only movement (κίνησις), but movement sated (διακορής) and full": movement, not Act. |
| VI.7.16.p2 | knows that fact in virtue of its self-knowing | The Greek has "in its awareness of itself" (ἐν συναισθήσει αὑτοῦ): a joint perception of itself, not knowing. |
| VI.7.16.p2 | seeing all by the light from the Giver and bearing that Giver with it | The Greek says it sees them with light from the giver, "bringing this (τοῦτο) along too": it carries the light, not the giver. |
| VI.7.16.p3 | the Supreme may be understood to be the cause at once of essential reality and of the knowing of reality | The Greek says that one "is said" (λέγεται) to be cause of substance (οὐσίας) "and of its being seen", Plato's words; the modern version keeps "substance", and "essential reality" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.16.p3 | cause of Being and Intellectual-Principle | The Greek has "cause of substance (οὐσίας) and intellect"; the modern version keeps "substance", and "Being" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.16.p4 | This filling procures the existence ; after the filling the being | The Greek says "while being filled it was coming to be (ἐγίνετο), when filled it was (ἦν)": becoming, then being; "procures the existence" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.16.p4 | gave shape to the filled | The Greek says that, being filled, it was "as it were stamped" (οἷον ἐτυποῦτο): the stamp (τύπος) of the chapter's first sentences. |
| VI.7.17.p1 | But in what mode are these secondaries, and Intellectual-Principle itself, within the First ? | The Greek asks how these are "in it" (ἐν αὐτῷ), in intellect, when they were not in the one that filled it: not how they are within the First. |
| VI.7.17.p1 | Our sphere containing life, there is a Giver of Life | The Greek says "if, then, there is life in this one (ἐν τούτῳ)", in intellect: the life is intellect's, not our sphere's. |
| VI.7.17.p1 | this possessed Life and had no need to look for it to any giver in possession of Life’s variety | The Greek says intellect "had life, and had no need of a varied (ποικίλου) giver": it is intellect that had life, and the giver needed no variety. |
| VI.7.17.p2 | Life looks to unity and is determined by it, taking bound, limit, form | The Greek says "looking toward one thing (πρὸς ἕν τι)" it is bounded at once: toward something one; "unity" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.17.p2 | limitless itself as radiated from its great Prior | The Greek says the life is "many and unlimited (ἀπείρου)", "as shining out (ἐκλαμψάσης) from such a nature"; the modern version keeps "the unlimited", and "its great Prior" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.17.p3 | all its multiplicity resolves itself into Intellectual-Principles | The Greek says "all, then, are intellects (νόες), and the whole (πᾶς) is intellect, and each (ἕκαστοι) is an intellect"; "collective" and "particular" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.17.p3 | It takes its characteristic difference by becoming entirely a unity within the collective whose totality could not be identical with any particular | The Greek says it had its difference "in becoming wholly one" (εἷς ὅλως γενέσθαι), for "the whole is not the same as any intellect at all"; "within the collective" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.17.p4 | Thus the Life in the Supreme was the collectivity of power | The Greek says "life was all power (δύναμις πᾶσα)": the life of intellect, the trace, not a life in the Supreme. |
| VI.7.17.p4 | Intellectual-Principle, thus arising, is manifested as this universe of Being | The Greek says intellect "appeared as all these things" (αὐτὰ ... τὰ πάντα), the all things of the sight; "this universe of Being" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.17.p4 | that it may serve as base to the Form of the Firsts, the Formless Form | The Greek says it sits upon them to seat them, "having seen the form of the firsts, which is itself formless (ἀνείδεον)": the formless one is the first, the Good, not a "Formless Form". |
| VI.7.17.p4 | it shapes it into Reasoning Soul | The Greek has "rational" (λογικήν): a soul given a forming principle; "Reasoning Soul" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.17.p5 | if the Prior were a Form, the Intellectual-Principle itself could be only a Reason-Principle | The Greek says intellect "would be a forming principle" (λόγος); the modern version keeps "forming principle" for λόγος, and "Reason-Principle" and "only" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.18.p1 | as anything going back to warmth or sweetness carries the memory of those originals | The Greek says "what comes from fire is a trace of fire, and what comes from sweet a trace of sweet": fire and sweet, and a trace, not a memory. |
| VI.7.18.p1 | must inevitably have goodness | The Greek says all "would be good-formed" (ἀγαθοειδῆ ἂν εἴη): a conclusion under a condition, and good-formed, not "having goodness". |
| VI.7.18.p2 | there is difference, again, in the effect here | The Greek says "what comes after these (τὸ ἐπὶ τούτοις) is other again": what comes next, not "the effect here". |
| VI.7.18.p2 | Nonetheless every item may be good in a degree of its own | The Greek says nothing prevents each from being good-formed, "yet more so (μᾶλλον) by another": the question is which is most, not a degree of its own. |
| VI.7.18.p3 | very different from the Life known here | The Greek asks whether life is good "seen bare and stripped" (ψιλὴ ... ἀπογεγυμνωμένη), or the life from it, "and what is from it is something other, of this kind"; "very different from the Life known here" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.18.p3 | so too we must admit in the case of Authentic Intellectual-Principle that its Life because good derives from that First | The Greek says of "the true intellect, that first one" (τοῦ πρώτου ἐκείνου), "that it is good": intellect is good, not its life derived. |
| VI.7.18.p3 | as a common property or as a distinct attribution or as held in some distinct measure | The Greek says "either common, or one more than another, or one first and another next and second" (πρώτως ... δευτέρως): an order of first and second. |
| VI.7.18.p4 | for Life we found to be good not in the bare being but in its derivation from the Authentic | The Greek says life was good "because it was called true (ἀληθινή) and because it was from that one": true life, not "derivation from the Authentic". |
| VI.7.18.p4 | thus life in man or horse yields the notion of animal | The Greek has "living being (τὸ ζῷον) in man and horse": the genus itself, not life. |
| VI.7.18.p4 | the first case is a definition of Kind, the other two cite qualities, primary and secondary respectively | The Greek has two cases: the living being said "as a genus", and the hot said "first in one and second in the other" (fire first, water second). |
| VI.7.18.p4 | Both or one part of Intellect, then, would be called by the one term good | The Greek says "or each of the two, or each, would be called good homonymously (ὁμωνύμως)": by name only, the alternative to be avoided; "part of Intellect" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.18.p5 | Each of them is good but the goodness is not that of the Unity-Good | The Greek says "each whole is good, not good by one thing" (οὐ καθ’ ἕν): the whole thing good, not by one part in it; "the Unity-Good" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.18.p5 | By the mode of parts | The Greek asks it ("as parts?"), and answers at once: "but the Good is without parts". |
| VI.7.18.p5 | but here it has become particularised | The Greek says "it is one itself, but this is good in this way, that in that" (οὑτωσὶ δὲ τόδε): each in its own way; "particularised" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.18.p5 | the good that is an ordered system derived from that earlier, and the good that is in the actualisation (in the thing participant) | The Greek has three: the first activity, good as coming to be by that one; what was bounded on it, good as an order (κόσμος) from it; and both together (συνάμφω), good as both. "In the actualisation (in the thing participant)" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.18.p5 | like the speech and walk and other characteristics of one man, each playing its due part | The Greek says "all done rightly" (κατορθούμενα): from the same man, all right actions; "characteristics" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.18.p6 | We might answer that in the case of the sense-order, too, the good is imposed | The Greek says "here (ἐνταῦθα) the being well (τὸ καλῶς) is wholly from outside": no "too", and "sense-order" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.18.p6 | we do not deny that things deriving Thence are good | The Greek says "honourable" (τίμια), of worth: their goodness is what is asked. |
| VI.7.18.p6 | our subject demands that we discover the mode by which they come to possess that goodness | The Greek says "the argument (ὁ λόγος) longs (ποθεῖ) to grasp" in respect of what their good is: λόγος here is the argument, not the forming principle, and it longs. |
| VI.7.19.p1 | Are we to rest all on pursuit and on the soul? | The Greek has "desire" (ἔφεσις) and "the judgment" (τὴν κρίσιν): whether to give the judgment over to desire; "pursuit" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.19.p1 | is the good to be dismissed as choice ? | The Greek asks whether we shall "give (δώσομεν) the good over to desire": hand it over to desire to judge, not dismiss it. |
| VI.7.19.p1 | The good becomes a mere attribute of things | The Greek says "the good will be one of the things relative (τῶν πέρι)": said in relation to something; "a mere attribute" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.19.p1 | objects of pursuit are many and different | The Greek says "the things that desire (τὰ ἐφιέμενα) are many, and desire different things": the desirers, not the objects. |
| VI.7.19.p2 | Are we to determine the good by the respective values of things ? | The Greek says "by each thing's virtue (ἀρετήν)", its excellence; the modern version keeps "virtue", and "values" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.19.p2 | This is to make Idea and Reason-Principle the test : all very well | The Greek says "we shall lead it up (ἀνάξομεν) to form and forming principle (λόγον), going rightly"; the modern version keeps "forming principle" for λόγος, and "Reason-Principle" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.19.p2 | we are without a standard There where no evil exists, the Bests holding the field, alone | The Greek says "we shall be at a loss" (ἀπορήσομεν) there, where the better things are "by themselves"; "the Bests holding the field" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.19.p3 | Reason demands to know what constitutes goodness | The Greek says "the argument (ὁ λόγος) seeks the why (τὸ διότι)": the cause, the why of the demonstrations; and it is asked as a question, "is it that ...?". |
| VI.7.19.p3 | the doubt still remains until our reason claims its rights There | The Greek says the difficulty is the same "since the argument does not reach (μὴ φθάνοντος) there": it does not get there at all; "claims its rights" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.20.p1 | form, shapelessness; real-being, decay | The Greek has "substance (οὐσίαν) and destruction (φθοράν)"; the modern version keeps "substance", and "real-being" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.20.p1 | reasoning soul, at least | The Greek has "the wise one (ἔμφρων) at least": a soul with wisdom in it, not reasoning. |
| VI.7.20.p1 | so therefore does all that a reasoned life aims at | The Greek says "what a wise life (ἔμφρων ζωή) desires": wise, not reasoned. |
| VI.7.20.p2 | that principle is the Term of Soul which on judgement sets itself towards Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says "soul desires (ἐφίεται) this", and judges and desires intellect; "the Term" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.20.p2 | pronouncing right preferable to wrong | The Greek has "justice (δικαιοσύνην) better than injustice"; the modern version keeps "justice". |
| VI.7.20.p2 | and thus ranking by its choosing | The Greek says "what it prefers is what it chooses (αἵρεσις)": preferring and choosing are of the same things. |
| VI.7.20.p3 | The soul aiming only at that Principle would need a further lessoning | The Greek says "if it desired intellect only, perhaps more argument (λόγου) would be needed": more argument for us, to show that intellect is not the last; not a lesson for the soul. |
| VI.7.20.p3 | life itself is desirable only in view of good | The Greek says "since life is so too" (ἐπεὶ καὶ ἡ ζωὴ οὕτως): life too is from good and toward good; "only" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.21.p1 | Intellectual-Principle and that life are of the order of good | The Greek says they are "good-formed" (ἀγαθοειδῆ), the word of chapters 15 to 18: in the form of the good, not "of the order". |
| VI.7.21.p1 | because Life is an Activity in The Good | The Greek says life is "the activity of the Good (τἀγαθοῦ), or rather from the Good": of, not in. |
| VI.7.21.p1 | while Intellectual-Principle is an Activity already defined Therein | The Greek says intellect is "that activity already bounded" (ἤδη ὁρισθεῖσαν); "Therein" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.21.p1 | the related if not good is shunned in spite of that relationship | The Greek says "the akin (τὸ οἰκεῖον), if it is not good, is akin, but one flees it": the akin, the word of chapters 24 and 27. |
| VI.7.21.p2 | is due not to what they are but to the consideration of their nature as something apart, received from above themselves | The Greek says the love comes "when, being already what they are, they take on (προσλάβῃ) something else from there": something added, not "the consideration of their nature". |
| VI.7.21.p2 | or even they would not be visible to themselves and beyond | The Greek says "so that they may be seen both by themselves and by another (ὑπ’ ἄλλου)": by another, not "beyond". |
| VI.7.22.p1 | Every one of those Beings exists for itself | The Greek says "each is what it is by itself" (ἐφ’ αὑτοῦ): by itself, not for itself. |
| VI.7.22.p1 | The soul taking that outflow from the divine is stirred | The Greek has "the flow from there" (τὴν ἐκεῖθεν ἀπορροήν), from the Good; "the divine" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.22.p1 | goaded by these goads | The Greek says "filled with goads" (οἴστρων πίμπλαται), the gadfly's stings of the Phaedrus. |
| VI.7.22.p2 | for that beauty is dead until it take the light of The Good | The Greek says the beauty is "idle" (ἀργόν), without work, as in chapter 13: idle, not dead. |
| VI.7.22.p2 | spreads true wings | The Greek says it "really grows wings" (ὄντως πτεροῦται), the Phaedrus' feathering of the soul: the wings grow, they are not spread. |
| VI.7.22.p2 | speeds its buoyant way elsewhere, to something greater to its memory | The Greek says it "is lifted (κουφίζεται) toward another, greater, as by memory (οἷον τῇ μνήμῃ)"; "speeds its buoyant way" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.22.p3 | but not of power to hold its gaze | The Greek says the face "cannot yet move (κινεῖν) the sight": it does not stir the seer, not hold the gaze. |
| VI.7.22.p4 | though the face yet retains all its fulness and symmetry | The Greek says "not yet wasted (μεμαρασμένου) in its flesh and symmetries": not yet decayed; "all its fulness" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.22.p4 | Why are the most living portraits the most beautiful | The Greek has "statues" (ἀγαλμάτων), the more lifelike (ζωτικώτερα) of them: statues, not portraits. |
| VI.7.22.p4 | because there is more of the Idea of The Good | The Greek says "because it is more good-formed (ἀγαθοειδέστερον)": the word of chapters 15 to 21; "the Idea of The Good" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.22.p4 | so that the whole man is won over to goodness, and in the fullest measure stirred to life | The Greek says it "makes good (ἀγαθοποιεῖ) and wakes, as far as it can (ὡς οἷόν τε), what it has": the body it has, as far as it can; "the whole man" and "the fullest measure" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.23.p1 | leaving its mark wherever it falls | The Greek says "falling (ἐμπεσόν) on it, moves its trace (ἴχνος)": it moves its own trace in intellect; "wherever it falls" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.23.p1 | From it came all and so there is nothing mightier | The Greek says "nothing is better (κρεῖττον) than it, and all are less": better, not mightier. |
| VI.7.23.p1 | when evil had yet no place in things | The Greek has "vice" (κακία): before there was even vice; evils come in the next sentence. |
| VI.7.23.p2 | there found where there is no trace of This | The Greek says evils are "in the things that have not shared (μετειληφόσι) in this even in one respect": in what has no share, not "no trace". |
| VI.7.23.p2 | there can then be no difference in objects coming up for choice | The Greek says things would be "indifferent (ἀδιάφορα) by nature for choosing": the Stoics' word for what is neither good nor evil. |
| VI.7.23.p3 | the souls which Intellectual-Principle sends forth | The Greek says "souls from this" (ἐκ τούτου); "which Intellectual-Principle sends forth" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.23.p3 | everything else that partakes of Reason, of Intellectual-Principle or of Life | The Greek has λόγου: what shares in a forming principle, as plants do; the modern version keeps "forming principle" for λόγος, and "Reason" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.24.p1 | We may recall what we have said of the nature of the light shining from it into Intellectual-Principle and so by participation into the soul | The Greek says "or let us speak again about the light, what the light is (τί τὸ φῶς)": to ask what it is, which is then put off. |
| VI.7.24.p1 | No doubt this universal questing would make the goodness evident | The Greek says "one could make this a witness (μαρτύριον)" that it is good; "universal questing" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.24.p2 | Further, is the questing determined by the hope of some acquisition or by sheer delight ? | The Greek asks whether they desire "by receiving something (τι δέχεσθαι)" or by delighting: receiving, not the hope of acquiring. |
| VI.7.24.p2 | Is goodness in the appropriate or in something apart | The Greek has "the akin" (τῷ οἰκείῳ), the word of chapters 21 and 27: what belongs to one. |
| VI.7.24.p2 | Any good is such, necessarily, not for itself but for something outside | The Greek puts it as one side of the question ("or whatever is good ..."), and it is answered at once by "a nature to which nothing is good". |
| VI.7.24.p3 | you scatter praises here, there and everywhere | The Greek says "why do you put on airs with names (ἀποσεμνύνετε τοῖς ὀνόμασιν), up and down": exalting by grand names, not scattering praise. |
| VI.7.24.p3 | Or what do we gain by seeing the Ideas themselves if we see only a particular Idea and nothing else | The Greek asks "what good would one who thinks the forms themselves have, contemplating each one itself (αὐτὸ ἕκαστον)": each form in itself; "only a particular Idea and nothing else" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.24.p3 | If we are happy here we may be deceived into thinking life a good when it is merely pleasant | The Greek says "if he were deceived and pleased (ἡδόμενος) by these": pleased by the forms, not "happy here". |
| VI.7.24.p3 | but suppose our lot unhappy | The Greek says "if he stood in being without pleasure (ἀνήδονος)": without pleasure, not unhappy. |
| VI.7.25.p1 | makes pleasure an element in the Term | The Greek says "mixing (μιγνύς) pleasure with the end (τῷ τέλει)": the mixed life of the Philebus; "the Term" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.25.p1 | since he sees no attractive power in it | The Greek says "not seeing what moves (τὸ κινοῦν) in it": what moves the soul, the stirring of chapter 22. |
| VI.7.25.p2 | it is a mingled thing | The Greek says the being whose good it is "falls short and is perhaps composite (συνθέτου)": the being is composite, not the good. |
| VI.7.25.p2 | we are to understand that good does not hold place in the One and Alone whose being is too great and different for that | The Greek says "what is solitary and alone (τὸ ἔρημον καὶ μόνον) has no good, but is otherwise and greater": it has no good because it is good in another way; "the One" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.25.p3 | The solution, it would seem, lies in priority | The Greek has no such sentence: it goes straight on to the question of the ascent (ἀνάβασις), "for the last among beings, what is before it". |
| VI.7.25.p3 | so long as the movement does not tend awry but advances continuously towards the superior | The Greek says "if the ascent does not depart from proportion (τοῦ ἀνὰ λόγον)": it keeps its proportion, step by step; "tend awry" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.25.p3 | the supremely sovran | The Greek has "most properly (μάλιστα κυρίως)": good in the most proper sense; "sovran" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.25.p4 | Matter would have Forming-Idea for its good | The Greek has "form" (τὸ εἶδος); "Forming-Idea" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.25.p4 | some will confer order and place | The Greek has "order and adornment (κόσμον)": the ordering of a world, not place. |
| VI.7.25.p4 | Intellectual-Principle will draw upon the Authentic Good which we hold to be coterminous with it | The Greek says "to intellect, the Good, which we say comes (ἥκειν) into it too": the Good comes into intellect; "coterminous with it" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.25.p4 | as even now taking light from it | The Greek says the Good "now too gives (δίδωσι) what is called light": the Good gives, not intellect takes. |
| VI.7.26.p1 | Any conscious being if the good come to him will know the good | The Greek has "what is by nature able to perceive (αἰσθάνεσθαι)": perceiving, not conscious. |
| VI.7.26.p1 | All the striving, all the pain, show that to everything something is a good | The Greek has "travail" (ὠδίς), the birth pangs of the Symposium's lover, not pain. |
| VI.7.26.p1 | the lifeless finds its share in something outside itself | The Greek says "for the soulless (ἀψύχοις) the gift (δόσις) of the good is from another": given, not found. |
| VI.7.26.p1 | the living plan for their own good | The Greek says "for the living their providence (πρόνοια) is from themselves": they care for themselves, as the dead are cared for by others. |
| VI.7.26.p2 | The witness of attainment is betterment, cleaving to state, satisfaction, settlement, suspension of pursuit | The Greek says it is confirmed when one becomes better "and without regret (ἀμετανόητον)", filled, staying and seeking nothing else: the regret is left out, and "cleaving to state" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.26.p2 | The good, worthy of the name, can be no such tasting of the casual | The Greek says the good one chooses "must not be the affection (πάθος) at what one gets": the feeling, not "the casual". |
| VI.7.27.p1 | to soul moral excellence is this Form | The Greek has "virtue (ἀρετή) is the soul's form"; the modern version keeps "virtue". |
| VI.7.27.p1 | No: the aptest would be the most resemblant to the thing itself | The Greek says "the like (τὸ ὅμοιον) is akin": the like, not "the aptest" or "the most resemblant". |
| VI.7.27.p1 | to be a good to another a thing must have something beyond aptness | The Greek asks "but shall we say it is not akin, when we say it is good?", and answers that the akin is judged by what is better; "something beyond aptness" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.27.p2 | A thing is potentially that to which its nature looks | The Greek says "being in potency toward what it is (πρὸς ὃ ἔστιν), it lacks it": "its nature looks" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.27.p3 | This is why the least alloyed and nearest to the good are most at peace within themselves | The Greek has "at home with themselves" (οἰκείωσις πρὸς αὑτούς): a belonging to oneself, the akin of the chapter, not peace. |
| VI.7.27.p3 | so that it must strain outside its essential quality to the good which it effectually is | The Greek says "as if it had to stand outside (ἐξίστασθαι) its own nature toward itself, and not love (ἀγαπᾶν) itself as good": the love of self is what is left out. |
| VI.7.27.p4 | the striving of the lower possesses itself of the good as of a thing resident in a certain Kind | The Greek says "the ascent (ἐπανάβασις) has the good lying in some nature (φύσει)": the ascent, not the striving of the lower, and a nature, not a Kind. |
| VI.7.28.p1 | for the good seeks to subject everything to itself | The Greek says "everything seeks the good for itself (αὑτῷ πᾶν ζητεῖ)": each thing seeks its own good, which would not be its perishing; MacKenna turns the sentence round. |
| VI.7.28.p1 | would not wish to remain at its own level | The Greek says "it will not seek to be matter (ὕλη εἶναι), but to be": "at its own level" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.28.p2 | and we asserted that the entry of Form, that dream of the Good, must raise it to a nobler order | The Greek says form came upon it "as a good dream (ὥσπερ ὀνείρατος ἀγαθοῦ)": a dream that is good, not a dream of the Good; and it "came to be" in a more beautiful order, not "must raise". |
| VI.7.28.p2 | a wrong thing, let us say | The Greek has "such as vice (κακία)", the soul's evil, set beside matter. |
| VI.7.28.p2 | But not what is evil in Matter would be the quester of good but that element in it (lowest Form) which in it is associated with evil | The Greek says "it was not the vice that chose, but what was made bad (τὸ κακούμενον)": the one made bad by vice, the soul; "in Matter" and "(lowest Form)" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.28.p3 | This question implies that if Evil were self-conscious it would admire itself | The Greek asks it: "if evil got perception of itself, would it love (ἀγαπήσει) itself?": a question, and love, not admiration. |
| VI.7.28.p3 | and did we not discover that the good must be apt to the nature ? | The Greek says the opposite: "we did not set (οὐ ... ἐθέμεθα) the good in the akin (τῷ οἰκείῳ)", as chapter 27 showed: the good is not what is akin. |
| VI.7.28.p4 | Soul more truly Form than body is | The Greek says soul is more form "than the body's form" (ἢ σώματος εἶδος): than the form in a body. |
| VI.7.28.p4 | Intellectual-Principle standing as Form to soul collectively | The Greek says intellect is more form "than soul" (ψυχῆς); "collectively" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.28.p4 | then the Good advances by the opposite of Matter and, therefore, by a cleansing and casting away to the utmost possible at each stage | The Greek says the good "would draw near (προσχωροῖ) to what is opposite to matter, and is as it were purified (καθαιρομένῳ)": the good comes near to what is purified and puts matter off; MacKenna has the good advance by a cleansing. |
| VI.7.28.p4 | must hold itself aloft with that Formless in which Primal Form takes its origin | The Greek says it "would have fled up into the formless nature (εἰς τὴν ἀνείδεον φύσιν), from which the first form comes": the Good is that formless nature, as in chapter 17, not with it. |
| VI.7.29.p1 | this is quite likely in the case of the wiser and least dependent | The Greek has "the more self-controlled" (σωφρονεστέρῳ), not the wiser; the modern version keeps "self-control", as for σωφροσύνη throughout. |
| VI.7.29.p1 | and indeed it is so with the First, immune not merely because simplex | The Greek says that being moved along with having the good "does not belong to the first either" (οὐδὲ τῷ πρώτῳ): the first has no pleasure in it; "immune" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.29.p1 | and the rebuttal of the argument brought up against us | The Greek has "that opposing (ἀντίτυπον) argument", pushed off once the way is cleared; it is not said to be brought against "us". |
| VI.7.29.p2 | What gain is there in the Good to one who, fully conscious, feels nothing | The Greek has "one who has intellect" (ὁ νοῦν ἔχων), the sensible man, who is "not struck at all" (οὐδὲν πληττόμενος) when he hears; "fully conscious" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.29.p2 | because he holds to false values | The Greek says he "takes each of them for something else" (ἄλλο τι ἕκαστον αὐτῶν ὑπολαμβάνων): a misreading of the words, not false values. |
| VI.7.29.p2 | there will generally have been, even, some inkling of the good beyond Intellection | The Greek says "perhaps" (τάχα), not "generally", and he divines "what is beyond intellect" (τὸ ὑπὲρ νοῦν). |
| VI.7.29.p3 | otherwise he will not even hold ignorance an evil | The Greek has "folly" (ἄνοια), the want of intellect (νοῦς), set against the thinking everyone chooses; not ignorance. |
| VI.7.29.p3 | so that our very sensations seek to ripen into knowledge | The Greek says "the senses too bear witness, wishing to be knowings (εἰδήσεις)"; "ripen" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.29.p3 | what must be present to those of power to see the Author and Father of Intellect | The Greek asks "what would one imagine (φαντασθείη), if one could, of its begetter and father?": what one could picture of it, not what is present to those who see it. |
| VI.7.29.p3 | brings evidence against himself and all his state | The Greek says he witnesses against himself "and all his own affections (πάθεσι)": his own clinging to being and life speaks against his scorn of them. |
| VI.7.30.p1 | is a question not to be ignored in any enquiry into the nature of the good | The Greek says it is fitting "now", for those "laying hold of the good" (ἐφαπτομένους τοῦ ἀγαθοῦ), to look at it; the enquiry is theirs, not any enquiry. |
| VI.7.30.p1 | that state of soul or mind which springs from wisdom | The Greek has "the soul's affection (πάθος) that comes from being wise (φρονεῖν)": a feeling, the joy of it, not a state of soul or mind. |
| VI.7.30.p2 | this theory would maintain that the isolated and unrelated could be the good, could be an object of desire | The Greek says the opposite: "what is bare and alone (τὸ ἔρημον καὶ μόνον) can neither come to be nor be chosen as a good", which is why this opinion mixes pleasure in. |
| VI.7.30.p2 | the unreasoning satisfactions of soul (or lower mind) | The Greek has "the irrational joys (χαραὶ ... ἄλογοι) of the soul"; "(or lower mind)" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.30.p3 | will be followed and as it were escorted by the overdwelling consciousness | The Greek has "what runs over it" (τὸ ἐπιθέον), the bloom that comes on an activity; there is no consciousness in the Greek. |
| VI.7.30.p3 | so that the life is the less self-guided | The Greek says the hindrance "does not let the life be its own (ἑαυτῆς)": a life not its own, not less self-guided. |
| VI.7.30.p4 | the very principle of all the colour and radiance and brightness found here | The Greek says "the cause is what coloured (ἐπιχρῶσαν) these things, and shone on them": the Good colours the welcome things there, as in chapters 21 and 22; "found here" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.30.p4 | this best is what is symmetry, beauty, collective Idea | The Greek has "a composite form" (εἶδος σύνθετον): a form made of parts, not a collective Idea. |
| VI.7.30.p4 | life clear, Intellective and good | The Greek has a life "clear and intellectual and beautiful (καλήν)": beautiful, not good. |
| VI.7.31.p1 | which flashed Nature into being | The Greek says intellect "lit up its own nature (τὴν φύσιν ἐξέλαμψε)" by the radiance of its activity: its nature shone out; Nature was not flashed into being. |
| VI.7.31.p1 | Thence soul took power towards life, in virtue of that fuller life streaming into it | The Greek has "since more life came into it": "streaming" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.31.p1 | clung to what it saw | The Greek says the soul "turned" (ἐπιστραφεῖσα) and, as it knew and saw, "took pleasure in the sight" (ἥσθη τῇ θέᾳ); there is no clinging. |
| VI.7.31.p2 | but having in itself something of that principle it felt its kinship | The Greek says it "perceived it with itself" (συνῄσθετο): it was aware of what it had in itself; "kinship" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.31.p3 | they are unwilling to fall short in moral quality or in other graces | The Greek has the beloved's "self-control (σωφροσύνῃ) and the rest of his virtue (ἀρετῇ)"; the modern version keeps self-control and virtue. |
| VI.7.31.p3 | and only among such can true love be | The Greek says "these are the ones who are able to be with (συνεῖναι) them": to be with the beloved, not true love. |
| VI.7.31.p3 | In the same way the soul loves the Supreme Good | The Greek says the soul "loves that one" (ἐρᾷ ἐκείνου), moved by him to love; "the Supreme Good" is MacKenna's name for him. |
| VI.7.31.p4 | The soul which has never strayed from this love | The Greek has the soul "that has its love ready to hand (πρόχειρον)", near and at hand, not one that has never strayed. |
| VI.7.31.p4 | with one glance at the beauty of the universe dismisses all | The Greek says, seeing the beautiful things in this All, "it is suspicious (ὑποψίαν ἔχει) of them": suspicion, not dismissal at a glance. |
| VI.7.31.p4 | for it sees that all is put together of flesh and Matter | The Greek says it sees them "in flesh and bodies (σώμασιν)": there is no matter here. |
| VI.7.31.p4 | By only noting the flux of things it knows at once | The Greek says "when it sees them flowing past (παραρρέοντα) as well": their flowing away is a further sign, not the only one. |
| VI.7.31.p5 | it enters by that neighbouring into conscious possession of what it has long been seeking | The Greek says "being near, it perceives (αἰσθάνεται)" what it has long sought: perception of what is near, not possession. |
| VI.7.32.p1 | the begetter of the veritable | The Greek says he "begot substance" (γεννήσας οὐσίαν); the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία. |
| VI.7.32.p1 | You see the splendour over the things of the universe with all the variety begotten of the Ideas | The Greek asks "do you see the beauty on all these forms (εἴδεσι), many-coloured as they are?": the beauty on the forms there, not on the things of the universe, and nothing begotten of the Ideas. |
| VI.7.32.p2 | were it so, it too would be a thing of parts | The Greek says "it would be some one of them, and a part (μέρος)": itself a part of them, not a thing of parts. |
| VI.7.32.p2 | but what shape can that have which no one has shaped | The Greek asks "what no one made (ἐποίησε), who would make it?": made, not shaped; nothing made it something. |
| VI.7.32.p3 | The greatness of the Authentic cannot be a greatness of quantity | The Greek has "the greatness of substance (τῆς οὐσίας)": the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.7.32.p3 | Its eternity and universal reach entail neither measure nor measurelessness | The Greek has "for ever and to all" (τὸ εἰς ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς πάντα), its power reaching always and to everything; "eternity" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.32.p4 | granted beauty, the absence of shape or form to be grasped is but enhancement of desire and love | The Greek says "of what is longed for (ποθεινοῦ)": the longing is there already, not beauty granted. |
| VI.7.32.p5 | it cannot be beauty since it is not a thing among things | The Greek asks "being none of the things, what beauty is it?" and answers that, being lovable, "what begets would be beauty": MacKenna makes the question a denial. |
| VI.7.32.p5 | it will be the ultimate of beauty | The Greek has "the flower (ἄνθος) of beauty", its bloom, not its ultimate. |
| VI.7.32.p5 | the source and height of beauty | The Greek has "the principle (ἀρχή) of beauty and the limit (πέρας) of beauty": its beginning and its end, not its height. |
| VI.7.33.p1 | When therefore we name beauty, all such shape must be dismissed | The Greek says "one must rather flee (φευκτέον) from such a shape, and not set it before the eyes": a flight from shape, not its dismissal. |
| VI.7.33.p1 | even that given in thought to mark difference | The Greek has "the shape in an account (ἐν λόγῳ)", the shape of a definition, by which justice differs from self-control; not "given in thought". |
| VI.7.33.p1 | as for instance the difference between Justice and Sophrosyny | The Greek has "justice and self-control (σωφροσύνην)"; the modern version keeps "self-control". |
| VI.7.33.p2 | The Intellectual-Principle is the less for seeing things as distinct | The Greek says intellect is lessened "since it thinks something of its own (ἴδιόν τι)", a shape proper to it, even if it takes all together; seeing things as distinct is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.33.p2 | reason, however, urging that This at last is the Authentic Term | The Greek says "the argument (ὁ λόγος) says that this is the real (τὸ ὄντως)"; "the Authentic Term" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.33.p3 | reason answers that while there exists the giver having shape to give | The Greek says "the argument says that what has shape, and the shape, and the form, are all measured (μεμετρημένον)": there is no giver of shape in the sentence. |
| VI.7.33.p3 | the beauty in it must be, simply, the Nature of the Intellectual Good | The Greek says "the beauty there (ἐκεῖ) is the nature of the intelligible good (νοητοῦ ἀγαθοῦ)": intelligible, not intellectual. |
| VI.7.33.p4 | so long as the attention is upon the visible form, love has not entered | The Greek says "as long as one is with (ἐν) what has the perceptible impression": with the perceptible one, not attending to a visible form. |
| VI.7.33.p4 | then the lover longs for the sight of the beloved to make that fading image live again | The Greek says he seeks to see the beloved "so as to water (ἐπάρδῃ)" the impression "as it withers": watering, as of a plant or of the wings. |
| VI.7.33.p5 | Shape is an impress from the unshaped | The Greek has "the trace (ἴχνος) of the shapeless is shape", the trace of chapters 17 and 18; MacKenna's "impress" is near, and the modern version keeps "trace". |
| VI.7.33.p5 | but to that which draws upon Form | The Greek has "what has been formed by the form (τὸ εἰδοποιηθὲν διὰ τὸ εἶδος)": the thing made a form, not something that draws upon form. |
| VI.7.34.p1 | even to the Intellectual shape that has informed it | The Greek says "whatever shape of the intelligible (νοητοῦ) is in it": intelligible, and nothing of its having informed the soul. |
| VI.7.34.p1 | There is no vision, no union, for those handling or acting by any thing other | The Greek says it is not possible "having something else and being active about it, either to see or to be fitted (ἐναρμοσθῆναι) to it": fitted, as a harmony, not union. |
