| VI.7.34.p2 | by those preparings and adornings which come unbidden to those growing ready for the vision | The Greek says "the preparation and the adorning are clear (δήλη), I think, to those who prepare themselves": known to them, not coming unbidden. |
| VI.7.34.p2 | any observation of such things falls away | The Greek says "the sight of these is somehow uneven (ἀνώμαλος)": a sight out of keeping with the even one she has; not that it falls away. |
| VI.7.34.p3 | she is of perfect judgement and knows that This was her quest | The Greek says "then she has the power to judge well (κρίνειν καλῶς)": a judging she has then, not a perfect judgement. |
| VI.7.34.p3 | and the truth she affirms, that she is, herself | The Greek says "what she says, then, is 'it is that one' (ἐκεῖνό ἐστι)": she says what it is, not that she is herself. |
| VI.7.34.p3 | for this is no affirmation of an excited body | The Greek has a body "tickled" (γαργαλιζομένου), the pleasure of scratching, not excitement. |
| VI.7.34.p4 | linked to This she can fear no disaster nor even know it | The Greek says she does not fear "while she is with that one, or even if she has only seen it at all (οὐδ’ ὅλως ἰδοῦσα)": even the sight is enough; "nor even know it" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.35.p1 | she has nothing to say of this very Intellectual-Principle by means of which she has attained the vision | The Greek says "she says that the one (ἐκεῖνον) she sees is not moved either": she speaks of what she sees, the Good, not of intellect. |
| VI.7.35.p1 | so as to be able to take stand in that Intellectual space | The Greek has "the intelligible place" (τῷ τόπῳ τῷ νοητῷ), Plato's phrase: intelligible, not intellectual. |
| VI.7.35.p1 | no thing of ornament but calling for the truest attention | The Greek says the master is "not like the nature of the statues (ἀγαλμάτων)" in the house, "but worthy of the true sight (θέας)": the statues, and a sight, not attention. |
| VI.7.35.p2 | the first seeing is that of Intellect knowing | The Greek has the sight "of intellect in its senses (νοῦ ἔμφρονος)", the sober intellect, set against intellect "out of its senses" (ἄφρων); not knowing. |
| VI.7.35.p2 | by this excess it is made simplex and is happy | The Greek says it is "simplified into well-being by its fill (τῷ κόρῳ)": its fill, as in chapter 16, not an excess. |
| VI.7.35.p2 | whereby to know its transcendent | The Greek says it sees "what is beyond (ἐπέκεινα) itself by a kind of direct touch (ἐπιβολῇ) and reception": seeing by touch, not knowing. |
| VI.7.35.p3 | reason unravelling gives process | The Greek says "the account (λόγος), in teaching, makes them come to be (γινόμενα)": it tells in order what is at once, as the myths do in III.5.9; "unravelling" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.35.p3 | In this seeing of the Supreme it becomes pregnant | The Greek says "in seeing that one it had offspring (γεννήματα)", already born, which it perceived as they came to be and were in it. |
| VI.7.35.p3 | in a later (lower) stage it is to become | The Greek says it sees that one "by the power by which it was going to think" (ᾗ δυνάμει ἔμελλε νοεῖν): the power from which its thinking came, not a later or lower stage. |
| VI.7.35.p4 | or in that realm of difference where everything is rooted in some other thing | The Greek says "nor in anything else in which one thing is by nature in another": there is no realm of difference. |
| VI.7.35.p4 | The Good spreading out above them and adapting itself to that union which it hastens to confirm | The Greek says the Good is "fitted to the joining (συστάσει) of both, running over them (ἐπιδραμὸν)": running over, as the bloom of chapter 30; "hastens to confirm" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.36.p1 | We need not carry this matter further; we turn to a question already touched | The Greek says "the rest is clear, and something has been said about this too": the question is the same one, the Good, not a new one. |
| VI.7.36.p1 | this—we read—is the grand learning | The Greek says "he says" (φησι), Plato, that this is "the greatest study" (μέγιστον μάθημα), his word in the Republic. |
| VI.7.36.p2 | We come to this learning by analogies, by abstractions | The Greek has "negations" (ἀφαιρέσεις), takings away of what it is not; "abstractions" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.36.p2 | Purification has The Good for goal | The Greek says "purifications (καθάρσεις) ... bring us on our way (πορεύουσι)" to it, with the virtues and the rest: they lead, they are not said to have it as goal. |
| VI.7.36.p2 | identical with Being and Intellectual-Principle and the entire living all | The Greek says "having become substance (οὐσία) and intellect and complete living being (ζῷον παντελές)": the modern version keeps "substance", and the complete living being is Plato's. |
| VI.7.36.p2 | radiant above the Intellectual | The Greek says it is "shining over all the intelligible (ἐπὶ παντὶ τῷ νοητῷ)": intelligible, not intellectual. |
| VI.7.36.p3 | and allowed them to occupy the quester’s mind | The Greek says the radiance let them "be beside it" (εἶναι παῤ αὐτῷ), beside the Good; there is no quester's mind. |
| VI.7.36.p3 | With This he himself becomes identical, with that radiance | The Greek says "he himself (αὐτὸς) is a radiance only, begetting intellect": he is the Good, which begets intellect; MacKenna makes him the quester, made one with it. |
| VI.7.36.p3 | disciplined to this pitch | The Greek has "led as by a tutor (παιδαγωγηθεὶς) up to this": the slave who leads a boy to school, not discipline. |
| VI.7.37.p1 | Those ascribing Intellection to the First have not supposed him to know the lesser, the emanant | The Greek has "the lesser things and what comes from it (τῶν ἐξ αὐτοῦ)": there is no word of emanation. |
| VI.7.37.p1 | not something whose value derives from him | The Greek says "and not as if he made thinking solemn (σεμνύνοντος)": he would give it its dignity, not its value. |
| VI.7.37.p2 | If in the Intellection, he has no worth or the less worth | The Greek says "it is not honourable by itself (αὑτῷ), or less so": its honour would be another's; "by itself" is what MacKenna leaves out. |
| VI.7.37.p2 | Now if this means that he is an essence eternally intellective | The Greek has "a substance (οὐσία) always thinking"; the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία. |
| VI.7.37.p2 | though the two are inseparable | The Greek says "even if they always see" (κἂν ἀεὶ βλέπωσιν): the seeing is still added to the eyes; "inseparable" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.37.p3 | But do not we ourselves assert that the Beings There are essence and Act | The Greek has "substance (οὐσίαν) and activity"; the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.7.37.p3 | bent inward for this vision | The Greek says it is "turned (ἐπιστραφὲν) to it in the sight": turned toward what made it, not bent inward. |
| VI.7.37.p4 | why import one and declare a defect because it is not performed | The Greek says "accuse it by privation (κατὰ στέρησιν)": a privation is only of what is by nature able, so it does not apply here. |
| VI.7.38.p1 | it is to state identity | The Greek says the "is" is said "as signifying what it is (σημαῖνον ὃ ἔστι)"; the sentence is about what the "is" signifies. |
| VI.7.38.p1 | it conveys an identification | The Greek says we say "the Good" about it "not predicating that it belongs to it, but that it is it (ὅτι αὐτό)". |
| VI.7.38.p1 | it is that sheer negation does not indicate | The Greek says "not being able to show it if one took it away altogether (εἰ ... ἀφέλοι)": if one took away the word, or the "the"; not negation. |
| VI.7.38.p2 | But he does not possess Being | The Greek says "but it is not" (ἀλλ’ οὐκ ἔστι): it is not, not that it does not possess being. |
| VI.7.38.p2 | adding nothing : the goodness would be taken without the being and all duality avoided | The Greek asks "but will it say only 'good', adding what?": the question is what it would add; "all duality avoided" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.38.p3 | No such foreign matter is present to it | The Greek says "nothing else (οὐδὲν ἄλλο) is present to it": no other, not foreign matter. |
| VI.7.38.p3 | it can have only an immediate intuition self-directed | The Greek has "a simple direct touch (ἁπλῆ ἐπιβολή)", the word of chapter 35, not an intuition. |
| VI.7.39.p1 | at the point where Intellectual-Principle and Being are differentiated | The Greek says otherness is taken "where there are intellect and substance (οὐσία)": where they are, not where they are told apart; the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.7.39.p1 | must inevitably comport difference with identity | The Greek has "otherness and sameness" (ἑτερότητα καὶ ταυτότητα), two of Plato's greatest kinds. |
| VI.7.39.p2 | nothing could prevent it knowing all things; but this is impossible | The Greek asks "why not all things? Or will it be unable (ἀδυνατήσει)?": a question, not the assertion that knowing all is impossible. |
| VI.7.39.p2 | That act requires a manifold—agent, object, movement | The Greek says "having thought, it becomes many itself": intelligible, thinking, moving; it is the first that would become many, not the act that requires it. |
| VI.7.39.p3 | but will hold an august repose | The Greek has "will stand still in solemnity" (σεμνὸν ἑστήξεται), Plato's words in the Sophist; "repose" is MacKenna's, and he says himself below that it is used for want of a fitter word. |
| VI.7.39.p3 | the dependent he cannot know when he has no knowledge of himself | The Greek asks "and how is it toward itself (πρὸς αὐτόν), if it does not think itself?": its relation to itself, not knowledge of the dependent. |
| VI.7.39.p4 | Plato dealing with essential Being allows it intellection | The Greek has "speaking about substance (οὐσίας)": the modern version keeps "substance"; and it is Plato's Sophist that gives it thinking. |
| VI.7.39.p4 | That which transcends (the movement of) Intellection | The Greek has "what is beyond thinking (τὸ ὑπερβεβηκὸς τὸ νοεῖν)"; "(the movement of)" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.40.p1 | we need overwhelming persuasion | The Greek says "the persuasion must have necessity (ἀνάγκην) mixed with it": persuasion joined with proof, not overwhelming persuasion. |
| VI.7.40.p1 | but some further confirmation is desirable | The Greek has "some encouragements (παραμύθια)", words of comfort to bring to the argument, not confirmation. |
| VI.7.40.p2 | There is the intellection that remains within its place of origin | The Greek has "the one that is with (συνοῦσα) what it is from": being with it, as an activity is with its subject. |
| VI.7.40.p3 | There is also the intellection inbound with Being—Being’s very author | The Greek has "the thinking that is with substance (μετ’ οὐσίας) and has brought substance into being"; the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.7.40.p3 | it produces therefore of its own motion and its act is Real-Being | The Greek says "it begot by itself (ἐφ’ ἑαυτῆς), and its activity is substance": "Real-Being" for οὐσία is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.40.p3 | save the logical distinction of thinker and thought | The Greek says they are not other "except in account (λόγῳ)": in the account of them, not by a logical distinction. |
| VI.7.40.p4 | If instead of moving outward it remained with the First | The Greek says "if it were of that one and not from (ἀπ’) that one": belonging to it, not coming from it; "moving outward" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.40.p4 | it is an image, but of an original so great that the very copy stands a reality | The Greek says it is an image "of something so great that it became substance (ἐγένετο οὐσία)": substance, the word of the chapter. |
| VI.7.40.p5 | we come not to more being and more intellection but to what overpasses both | The Greek says one comes "neither to substance nor to thinking, but beyond substance (ἐπέκεινα οὐσίας) and thinking": Plato's "beyond substance". |
| VI.7.40.p6 | all intellection, even of the Good, is beneath it | The Greek says "thinking, if it is of the good, is worse (χεῖρον) than it": the thinking of the good is worse than the good, not beneath the first beside all other thinking. |
| VI.7.40.p6 | leaving the Good unsoiled by it as by all else | The Greek says thinking leaves it "pure (καθαρὸν)" of itself, as of the other things: pure, not unsoiled. |
| VI.7.40.p7 | Anyone making the Good at once Thinker and Thought identifies it with Being | The Greek says he makes it "substance (οὐσίαν) and thinking together with substance": substance, kept here for οὐσία. |
| VI.7.40.p7 | or it points, by that duality, to a prior principle having intellection as a characteristic | The Greek says thinking, as completion or as coming into being with another (συνυπόστασις), has another nature before it "to which thinking fittingly belongs (εἰκότως)"; "by that duality" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.40.p8 | To essay its power of knowing | The Greek asks whether it would seek "its power, how great it is (ὅση)": the extent of its power, not its power of knowing. |
| VI.7.41.p1 | what must take the light through the eye needs the light because of its darkness | The Greek says what needs the eye "seeks light, having darkness in itself (σκότον ἔχον παῤ αὑτῷ)": it seeks the light, and the darkness is its own. |
| VI.7.41.p2 | What could even intellection need and add to itself for the purpose of its act | The Greek has "intellect itself (αὐτὸς ὁ νοῦς)" that needs to add, in order to think: intellect, not intellection. |
| VI.7.41.p2 | It is no duality but, rather, a manifold | The Greek says "nor is it two, or rather more" (οὐδ’ ἐστὶ δύο, μᾶλλον δὲ πλείω): it is neither two nor many; MacKenna makes it a manifold. |
| VI.7.41.p2 | but the distinction has existed | The Greek says that if they were one "they will make themselves vanish in themselves (ἀφανιεῖ)", and if distinguished by otherness they will not be the first; nothing of a distinction that has existed. |
| VI.7.41.p3 | to the Intellectual-Principle it is appropriate as being one thing with the very essence | The Greek says "its being (τὸ εἶναι) is the same" as thinking: being, not essence. |
| VI.7.41.p3 | carrying the assurance of identity, of the unity of the two | The Greek says it takes in an understanding of itself "that this is this, that the two are one (ὅτι τὰ δύο ἕν)". |
| VI.7.41.p4 | The First on the contrary if it have content must exist in a way too great | The Greek says "if it is anything to itself (τί ἐστιν αὐτῷ), it is so in a greater way": anything to itself, not content. |
| VI.7.41.p4 | it would be absurd to suppose The Good standing in need of goodness | The Greek says "that would be laughable (γελοῖον): for so it would also be in need of itself": of itself, not of goodness. |
| VI.7.41.p5 | even Being cannot be There | The Greek has "as substance (οὐσία) is not either": the modern version keeps "substance". |
| VI.7.41.p5 | which is a thing of the lower sphere | The Greek says "if substance is here (ἐνταῦθα)", with intellect, where the first thinking and being are together: in the intelligible, not a lower sphere. |
| VI.7.41.p5 | Reason, perception, intelligence, none of these | The Greek has "neither account (λόγος) nor perception nor knowledge (ἐπιστήμη)": knowledge, not intelligence, and λόγος as the account, as in the Parmenides. |
| VI.7.42.p1 | Secondaries are to be ranged under the First, tertiaries under the secondaries | The Greek says set the second "about (περὶ) the first", and the third about the second: about, as in the Letter quoted next, not under. |
| VI.7.42.p1 | Faced by the difficulty of placing these powers | The Greek says "these things (ταῦτα)", the solemn things of chapter 41, thinking and knowing; "powers" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.42.p2 | for certainly His very nature requires that nothing of the later be in Him | The Greek says "or else they would no longer be all things, if anything of the others after him were present to him": the argument is about the all, not his nature's requirement. |
| VI.7.42.p2 | and they reach to Him as to something differing from them all | The Greek says they reach for him "as it were" (οἷον), as other than all things; MacKenna leaves out the "as it were". |
| VI.7.42.p3 | He being set above all that is beautiful here | The Greek says he is set "above all this beautiful (ὑπὲρ τὸ καλὸν πᾶν τοῦτο)", the beauty in the forms; "here" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.7.42.p3 | Where we read that He is the cause of all beauty we are clearly to understand that beauty depends upon the Forms | The Greek says "he seems (φαίνεται) to set the beautiful in the forms": Plato seems to, Plotinus reads; MacKenna makes it clear. |
| VI.8.1.p1 | Can there be question as to whether the gods have voluntary action ? | The Greek asks whether "anything is up to them" (εἴ τί ἐστιν ἐπ’ αὐτοῖς), the phrase the whole treatise turns on; the "voluntary" (ἑκούσιον) is another thing, which the chapter goes on to set apart from it. |
| VI.8.1.p1 | with their combination of powerlessness and hesitating power | The Greek has "weaknesses and disputed powers" (ἀμφισβητησίμοις δυνάμεσι): powers that can be argued over, not powers that hesitate. |
| VI.8.1.p1 | lest in this ascription we be really making power identical with Essential Act | The Greek fears the opposite: that "we should call one thing power and another activity (ἐνέργεια)", dividing the first into a power and an act still to come; MacKenna's "identical" and "Essential Act" are his. |
| VI.8.1.p2 | To establish this will help to show whether we are to ascribe freedom to the gods | The Greek asks whether it fits "to carry over" (μεταφέρειν) the notion to the gods: a word moved from us to them, as a metaphor, and what is carried over is "up to us", not "freedom". |
| VI.8.1.p3 | the voluntary is conceived as an event amenable to will | The Greek says that this is the notion of "what is up to us" (τοῦ ἐφ’ ἡμῖν): "what is a slave to the will" (ὃ τῇ βουλήσει δουλεύει). MacKenna puts "the voluntary" where the Greek has "up to us", which the next sentence sets apart from the voluntary. |
| VI.8.1.p3 | compulsions, violent assaults of passion crushing the soul | The Greek says the passions "hold the soul fast" (τὴν ψυχὴν κατεχούσαις), as one holds a captive; "crushing" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.1.p4 | Perhaps however that ignorance is not compatible with real freedom | The Greek says "perhaps that too (κἀκεῖνο) would disagree with having it up to oneself": the deed done in ignorance would not even be up to the man; "real freedom" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.1.p4 | in the failure to recognise a father and not so in the failure to recognise the wickedness of murder | The Greek says "if he does not know that he is his own (φίλιος) ... if he does not know that one must not": that one must not kill, the rule itself; "the wickedness of murder" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.1.p4 | Differing conceptually, the two conditions will often coincide but sometimes will clash | The Greek says the two would "run together" in many cases "though their account (λόγος) is different": the definition of each, as Aristotle's word is; "conceptually" says the same more loosely. |
| VI.8.2.p1 | as when we act or omit in lust or rage | The Greek has "spirit" and "appetite" (θυμῷ ἢ ἐπιθυμίᾳ), the two lower parts of the soul in Plato's Republic (IV 439d-441a); "lust" and "rage" make them narrower than they are. |
| VI.8.2.p1 | the victims of malpractice producing incontrollable delusions | The Greek has two cases: "those caught by drugs" (φαρμάκοις ἁλοῦσι), and those caught by "the impressions that fall upon them, of which they are not masters" (ὧν οὐ κύριοι); "malpractice" and "delusions" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.2.p1 | It must be founded in impulse or in some appetite | The Greek asks it, "to impulse (ὁρμῇ) and to any desire whatever?", the first of the answers the chapter weighs and puts aside; MacKenna makes it an assertion. |
| VI.8.2.p2 | Further, every emotion is preceded by some meagre reasoning | The Greek says "a bare reckoning (λογισμὸς ψιλὸς) goes before the affections": a reckoning by itself, without knowledge or will in it, not a "meagre" one. |
| VI.8.2.p2 | fire acts in accordance with its characteristic being | The Greek says fire "acts as it came to be" (ποιεῖ γὰρ ὡς γεγένηται): as it was made by another, which is the point against what has its starting point in another. |
| VI.8.2.p2 | the desires of the conjoint of soul and body | The Greek says "the living being (ζῴου) and the compound (τοῦ συνθέτου)"; "the conjoint of soul and body" is MacKenna's gloss, a fair one. |
| VI.8.2.p3 | freedom does not depend upon act but is a thing of the mind | The Greek says it "will stand in intellect" (ἐν νῷ στήσεται): νοῦς, where the treatise goes on to set what is up to us, not "the mind". |
| VI.8.2.p3 | is still of mixed value and cannot carry freedom | The Greek says all that is in action "is mixed and cannot have what is up to us pure" (μικτὸν καὶ οὐ καθαρὸν): not that it has none, but that it has none unmixed. |
| VI.8.2.p3 | We may be reminded that the Living Form and the soul know what they do | The Greek has "the living being" (τὸ ζῷον); "Living Form" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.3.p1 | We have traced self-disposal to will, will to reasoning and, next step, to right reasoning | The Greek sets the will "in reason" (ἐν λόγῳ) and then "in right reason" (ἐν λόγῳ ὀρθῷ), the ὀρθὸς λόγος of Aristotle and the Stoics; "reasoning" is MacKenna's, and what is traced is "up to us", not "self-disposal". |
| VI.8.3.p1 | of some presentment from the fancy with no knowledge of the foundations of that rightness | The Greek says "if he did not know why (διότι) it was right": not knowing the reason why, the cause; "presentment from the fancy" is MacKenna's for an "impression" (φαντασία). |
| VI.8.3.p2 | the proposals emanating thence are freedom | The Greek says "the premises (προτάσεις) from there are truly free": the first premises of a reasoning, the starting points of chapter 2's search; "proposals" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.3.p2 | Self-disposal, to us, belongs to those who, through the activities of the Intellectual-Principle, live above the states of the body | The Greek says "to the one who through the activities of intellect is free (ἐλευθέρῳ) of the affections of the body": the freedom the chapter seeks is already in the word; "live above" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.3.p2 | cannot be credited with self-disposal or voluntary act | The Greek grants the bad neither what is "up to them" (τὸ ἐπ’ αὐτοῖς) nor "the voluntary" (τὸ ἑκούσιον), the two terms chapter 1 set apart; MacKenna's "self-disposal" stands for both "up to us" and self-determination. |
| VI.8.4.p1 | These higher beings, too, obey their own nature ; where then is their freedom ? | The Greek says they are "slaves (δουλευόντων) to their own nature": the slavery of chapter 1, which the next paragraph answers; "obey" softens it. |
| VI.8.4.p1 | since failing motive, act will not be performed | The Greek says "for they will not act for nothing" (οὐ γὰρ μάτην πράξουσιν): they act for an end outside them, which is the necessity from outside; "failing motive" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.4.p2 | the involuntary is, precisely, motion away from a good and towards the enforced | The Greek says the involuntary is "a leading away (ἀπαγωγὴ) from the good": the slave's being led off by another, which the paragraph goes on to; "motion away" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.4.p2 | Hence the shame of slavedom is incurred not when one is held from the hurtful | The Greek says slavery is blamed "not where one has no power (ἐξουσίαν) to go to evil (τὸ κακὸν)": to have no power for evil is no slavery; "the hurtful" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.4.p2 | being debarred from the preferred path in a menial obedience | The Greek says the slave is led away from his own goods "because another stronger (κρείττονος) stands over him": the master who is stronger, not a menial obedience. |
| VI.8.4.p3 | Freedom may of course be an inappropriate term: there is something greater here | The Greek says "if 'up to it' (τὸ ἐπ’ αὐτῷ) does not fit": it is "up to it", not "freedom", that may be too small a word for intellect. |
| VI.8.4.p3 | In a principle, act and essence must be free | The Greek says nothing else is master of its activity, "nor of its substance (οὐσίας) either, if it is a principle"; the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία, where MacKenna has "essence". |
| VI.8.4.p4 | this its act is self-centred and must entail its very greatest good | The Greek says the impulse towards the good is "from itself and in itself" (ἐξ αὐτοῦ ... καὶ ἐν αὑτῷ), and that this "would be better for it" (ὃ ἄμεινον ἂν εἴη αὐτῷ); "self-centred" and "its very greatest good" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.4.p4 | Intellectual-Principle is within the Good | The Greek says intellect's principle is "not outside itself but in the Good" (ἐν τῷ ἀγαθῷ): it is its principle that is in the Good, and so not outside it. |
| VI.8.5.p1 | that may very well be freedom; but is the freedom ours ? | The Greek asks "but how is that up to us?" (πῶς ἐφ’ ἡμῖν;): whether the deed is up to us, when its occasion is not; not whether the freedom is ours. |
| VI.8.5.p1 | there is always some impinging event leading out our quality to show itself in this or that act | The Greek says virtue "is compelled" (ἀναγκαζομένης) by what befalls to work this or that: a compulsion, which the next paragraph takes up; "leading out our quality" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.5.p1 | it certainly cannot be allowed in regard to issue, for we are not master of events | The Greek says we are not masters "of attaining" (τοῦ τυχεῖν), of hitting what we aim at, not of "events" in general. |
| VI.8.5.p2 | poverty that it may show independence | The Greek has τὸ ἐλευθέριον, "liberality", the virtue of the free man (ἐλεύθερος) in giving, which poverty, in others, calls out; "independence" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.5.p2 | how can it have untrammelled self-disposal ? | The Greek asks how it could have what is up to it "purely" (καθαρῶς), the unmixed of chapter 2's end; and it says virtue "has been compelled to help" (ἠνάγκασται βοηθεῖν), as a helper is called in. |
| VI.8.5.p3 | taming passions and appetites | The Greek says virtue brings the affections and desires "into measure" (συμμετρουμένην), the measure of Plato and of Aristotle's mean; "taming" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.5.p3 | In what sense, at that, can we hold our goodness to be our own free act, our fine conduct to be uncompelled ? | The Greek asks how "being good" is up to us, and how virtue is "without a master" (ἀδέσποτον), the word of Plato's myth; "our fine conduct to be uncompelled" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.5.p3 | If then virtue is, as it were, a second Intellectual-Principle, and heightens the soul to Intellectual quality | The Greek says virtue is "as it were another intellect (νοῦς τις ἄλλος)" and "a state (ἕξις) that makes the soul as it were intellect" (νοωθῆναι): a state, Aristotle's word for virtue, which turns the soul into intellect. |
| VI.8.6.p1 | at its discretion it sacrifices a man | The Greek says "if it thinks fit, it gives him up" (προιεμένην): virtue lets go of the man in danger, and bids him let go of his life; "sacrifices" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.6.p1 | it looks to its own high aim and not to the safeguarding of anything lower | The Greek says virtue has "its own fine (τὸ καλὸν αὑτῆς) as its aim, and not the being of what is under it": the fine, for whose sake the brave man acts; "high aim" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.6.p1 | in so far as it does so it is free and confers freedom | The Greek says virtue wishes "to be up to itself, standing over the soul so that it may be good", and "so far" (μέχρι τούτου) to be free and to make the soul free: "so far" as it stands over the soul, not "in so far as it does so". |
| VI.8.6.p2 | touch closely—we read—on body | The Greek says "he says" (φησιν): Plato, whose words these are; MacKenna's "we read" hides the quotation. And they are "set right by habits and exercises" (ἔθεσι καὶ ἀσκήσεσι), Plato's words. |
| VI.8.6.p2 | since such experiences, amenable to morality and discipline | The Greek says the affections are "enslaved or measured by reason (τῷ λόγῳ)" and "set right by habits and exercises"; "morality and discipline" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.6.p2 | This makes it all the more evident that the unembodied is the free | The Greek says "the immaterial (τὸ ἄυλον) is the free": what is without matter, not "the unembodied". |
| VI.8.6.p3 | it is called will because it expresses the Intellectual-Principle in the willing-phase | The Greek says it is called will "because it is according to intellect" (ὅτι κατὰ νοῦν); "the willing-phase" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.6.p3 | Will strives towards the good which the act of Intellectual-Principle realises | The Greek says "the will wants the good, and to think truly is in the good (ἐν τῷ ἀγαθῷ)": thinking is in the good already, not a realising of it. |
| VI.8.6.p3 | Any one scrupulous about setting self-disposal so high may find some loftier word. | The Greek says it must be set "as greater, if one does not want to raise 'up to it' to this" (εἰ μή τις ἐθέλοι εἰς τοῦτο ἀναβαίνειν, τὸ ἐφ’ αὑτῷ): the "greater than 'up to it'" of chapter 4, not a loftier word. |
| VI.8.7.p1 | to soul when it fully attains, to Intellectual-Principle by connate possession | The Greek says the others have what is up to them "when the one (soul) can attain it unhindered (ἀνεμποδίστως), and the other (intellect) can have it"; "connate possession" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.7.p1 | That principle of Good is the sole object of desire | The Greek says the nature of the Good is "the desired itself" (αὐτὸ τὸ ἐφετὸν), what all desire; "sole" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.7.p2 | It is rash thinking drawn from another order | The Greek says "some bold argument (τολμηρὸς λόγος) sent from elsewhere (ἑτέρωθεν σταλεὶς)": an argument launched from another camp, as an attack; "rash thinking drawn from another order" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.7.p2 | a First Principle to be chance-made what it is | The Greek says the nature of the first "happened (τυχοῦσα) to be as it is": "happened", the word the treatise answers from here on, not "chance-made". |
| VI.8.7.p2 | No doubt a concept leaves the reality untouched and unappropriated | The Greek says "the notion (ἔννοια) does not busy itself with the substance (οὐσίαν)": what a thing is is not the notion's question; "the reality" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.7.p2 | empty names for the non-existent | The Greek says "sounds (φωνὰς) for things without existence (ἀνυποστάτων)": the bare sounds of the voice, which the next sentence takes up ("this sound"), not names. |
| VI.8.7.p3 | is self-complete and has no higher | The Greek says it is solitary "by being this itself and as it were pleasing itself (ἀρέσκειν ἑαυτῷ)": the Good's being content with itself, which MacKenna leaves out; "self-complete" is his. |
| VI.8.7.p3 | The objection would imply that where there is most good there is least freedom | The Greek says one would take self-determination from "what most attains (τυγχάνον) the good": what most reaches the good, not "where there is most good"; the Good itself comes in the next sentence. |
| VI.8.7.p4 | from the two (Being and Act) it forms itself into The Good, self-springing and unspringing | The Greek says it makes itself from both, "its own and no one's" (ἑαυτοῦ καὶ οὐδενός): belonging to itself and to nothing else; "The Good" is not named, and "unspringing" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.7.p4 | Where—since we must use such words—the essential act is identical with the being | The Greek says "its existence (ὑπόστασις), as it were, is its activity, as it were": the "as it were" (οἷον), set before each word, is how Plotinus speaks of the first from here on; MacKenna gives it once. |
| VI.8.8.p1 | But it is not, in our view, as an attribute that this freedom is present in the First | The Greek says "we do not view self-determination as an accident (συμβεβηκὸς) of that one": Aristotle's word for what belongs to a thing not of itself, which the next chapters play on with "it happened" (συνέβη); "attribute" loses the link. |
| VI.8.8.p1 | For This is principle of all, or, more strictly, unrelated to all | The Greek says "it is the principle of these, and yet in another way it is not a principle" (ἄλλον τρόπον οὐκ ἀρχή); "unrelated to all" is MacKenna's gloss, from the next paragraph's "in relation to nothing". |
| VI.8.8.p1 | which in fact indicate manifestation upon the extern | The Greek says self-determination "already speaks of an activity towards another (εἰς ἄλλο)": an activity, not a "manifestation". |
| VI.8.8.p2 | primally to Essential Existence as next to this First | The Greek says "nature" would be said first "of substance (οὐσίας), because it grew (ἔφυ) from that one": nature (φύσις) is what has grown, the word's own root; "Essential Existence" is MacKenna's for substance. |
| VI.8.8.p2 | On the other hand, we are not to deny that it is derived from Essential Existence for that would be to take away its existence | The Greek, as the modern version reads it, says of the first that it must not be called "not from itself" (οὐ παρ’ αὐτῆς), since we took away its being, and "not from itself" is said of what is by another; MacKenna makes it something "derived from Essential Existence". |
| VI.8.8.p3 | Chance did not yet exist ; there was no | The Greek says "there was not yet (πω) chance, nor the spontaneous (τὸ αὐτόματον) either": the spontaneous is Aristotle's other cause by accident, beside chance; MacKenna gives it as "automatic action". |
| VI.8.8.p3 | happening belongs only to the multiple where, first, existence is given and then something is added | The Greek says "it happened" (συνέβη) is among many things, "when some things are, and something else happens (συμβῇ) to them besides": the same word, συμβαίνειν, as the "accident" (συμβεβηκός) of the chapter's first sentence. |
| VI.8.9.p1 | compulsion did not co-exist with the Supreme but has place only among secondaries and even there can exercise no tyranny | The Greek says "there was no necessity (ἀνάγκη)": necessity is in the things that follow the principle, "and even it does not have force (βίαν) in them"; necessity, not compulsion, and "the Supreme" and "tyranny" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.9.p1 | and if the difference were for the worse it would manifest itself as that lesser being | The Greek says "if worse, it would have been active according to its own substance (οὐσίαν)": the objector's point is that it would act as it was, whatever it was. |
| VI.8.9.p1 | It is therefore in a sense determined—determined, I mean, by its uniqueness | The Greek says "determined (ὡρισμένον), because it is alone of its kind (μοναχῶς)": the solitary of chapter 7, not "uniqueness" as a property; and the "in a sense" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.9.p2 | All the rest waits for the appearing of the king | The Greek says the others must wait to see "what the king will appear to them to be" (τί ποτε αὐτοῖς ὁ βασιλεὺς φανείη), and then "set him down" as what he is: the waiting is to know him, not only to greet him. |
| VI.8.9.p2 | not a being that acts in conformity with goodness—and so, recognisably, a secondary | The Greek says "not active according to the good (κατὰ τὸ ἀγαθόν), for so he would seem to follow another": "a secondary" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.9.p3 | just as Being exists in complete identity with its own essential nature and that of Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says being "is as substance (οὐσία) is, being what it is, and what intellect is"; it then adds that "one might say of intellect too, 'it happened to be intellect'", as if it could be other than its nature, which MacKenna leaves out. "Essential nature" and "identity" are his. |
| VI.8.9.p3 | how then can we think that this happening can attach to the Transcendent of Being | The Greek says "what is beyond being" (τοῦ ἐπέκεινα ὄντος), the words of the Republic, "to which it belongs to have begotten (γεγεννηκέναι) being"; "the Transcendent of Being" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.9.p4 | it would imply limit, a defined form | The Greek says "so you would have bounded (ὁρίσας) it, and it would be a this (τόδε τι)": Aristotle's "this something", the single thing; "a defined form" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.9.p4 | what then are we to say when we mount and contemplate that which stands yet higher | The Greek says "what would someone say there, who had gone up (ἀναβὰς) to what is above this and looked into it (εἰσιδών)": one who has seen, which the next sentences turn on ("the one who has seen"), not "we" who think about it. |
| VI.8.9.p5 | at most it is to be conceived as the total power towards things, supremely self-concentred | The Greek says "a whole power (δύναμιν πᾶσαν), truly master of itself (αὑτῆς ὄντως κυρίαν)": master of itself, the question of the treatise; "towards things" and "self-concentred" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.9.p5 | being what it wills to be or rather projecting into existence what it wills | The Greek says "being this which it wishes, or rather having thrown off (ἀπορρίψασαν) into the beings what it wishes": what it wishes it has cast away from itself into the beings, as something less than itself. |
| VI.8.10.p1 | In the case of things thus conformed to reason the cause cannot be identified with chance but must lie in that very reason | The Greek has "according to a forming principle (κατὰ λόγον)": the principle that gives "form and limit and shape" in the sentence before; the modern version keeps "forming principle" for λόγος, and "reason" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.10.p1 | chance must be kept for what occurs apart from choice and sequence and is purely concurrent | The Greek says chance is in what is done "not by intention (μὴ προηγουμένως) nor in sequence, but by coincidences (συμπτώμασιν)"; "choice" is MacKenna's for what is done first-hand, for its own sake. |
| VI.8.10.p2 | Chance is no doubt master of many things but is not master of Intellectual-Principle, of reason, of order | The Greek has "intellect and forming principle (λόγου) and order": the modern version keeps "intellect" for νοῦς and "forming principle" for λόγος, where MacKenna has "Intellectual-Principle" and "reason". |
| VI.8.10.p2 | When we come to the source of all reason, order and limit, how can we attribute the reality there to chance? | The Greek asks how anyone could ascribe to chance "the existence (ὑπόστασιν)" of "the principle of every forming principle (λόγου), and of order and limit"; "the reality there" and "reason" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.10.p3 | He is still not master of his own essence | The Greek says "not being master of its own substance (οὐσίας)": the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία, where MacKenna has "essence". |
| VI.8.10.p3 | not self-originating but acting out of his nature as He finds it | The Greek says "not having brought itself into existence (οὐχ ὑποστήσας ἑαυτόν), but using itself (χρώμενος ἑαυτῷ) such as it is": the objector pictures one who uses what was given him. |
| VI.8.10.p4 | This absence of declination comports the fulness of power | The Greek says its not coming to anything else "has in it the excess (ὑπερβολὴν) of its power": power beyond all, not a "fulness". |
| VI.8.10.p4 | here permanence is very Act, a self-determination | The Greek says its not coming is "from itself and through itself" (παρ’ αὑτοῦ καὶ δι’ αὑτόν); "permanence is very Act" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.10.p4 | the Act of one who is necessity, law, to all | The Greek says it is "the necessity and law of the others (τῶν ἄλλων)": of the things after it, not "to all", itself included. |
| VI.8.11.p1 | We can but withdraw, silent, hopeless, and search no further. | The Greek says "setting it down in our judgement as something with no way through (ἐν ἀπόρῳ)": an aporia, where the search finds no passage; "hopeless" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.11.p1 | all questioning deals with the nature of a thing, its quality, its cause or its essential being | The Greek has four questions: "the what it is (τί ἐστιν), the what it is like (οἷον), the why (διὰ τί), and the being (εἶναι)", whether it is; "essential being" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.11.p1 | the question as to quality would be looking for an attribute in that which has none | The Greek says to ask what it is like is to ask "what has happened (συμβέβηκεν) to it, to which nothing has happened": the accident again, the word of chapter 8, which MacKenna gives as "attribute". |
| VI.8.11.p2 | we introduce God and proceed to enquire | The Greek says "this nature" (ταύτην τὴν φύσιν); God is not named here, and the words after it say "him" (αὐτόν). MacKenna names him. |
| VI.8.11.p2 | the presence and quality of this new-comer | The Greek says "its presence and, as it were, its substance (οἷον τὴν οὐσίαν)", with Plotinus' "as it were"; "quality" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.11.p3 | He must not be set in anything either as enthroned in eternal immanence | The Greek says "neither as always lying (κείμενον) and settled (ἱδρυμένον) in it": set in a place, as a statue is set up, not "enthroned in eternal immanence". |
| VI.8.11.p4 | Thus we conceive as far as we may, the spaceless | The Greek says "this placeless (ἄτοπον) thing": the word also means "absurd", "strange", and Plotinus plays on it; MacKenna's "the spaceless" keeps only one side. |
| VI.8.11.p4 | He has no quality since no shape, even shape Intellectual | The Greek says "not even an intelligible (νοητὴ) one": the modern version keeps "intelligible" for νοητός, where MacKenna has "Intellectual". |
| VI.8.11.p4 | It is on the contrary nearer the truth to | The Greek says it is truer to say "not even 'it happened so'" (τὸ οὐδὲ οὕτω συνέβη), and that it "did not even happen at all": the happening is denied outright, where MacKenna's "Thus He has happened not to be" still says that it happened. |
| VI.8.12.p1 | In us the individual, viewed as body, is far from reality | The Greek says each of us, in respect of the body, would be "far from substance (οὐσίας)": substance, the word of the whole chapter; "reality" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.12.p1 | This is a compound state, a mingling of Reality and Difference | The Greek says "as it were a compound of difference and substance (ἐκ διαφορᾶς καὶ οὐσίας)", and so "not substance properly, nor substance itself (αὐτοουσία)": difference is the differentia that, added to substance, makes each of us; "a mingling of Reality" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.12.p1 | The mind utterly reluctant returns to its doubt | The Greek says "the soul (ἡ ψυχή), not at all persuaded by what has been said, is again at a loss": the soul, and its aporia, not "the mind". |
| VI.8.12.p2 | preeminently to be known as the liberator | The Greek says it "might be called a free-maker" (ἐλευθεροποιόν), a word made for the place; "preeminently" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.12.p2 | Besides, the very question as to self-disposal falls in the case of what is First in reality | The Greek says that substance "has been let go (ἀφείθη) to be master of itself, in so far as it is first in substance": the second nature is left free to be master of itself; MacKenna makes the question fall away. |
| VI.8.12.p2 | On the contrary, it is the source of freedom to Being | The Greek says its substance "too is free from it (παρ’ αὐτοῦ ἐλευθέρα), and later, and it itself has no substance (οὐκ ἔχον οὐσίαν)": MacKenna leaves out that the first has no substance. |
| VI.8.12.p3 | self-mastery begins with Being while the Supreme is to be set in a higher order | The Greek says "we gave (ἀπέδομεν) 'master of itself' to substance (τῇ οὐσίᾳ), and set it in something more honoured (ἐν τιμιωτέρῳ)": assigned to substance, the second; "Being" and "a higher order" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.12.p3 | holding that Act begins with others moving about it | The Greek says the others, "being active about it, have their existence (ὑπόστασιν)": they exist by their activity about it; "begins" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.12.p4 | Where we speak of self-mastery there is a certain duality, Act against essence | The Greek says "there substance and activity are in a way two" (δύο πως), in the second nature, intellect; the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία, where MacKenna has "essence". |
| VI.8.13.p1 | it is merely for the sake of conveying conviction, at the cost of verbal accuracy | The Greek says it is "for the sake of persuasion (πειθοῦς)", "and yet in the accounts we must think past (παρανοητέον) the words": understand beyond what they say; "at the cost of verbal accuracy" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.13.p1 | Thus He is wholly master of Himself and holds His very being at His will. | The Greek says "having even its being up to itself (ἐφ’ ἑαυτῷ)": "up to itself", the phrase of chapter 1, now said of the first; "at His will" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.13.p2 | much more then must the very Principle, The Good, be desirable in itself | The Greek says the nature of the good is "far more to be chosen (αἱρετῆς) by itself (ἑαυτῇ)": chosen by itself, for itself, which is the point of the chapter; "desirable in itself" turns it outward. |
| VI.8.13.p2 | and becomes the chosen essence promoting that extern’s will and identical with the will that gave the existence ? | The Greek says the share of good in another is "a voluntary substance (οὐσία ἑκούσιος), come to be by willing (θελήσει), one and the same with willing, and brought into existence through willing": the thing's own willing, not an "extern's will", and substance, not "essence". |
| VI.8.13.p3 | It must be a consistent self willing its being | The Greek says "it runs together with itself" (σύνδρομος αὐτὸς ἑαυτῷ): it and its will coincide, as two runners keep pace; "a consistent self" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.13.p3 | from the absence of distinction between a given nature and one which would be preferred | The Greek says it is not one thing, "what it happened (ἔτυχεν) to be", and another, what it would have wished: "what it happened to be", the objector's word, which "a given nature" loses. |
| VI.8.13.p3 | it could have no fault to find with anything in its nature, as if that nature were imposed by force | The Greek says it would find no fault with itself "as being this by necessity (ὑπὸ ἀνάγκης)": the necessity of chapter 10, not force. |
| VI.8.13.p4 | towards a good not gained by any wiles | The Greek says "not bribed (δεδεκασμένου)", the word for a judge who has been bought, "nor drawn on by its own nature": nothing outside it, nor its nature, decides it; "wiles" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.13.p4 | this hypostasis of the Good must necessarily have self-option | The Greek says "in the existence (ὑποστάσει) of the good the choice (αἵρεσιν) and the willing of itself must be included"; "self-option" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.13.p4 | there will be inner discord | The Greek says each of the others "might even be displeased (δυσχεραίνοι) with itself": dissatisfaction with oneself, not "inner discord". |
| VI.8.13.p5 | we are forced to apply to the Supreme terms which strictly are ruled out | The Greek says the words are used "for the sake of pointing it out" (ἐνδείξεως ἕνεκα), as signs, and that one must "take the 'as it were' (τὸ οἷον) with each"; MacKenna gives this οἷον as his "So to speak". |
| VI.8.13.p5 | it holds its existence through choice and will, conditions of its very being | The Greek says "choice and will bring it into existence along with it" (συνυφίστησιν): they are with it in its existence; "conditions of its very being" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.13.p5 | thus reason shows the Supreme to be its own Author | The Greek says "the argument (ὁ λόγος) has found that it has made itself" (πεποιηκέναι αὑτόν): the argument, and the strong word "made itself", which MacKenna softens to "its own Author". |
| VI.8.14.p1 | Thus any given man is distinct from essential man though belonging to the order Man | The Greek says "this man is other, and being man (τὸ ἀνθρώπῳ εἶναι) another": Aristotle's "being for a man", what it is to be a man; "essential man" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.14.p1 | a soul and a soul’s essence are the same | The Greek says "soul and being soul (τὸ ψυχῇ εἶναι) are the same, if soul is simple and not said of another (κατ’ ἄλλου)"; "essence" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.14.p1 | the particular man may be considered as accidental | The Greek says this man "might come about by chance (κατὰ τύχην)": chance, the question of the treatise; "accident" (συμβέβηκε) comes only in the next sentence. |
| VI.8.14.p2 | all that reality is brought about in virtue of something emanating from the divine | The Greek says the perceptible things are such "by being from those" (τῷ ἀπ’ ἐκείνων), the beings in truth; "emanating from the divine" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.14.p2 | the reason of each of the several forms is that such is the plan of man | The Greek says "the why of this, that this is what being man (τὸ ἀνθρώπῳ εἶναι) is": what it is to be a man, not a "plan". |
| VI.8.14.p2 | in a word the mutual determinant is mutual adaptation | The Greek says "the harmony (συμφωνία) of all with each other is cause for each other": a harmony, the musical word; "mutual adaptation" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.14.p3 | not because that source has so conceived each separately | The Greek says the spring "did not reason" (οὐ λελογισμένης), but gave the why and the being "whole, all at once" (ὅλον ἀθρόον): the no-reasoning of VI.7.1; "conceived each separately" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.14.p4 | as author of reason, of causation, and of causing essence | The Greek says "father (πατήρ) of forming principle (λόγου), and of cause, and of causal substance (οὐσίας αἰτιώδους)": father, as Plato names the maker; the modern version keeps "forming principle" and "substance", where MacKenna has "reason" and "essence". |
| VI.8.14.p4 | the first self, transcendently The Self | The Greek says "it is itself first, and itself beyond being (ὑπερόντως)": the word for what is beyond being, not "transcendently The Self". |
| VI.8.14.p4 | God is cause of Himself | The Greek says "cause of itself" (αἴτιον ἑαυτοῦ) without naming God; MacKenna adds the name. |
| VI.8.15.p1 | once more God’s being and his seeking are identical | The Greek says "aiming (ἔφεσις) and substance (οὐσία) have appeared the same": substance, and God is not named; "identical" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.15.p1 | the sought serving as Hypostasis and substrate of the seeker | The Greek says what is aimed at is "according to its existence (ὑπόστασιν), and as it were the subject (ὑποκείμενον)": the "as it were" is left out, and the existence is the first's own, not the seeker's. |
| VI.8.15.p1 | not happening to be as some extern willed but existing as He wills it | The Greek says it "has not come to be as something else (ἕτερον) wished": coming to be at another's wish, the objector's "it happened" in another form. |
| VI.8.15.p2 | the freedom of that light which belongs to the order of the good | The Greek says "a light good-formed (ἀγαθοειδοῦς) and good": the word the modern version keeps as "good-formed" throughout; "belongs to the order of the good" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.15.p2 | we are untouched by all that has gathered round us subjecting us to happening and chance | The Greek says such a nature, if we found it in ourselves, "has none of the other things attached (συνήρτηται) to us": it is the nature that is free of them; MacKenna makes "we" untouched. |
| VI.8.15.p2 | an actuality whose advantage over Intellection is no adventitious superiority | The Greek says the activity is "greater than intellect, having what is above thinking not brought in (οὐκ ἐπακτὸν)": its being above thinking is its own, not added from outside; "advantage" and "superiority" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.15.p3 | it is reason dissipated, it is indetermination | The Greek says "a scattering (σκέδασις) of forming principle (λόγου), and lack of bound (ἀοριστία)": the modern version keeps "forming principle" for λόγος, where MacKenna has "reason". |
| VI.8.15.p3 | Ascending we come upon the Supreme, not as reason but as reason’s better | The Greek says "that one is not a forming principle, but finer (κάλλιον) than a forming principle": finer, more beautiful, not "better"; and "reason" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.15.p3 | the root of reason is self-springing | The Greek says "it is the root of forming principle (ῥίζα λόγου), from itself (παρ’ αὑτῆς)": the root is the first, and it is from itself; "reason" and "self-springing" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.16.p1 | they have not brought Him to be but happen, all, to Him | The Greek says it is "not having happened (συμβὰς) to them, but they to it": it did not happen to them, they happened to it; MacKenna makes it that they did not bring it to be. |
| VI.8.16.p1 | all lies subject to Him | The Greek says it "has all things as slaves" (πάντα δοῦλα ἔχει): the slaves of chapter 1, now on the other side; "lies subject" softens it. |
| VI.8.16.p2 | God therefore is issue of Act | The Greek says "so it is itself a work of activity (ἐνέργημα)", without naming God; the line is damaged in Volkmann's text, and is read here as "it is itself" (αὐτός). |
| VI.8.16.p2 | as self-dwelling Act and Intellectual-Principle, the most to be loved, He has given Himself existence | The Greek says "if it is an abiding activity (ἐνέργεια μένουσα), and the most lovable thing is as it were (οἷον) intellect": the "as it were" is left out, so that MacKenna makes the first intellect. |
| VI.8.16.p3 | He must again, since the word is inevitable, make Himself | The Greek has "as it were" (οἷον) four times: it "as it were stays firm", "as it were looks", its "as it were being", and it "would as it were make itself"; MacKenna keeps one "so to speak" and adds "since the word is inevitable". |
| VI.8.16.p3 | the will adopting the Best is not a thing of chance | The Greek says "being the willing of the best (τοῦ ἀρίστου)": the will that belongs to the best, not one that adopts it. |
| VI.8.16.p4 | That his being is constituted by this self-originating self-tendance | The Greek says "such an inclination (νεῦσις) of it towards itself": an inclination, the word for the soul's leaning down towards body, here turned inward; "self-tendance" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.16.p4 | This awakening is before being, before Intellectual-Principle, before rational life, though He is these | The Greek says "the waking is beyond substance (ἐπέκεινα οὐσίας) and intellect and wise (ἔμφρονος) life": "beyond substance", the Republic's words; "before being" and "rational" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.16.p4 | his being, then, is a self-presence, issuing from Himself | The Greek says "its being is from itself, for itself, and out of itself" (παρ’ αὑτοῦ ... αὐτῷ καὶ ἐξ αὑτοῦ); "a self-presence" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.17.p1 | We hold the universe, with its content entire, to be as all would be if the design of the maker had so willed it | The Greek says "if the choice (προαίρεσις) of the maker had willed it": Aristotle's word for deliberate choice, which the next sentences set the intelligible beyond; "design" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.17.p1 | therefore the reason-principles of things must lie always within the producing powers in a still more perfect form | The Greek says the forming principles (λόγους) lie in the makers "standing (ἑστῶτας) in a greater order (εὐθημοσύνῃ)": standing, as the things there stand; the modern version keeps "forming principle" for λόγος, where MacKenna has "reason-principles". |
| VI.8.17.p1 | before our universe there exists, not expressed in the outer, the Intellectual-Principle of all the All | The Greek says "before this All there stands (ἑστώς) intellect, from which and according to which this All is"; "not expressed in the outer" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.17.p2 | consisting of all the Reason-Principles of the universe gathered into the closest union | The Greek says "all the forming principles (λόγοι) held together by one throughout": "of the universe" and "the closest union" are MacKenna's, and the modern version keeps "forming principle" for λόγος. |
| VI.8.17.p2 | is without part or interval (implied by reasoned arrangement), is a one-all Reason-Principle | The Greek says "without extension (ἀδιάστατος), one forming principle that is all (εἷς πάντα λόγος)": the words in brackets are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.17.p2 | be an expression of its will | The Greek says intellect "as it were wills (θέλει) that": the second wills the first, as each thing wills its good in chapter 13; "an expression of its will" makes the will the first's. |
| VI.8.18.p1 | is Reason-Principle and Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says "forming principle (ὁ λόγος) and intellect (νοῦς)": the modern version keeps these names, where MacKenna has "Reason-Principle" and "Intellectual-Principle". |
| VI.8.18.p1 | Himself do not attempt | The Greek says "but let it alone" (αὐτὸν δὲ ἔα): leave it, do not seek in it; MacKenna's "do not attempt" is close. |
| VI.8.18.p2 | A circle related in its path to a centre must be admitted to owe its scope to that centre | The Greek says the circle has "its power (δύναμιν) from the centre", and is "as it were centre-formed (κεντροειδής)", as intellect is good-formed; "scope" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.18.p2 | radii and circle are images given forth by indwelling power | The Greek says the centre is "the father (πατέρα) of circle and lines, having given traces (ἴχνη) of itself": father and traces, the words of chapter 14 and of this one. |
| VI.8.18.p2 | as products of a certain vigour in it, not cut off from it | The Greek says it begot them "by an abiding power (δυνάμει μενούσῃ)" and "by a kind of strength (ῥώμῃ)": the power stays where it is while it begets. |
| VI.8.18.p3 | it generates Intellectual-Principle by its sheer wealth | The Greek says it "begot intellect from its power (ἐκ τῆς δυνάμεως αὐτοῦ)": from its power, the power of the circle's centre; "sheer wealth" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.18.p3 | how does it lend itself to chance, to hazard | The Greek asks what "coincidence (συντυχία), or what spontaneous (αὐτόματον), or what 'as it happened'" could "come near" (πλησίον ἥκοι) such a power: they cannot even come near it. |
| VI.8.18.p4 | so in a light diffused afar from one light shining within itself | The Greek says "from some one thing transparent (διαφανοῦς) in itself": "transparent", Aristotle's word for what light makes actual; "shining within itself" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.18.p4 | at every point is reason and cause | The Greek says "each part of it is forming principle (λόγος) and cause": the modern version keeps "forming principle" for λόγος, where MacKenna has "reason". |
| VI.8.18.p4 | author as it is not of the chance-made but of what the divine willed | The Greek says "begetting what is not as it chanced, but as it itself willed (ὡς ἠθέλησεν αὐτός)": "the divine" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.18.p5 | Thus Plato seeking the best account of the necessary and appropriate | The Greek says Plato names "the due and the timely (δέον καὶ καιρόν)": the due, what ought to be, not "the necessary"; and Plato aims "to signify it as far as was possible". |
| VI.8.18.p5 | that this Necessary is not to God an outside thing | The Greek says "it is the due not as a subject (ὑποκείμενον), but as the first activity": the due, not "this Necessary"; and God is not named. |
| VI.8.18.p5 | if its manner of being is the fitting, it is the utterly self-disposing | The Greek says "if it is the timely (καιρός), it is the most sovereign (κυριώτατον) among the things after it": the timely, not "the fitting". |
| VI.8.19.p1 | One seeing That as it really is will lay aside all reasoning upon it | The Greek says "having seen that in himself (ἐν αὑτῷ)": the seeing is within, which MacKenna leaves out; "as it really is" is his. |
| VI.8.19.p1 | unable at any venture to speak of This | The Greek says he could not even, "darting forward (ἀίξας), say 'where' (τὸ ποῦ) about it": the "where", since it is everywhere, which MacKenna leaves out. |
| VI.8.19.p1 | such that if it had essence that essence would be subject to it | The Greek says "if it had substance (οὐσίαν), the substance would be its slave (δούλην)": substance, and the slave of the treatise; "essence" and "subject to" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.19.p2 | So we are to understand the Beyond-Essence darkly indicated by the ancients | The Greek says "beyond substance (ἐπέκεινα οὐσίας), said by the ancients in a riddle (δι’ αἰνίξεως)": the Republic's words; the modern version keeps "substance" for οὐσία, where MacKenna has "Essence". |
| VI.8.19.p2 | He is subject neither to Essence nor to Himself | The Greek says it "is a slave (δουλεύει) neither to substance nor to itself": the slavery of chapter 4, now denied of the first; "subject" and "Essence" are MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.20.p1 | the Act here is not directed to an achievement but is God Himself unalloyed | The Greek says its activity "does not accomplish (οὐκ ἀποτελεστικῆς) anything else, but is the whole of this": "God Himself unalloyed" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.20.p1 | the making must be taken as absolved from all else | The Greek says "setting its making down as unbound (ἀπόλυτον)": a making loosed from any product, so that it is not a making of something; "from all else" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.20.p2 | Let no one suspect us of asserting that the first Activity is without Essence | The Greek says the opposite: "nor must we be afraid (φοβητέον) to set the first activity without substance (ἄνευ οὐσίας), but must set this very thing as its as it were existence": Plotinus does set the first activity without substance; MacKenna makes him deny it. |
| VI.8.20.p2 | on the contrary the Activity is the very reality | The Greek says we must set the activity as its "as it were existence" (τὴν οἷον ὑπόστασιν), with Plotinus' "as it were"; "the very reality" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.20.p3 | Now assuredly an Activity not subjected to essence is utterly free | The Greek says "an activity that has not been a slave (δουλεύσασα) to substance is purely (καθαρῶς) free": a slave, and purely, the words of chapters 2 and 4; "essence" is MacKenna's for substance. |
| VI.8.20.p3 | if it be true to say that He is his own container, then He inducts Himself | The Greek says "if it is rightly said to hold itself together (συνέχειν), it is itself also what brings itself forth (ὁ παράγων ἑαυτόν)": "brings itself forth", not "inducts", and "holds together", not "contains". |
| VI.8.20.p4 | but since what He is He is from before all time | The Greek says "before eternity (αἰῶνα) was": before eternity, not before time, which is the point, since eternity is the life of intellect and the first is before it. |
| VI.8.20.p4 | his self-making is to be understood as simultaneous with Himself | The Greek says "the having made and it itself run together (σύνδρομον)": the word of chapter 13; "simultaneous" says it in time, which the sentence has just denied. |
| VI.8.20.p5 | This is the source also of his self-disposal | The Greek says "hence too its ruling (τὸ ἄρχον) itself": a ruler, which, if it is one, is "ruling only", with nothing ruled; "self-disposal" is MacKenna's word, used throughout for what is up to us. |
| VI.8.20.p5 | a disposing without a disposed, an abstract disposing | The Greek says it is "ruling only (τὸ ἄρχον μόνον): for it does not have what is ruled (τὸ ἀρχόμενον)"; "an abstract disposing" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.21.p1 | it is that steadfast constant which is most decidedly power by inability to depart from unity | The Greek says it is "an unshaken and immovable (ἀστεμφεῖ καὶ ἀμετακινήτῳ) power, which is most of all power when it does not stand away from the one": the inability is in the power of opposites, not in staying one. |
| VI.8.21.p1 | that self-making must be definite once for all since it is the right | The Greek says the making is "once for all (ἅπαξ): for it is fine (καλή)": fine, beautiful, not "the right". |
| VI.8.21.p2 | But whence does He draw that will seeing that essence, source of will, is inactive in Him ? | The Greek asks where its will will come from, "from a substance without activity (ἀνενεργήτου)?": the objector's question; "essence" is MacKenna's for substance, and "source of will" his. |
| VI.8.21.p2 | God and will were primally identical | The Greek says "so it is itself first will" (πρῶτον ἄρα ἡ βούλησις αὐτός): the first is will; God is not named, and "identical" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.21.p2 | but it engendered nothing new; all existed from the first | The Greek says "it begot nothing further in it (ἐν αὐτῷ): for this already was": nothing within itself, since it was already whole; "all existed from the first" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.21.p3 | all in Him belongs to Him or rather He needs nothing from them in order to being Himself | The Greek says it "is all things to itself (πάντα ἑαυτῷ), or rather needs none of all things for itself": "all in Him belongs to Him" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.21.p3 | all traces back to the Supreme | The Greek says "all are carried back (ἡ ἀναγωγή) to it": the carrying back of chapters 3 and 6, where what is up to us was carried back to intellect; "the Supreme" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.21.p4 | see that you add nothing ; be sure that your theory of God does not lessen Him | The Greek says "do not seek what you may add (προσθῇς), but whether there is something you have not yet taken away (ἀφῄρηκας) from it in your judgement": the danger is of not having taken away enough; MacKenna turns it round. |
| VI.8.21.p4 | subject not even to its own law | The Greek says "it is not even a slave (δουλεῦον) to itself": the slavery to one's own nature of chapter 4, which the treatise ends by denying of the first; "law" is MacKenna's. |
| VI.8.21.p4 | while all else is thing and | The Greek says "each of the others is itself and something else (αὐτὸ καὶ ἄλλο)": itself and another, a compound of being and difference, as chapter 12 put it. |
